Every rule in this file is a standing rule set by the owner (Salem874) unless it says otherwise.

## Project

Internal portal platform (PHP 8.5, backward-compatible with 8.4, Bootstrap 5.3.3) hosted on DreamHost shared hosting. No CLI, no Composer.

> ⚠️ **Database versions are in flux — read this before writing any SQL.**
> **THE REFERENCE HOST MOVES TO MySQL 8.4 ON 9 NOVEMBER 2026** (DreamHost told
> the owner on 2 November; Ubuntu 24.04 → 26.04 carries MySQL 8.0 → 8.4, about
> fifteen minutes' downtime). So 8.0 stops being the version in front of us and
> becomes the fallback. `Portal\Core\DbServer` already judges this correctly and
> needed no change: 8.0 classifies `warn` — "works, but no longer getting
> security fixes" — and 8.4 classifies `ok`, in all four places that ask
> (installer, admin dashboard, Server Information, health probes).
>
> **The reserved words that come with 8.4, and the trap in the host's own
> notice.** DreamHost's e-mail names four new reserved words: `MANUAL`,
> `PARALLEL`, `QUALIFY`, `TABLESAMPLE`. **Measured on a real MySQL 8.4.13, only
> TWO of those are reserved** — `qualify` and `tablesample`. `manual` and
> `parallel` are keywords but NOT reserved, and work unquoted as identifiers.
> Building a guard from the notice would have failed on a column named
> `manual`, which is a thoroughly plausible name, and a check that cries wolf
> gets switched off (#483). **Take a reserved-word list from MySQL, never from
> a summary of MySQL.** None of the four appears anywhere in this schema, the
> migrations or the PHP, so the upgrade needs nothing from us.
>
> **`tools/audit-checks/check_reserved_words_quoted.py` is the guard (#718),
> and `fullText` is the only identifier it has to allow.** Exactly one of 229
> tables names a reserved word: `tblTranscript.fullText`, which is `FULLTEXT`.
> MySQL 8.0 reached the end of its extended support in April 2026.
> Everything here still assumes it. The automated database test covered only
> `mysql:8.0.36` until 25 September 2026; it is now built to run on MySQL
> 8.4.11 as well (not yet run on GitHub — it runs on pull requests and on
> updates to the release branches that change `web/_sql/`). **Since 12 October
> 2026 it also upgrades two older databases** — one built from the last release
> (the `main` branch) and one from the install script of 6 March 2026 — and
> requires each to end up equal to a fresh install. It also holds the
> installer's replay against the install script alone, and loads the install
> script a second time. It still cannot stand for a database whose history
> matches neither, and none of this has yet run on GitHub. MySQL 8.4 refuses a
> link that points at a non-unique key (ERROR 6125, #552);
> `tools/audit-checks/check_fk_references_unique_key.py` checks for that.
>
> Two things to be precise about, because loose wording has already caused
> confusion. First, **the version is now read, judged and acted on** — this
> changed on 10 September 2026 (#489). `Portal\Core\DbServer`
> (`web/_core/DbServer.php`) is the ONE place that decides whether a version is
> supported; the admin dashboard, the health page, the Server Information page
> and the installation wizard all call it, so they agree. The installer refuses
> to install onto a database too old to run the schema, and warns without
> blocking on anything else. **Do not write a second version check** — change
> the constants at the top of that file instead. **`DbServer::connect()` is also
> the ONE place under `web/` that opens a database connection (#593, 12 October
> 2026): it makes the connection strict, so a value that does not fit is an
> error on every installation. Never write `new mysqli` anywhere else under
> `web/`; `tools/db-strict-mode-selftest.php` fails if it finds one.** Second, **MariaDB IS now
> covered, but only by the calendar self-tests (#487, 28 October 2026).**
> `calendar-selftests.yml` runs its six tests on FIVE engines since 4 November
> 2026 — MySQL 9.7 and MariaDB 12.3 joined the three below, because one of those
> six steps depends on the engine's own grammar and the committed
> reserved-word list FAILED on both (see the #718 bullet). The three it has
> always run, with their measured results, are —
> `mysql:8.0.36`, `mysql:8.4` and `mariadb:11.4` — and all of them pass on
> each. Measured locally first, on MariaDB 11.4.13: feed-admin 933 passed with
> **one** skip, event-visibility 192, import-notifier 134, feed-resolver 208,
> feed-importer 139, and `full_schema.sql` loads clean (228 tables). The one
> skip is a limit of the TEST, not of MariaDB: `performance_schema` is OFF by
> default there and there is no `PS_CURRENT_THREAD_ID()`, so one part-D check
> has no instrument to ask — the portal queries `performance_schema` NOWHERE,
> which was checked rather than assumed. **The END-TO-END MIGRATION HARNESS NOW
> RUNS ON MARIADB TOO (#487, 28 October 2026), and that is the one that replays
> every migration.** All seven phases and all 42 checks pass on MariaDB
> 11.4.13 — the SAME 42 that pass on MySQL 8.0.36 and 8.4.11, counted rather
> than assumed, because the MariaDB log is 1,284 lines shorter and the
> difference is exactly the password warning the `mariadb` client does not
> print (2,976 + 1,284 = 4,260 precisely). `e2e-migrations.yml` now runs three
> jobs side by side. Two things in the HARNESS, never in the portal, had
> stopped it: MariaDB 11.x ships no `mysql` or `mysqladmin` program (only
> `mariadb` and `mariadb-admin`), and the version check compared the server's
> whole reported string, which `11.4.13-MariaDB-ubu2404` never matches.
> `E2E_DB_ENGINE` (`mysql` or `mariadb`) is now the ONE place that decides the
> image and both client program names; the version check compares the leading
> three-part number, which keeps the **moving-tag guarantee** exactly as it was
> — `8.4` still does not pass for `8.4.11`. **Never say it is "exactly as
> strict as before" without that qualifier:** it accepts `8.0.36-log` and
> `8.0.36-0ubuntu0.24.04.1`, which the old whole-string compare refused, so it
> is a deliberate loosening in that one way. It also refuses when the engine
> asked for is not the engine that answered — a cheap last line of defence
> only, since the engine and the image come from one variable and
> `wait_for_mysql` fails first on MariaDB 11.x.
>
> **The one real difference it found, and the trap in it.** Phase 2 failed on
> MariaDB because `tblEvents`' two links to `tblVenues` and `tblVenueRooms`
> came out `on update RESTRICT` where MySQL says `on update NO ACTION`. The
> first guess — that the servers normalise what they are told — was WRONG, and
> measuring it on all three disproved it: every engine records a DECLARED rule
> faithfully, and **only the default for an OMITTED clause differs** (MySQL
> 8.0/8.4 → `NO ACTION`, MariaDB 11.4 → `RESTRICT`). Migration 179 gives those
> two links `ON DELETE SET NULL` and no `ON UPDATE` at all, which is why they
> were the pair that showed it. The two words name the same rule in InnoDB, so
> `dump_inventory()` folds the two together in ONE place and prints them as the
> PAIR `NO ACTION/RESTRICT`. **Be careful how that is reported.** The run's
> verdict and all 42 `✓` lines are unchanged on MySQL (diffed), but saying the
> MySQL output is "byte-identical" is WRONG and was corrected after the
> independent check caught it: 26 links in `full_schema.sql` DECLARE
> `ON DELETE RESTRICT`, so their recorded wording changes. That is also why the
> fold prints the pair rather than one word — picking `NO ACTION` made a
> failure line read `on delete NO ACTION` for a constraint whose SQL says
> `RESTRICT`, so anybody grepping the schema for the word in the message would
> find nothing. **What the fold gives up, plainly: the harness can no
> longer tell a declared `RESTRICT` from a declared `NO ACTION`, and 54 places
> in `web/_sql` do declare `RESTRICT`.** That costs nothing real — the two
> behave identically — but it is not the place to catch such an edit, and on
> MariaDB an OMITTED clause and an explicit `RESTRICT` are the same text — but
> do NOT stretch that into "MariaDB cannot tell them apart", which is false and
> was measured false: it records a DECLARED `NO ACTION` and a DECLARED
> `RESTRICT` distinctly, exactly like MySQL. Note too that the failure was
> never in the comparison, which is always same-engine and found the same two
> links on both: it was the HARD-CODED exception list, written in one engine's
> spelling. Also worth knowing: MariaDB's default server collation is
> `utf8mb4_uca1400_ai_ci`, a third value alongside MySQL 8.0's
> `utf8mb4_0900_ai_ci` and the schema's own `utf8mb4_general_ci`.
>
> **Still do not say "MariaDB is supported" without saying what was measured.**
> Two automated bodies of work now pass on MariaDB 11.4 — the calendar
> self-tests and the migration harness — and that is a great deal more than
> before, but it is not the same as a customer running on it in production.
>
> `Portal\Core\DbServer` reads DreamHost's real version string correctly —
> `8.0.41-0ubuntu0.24.04.1` is parsed as MySQL 8.0.41 and judged `warn`,
> non-blocking, advising the customer to ask their host about 8.4. Measured
> against the owner's own `SELECT VERSION()` on 28 October 2026, together with
> the `-log` suffix, Ubuntu packaging, MariaDB's fake `5.5.5-` prefix and full
> Debian packaging. Nothing needed fixing.
>
> **MySQL 9.7 AND MariaDB 12.3 ARE NOW PROVED, AND THE MIGRATION HARNESS RUNS
> ON FIVE ENGINES (#475 part 1, 3 November 2026).** MySQL 9.7.4 and MariaDB
> 12.3.3 each pass all seven phases and all 42 checks — **the same 42** that
> pass on MySQL 8.0.36, 8.4.11 and MariaDB 11.4.13, counted rather than
> assumed. **Nothing here had ever been run on MySQL 9.x or MariaDB 12.x
> before**, so `MYSQL_SUPPORTED_SERIES` and `MARIADB_SUPPORTED_SERIES` had been
> naming them supported on the strength of nobody having tried. Both are in
> `e2e-migrations.yml`'s matrix now. Keep writing SQL to the MySQL 8.0 ∩
> MariaDB convention below: following a convention is still not the same as
> proving compatibility, and the convention is what makes five engines pass.
> **MySQL 9.7 has no `SHA1()`, `SHA()` or `MD5()` SQL functions (#887,
> measured on 9.7.4: ERROR 1305)**: every named lock was built with `SHA1()`,
> so on 9.7 nobody could sign in with a password. Write `LEFT(SHA2(…, 256),
> 40)`; `tools/audit-checks/check_no_removed_sql_functions.py` is the hard
> gate. A BOUND impossible date reads as a real one on 9.7: every site that relied on the database refusing one now checks in PHP first (#891, #892, #951 — `Giving::isRealDay()`; use `>= ? AND <= ?`, not `BETWEEN`, over a range that may hold one).
>
> **MySQL 8.0 AND MariaDB 10.6 STAY ACCEPTED, WITH A WARNING — the owner's
> decision, 3 November 2026. DO NOT "TIDY" `MIN_MYSQL` OR `MIN_MARIADB`
> UPWARDS.** Asked whether the portal should refuse a database that no longer
> gets security fixes, the owner chose the warning. MySQL 8.0's support ended
> 30 April 2026 and MariaDB 10.6's on 6 July 2026, so `MIN_MYSQL` stays
> `8.0.0` and `MIN_MARIADB` stays `10.6.0` on purpose. **That makes the warning
> the only protection there is**, which is why its truthfulness stopped being
> cosmetic.
>
> **WHAT THE PORTAL TELLS A CUSTOMER IS TESTED IS NOW DERIVED, AND TWO OF THE
> OLD SENTENCES WERE UNTRUE ON A CUSTOMER-FACING PAGE.** Every MariaDB answer
> said the tests "only ever run against MySQL" — **eight days after the harness
> began running on MariaDB 11.4** (#487) — and every supported-MySQL answer
> said they "run against the MySQL 8.0 line, so if you are on something newer
> you are ahead of what is routinely tested here", which told somebody on
> MySQL 9.7 that nobody had tested their version. Five copies in all, including
> the class docblock and the 8.0 message. All stale in the MODEST direction,
> which is the safer one and is not a defence. **`DbServer::TESTED_LINES` is
> the ONE list of the lines the HARNESS runs on and `testedLinesCaveat()` the
> ONE place the sentence is built**, differing BY LINE because that is what is
> true — the harness runs on none of MariaDB 10.6, 10.11 or 11.8, which the
> portal still accepts. **A constant alone just moves the staleness one step**,
> so `tools/audit-checks/check_tested_db_lines.py` compares it with the
> workflow's own matrix and FAILS BOTH WAYS, with no exception list. It
> describes the harness and NOTHING ELSE: the calendar self-tests run on their
> own engines, and folding them in would overstate both. Never write
> "supported" there, and never drop the sentence that nobody runs this portal
> on MariaDB in production. **How it was found is the transferable part: by
> PRINTING what `classify()` really says to a customer, not by reading it.** The
> sentence is correct English, correctly assembled, and wrong only against the
> world. (And print the WHOLE sentence — a first look truncated to 96
> characters produced a confident finding about MariaDB 10.6 that was false.)
>
> **PHP 8.5 IS LINTED NOW, AND `php-static-analysis.yml` MUST NOT BECOME A
> MATRIX.** It ran `php -l` on 8.4 only, so `PhpRuntime::PREFERRED_SERIES` —
> the version the portal RECOMMENDS — was never checked by the gate that exists
> to stop a syntax error reaching a customer. Both run as **two steps in one
> job**: a matrix renames the check to `PHP -l lint (8.4)`, and the ruleset
> "Protect main branch" requires `PHP -l lint` by that exact name (#474), so
> every pull request into `main` would wait for a check that never arrives.
>
> **SOMETHING WATCHES FOR END OF LIFE NOW.** `tools/check-end-of-life.py` reads
> the supported-version lists OUT OF `DbServer` and `PhpRuntime` and compares
> them with endoflife.date; `end-of-life-watch.yml` is SET to run it monthly and has run it NEVER — a `schedule` trigger cannot fire from a branch that is not the default one, so the monthly run begins at the merge (#754); its `pull_request` trigger is path-filtered to four files and can run it sooner. It keeps
> exactly ONE issue, rewrites its body every run and comments only when the
> findings CHANGE. It is **not** in `tools/audit-checks/` because it needs the
> internet and everything in that folder must run offline. It earned its place
> on the first run: **PHP 8.4 loses active support on 31 December 2026** and
> `MIN_PHP` is 8.4.0. Also found, neither urgent: **MySQL has moved to
> year-based release lines** (26.7, 26.10) above the 9.7 we record as newest
> known, and MariaDB has 13.0.

- **Version:** `web/_core/version.php` is the single source of truth, and since D-024 it is set ONCE, when a release is built (`package.yml`) — **never bumped on `alpha` or `beta`**. Read it rather than quoting it: `php -r 'echo require "web/_core/version.php";'`.
- **Brand layer:** the product name is chosen at install (#296); presets and per-brand assets are set in `web/_core/brand-defaults.php` and read through `Site::productName()`.
- **Licence:** All Rights Reserved — MWBM Partners Ltd (t/a MWservices)
- **Repo:** github.com/MWBMPartners/WebMS-Intra
- **Server (an example of one customer's set-up, not a default):** portal.millrdsdacambridge.uk
- **Full brief:** `.claude/ProjectBrief_Chat.claude`
- **Living feature inventory:** [FEATURES.md](../FEATURES.md) (always check this first)
- **Chronological history:** [CHANGELOG.md](../CHANGELOG.md)
- **Dev-facing technical notes:** [DEV_NOTES.md](../DEV_NOTES.md)
- **The mail library** lives in `web/_core/maillib/` (#592) and must reach for nothing of the portal; `php tools/maillib/tests/stands-alone.php` enforces that from a list of what the library may use (`check_maillib_stands_alone.py` only starts it). Its test is `php tools/maillib/tests/run.php`. Since part two (#592) `Mailer::send()` uses it for the SMTP route, through `web/_core/MailRoutes.php`, the ONE place that reads the SMTP settings and the installation's choice of route, and decrypts the mailbox's password (never read `$SETTINGS['mail']['provider']`). Its page test is `tools/mail-pages-selftest.php`. Since part three a message that cannot be sent at once is kept in the mail queue (`web/_core/MailQueue.php`, which holds every statement that moves a queue row; test `tools/mail-queue-selftest.php`) and sent by `/cron/mail-queue`; `Mailer::send()` answers true for sent OR kept, so anything that records or shows a result asks `Mailer::lastOutcome()`. `MailQueue::jobIsRunning()` is the ONE place that says whether the scheduled job is running (only the job's own page records that it ran); while it is not, the portal sends as it did before the queue. Every queue moment is UTC from `UTC_TIMESTAMP()`; a time on a clock becomes one through `MailQueue::useBy()`. An event's time zone is `tblEvents.timezone` (the only zone column an event has). The certificate is always checked unless a global administrator has deliberately decided otherwise: `MailRoutes::certificateCheck()` is the ONE place `mail.smtp.certCheck` is read (`on`, `off`, or `only:<fingerprint>`; anything else counts as `on`). A failure holds mail, and so does restoring a backup. The three ways past a failed check (one held message, accept only this certificate, switch off) are on the e-mail page, for a global administrator and SMTP only, through the one chain at the top of `web/_apps/admin/integrations/email.php`. **The e-mail page is the ONE place the mail server's name and the certificate setting are changed: the general Settings page refuses them (`MailRoutes::lockedInGeneralEditor()`).** Checking switches itself back on (`MailRoutes::recheckCertificate()`, asked at every run of the job whatever route is in use), and the standing warning is read from what is stored (`MailRoutes::standingWarning()`), never from a session and never from which route is in use; its `publicLine` names nobody and is what the health line shows. A send made for ONE message past the check must never clear the fault on record because it got through. **A fault on record for the SMTP mailbox stops mail only while SMTP is the route in use: `MailRoutes::standingProblem()` is the ONE place that says whether a fault stands in the way of mail, and `certificateOnRecord()` / `certificateProblem()` are for showing only.** Test: `tools/mail-queue-pages-selftest.php`. Sending a test, from the e-mail page or the Integrations page, is for a global administrator only: one mail route serves every organisation.
- **Several organisations (#502, #511).** `Site::preDetect()` is the ONE place the organisation of a request is decided, and `SiteGate` (called in `web/public_html/index.php` just before the router) is the ONE gate that refuses a signed-in person who is not a member. A page reached without the router (`error.php`) must ask `SiteGate::wouldStop()` itself. Do not write a second membership test in a page; `Site::organisationsFor()` is the one list of "your organisations". Test: `tools/site-modes-selftest.php`. **Every address a page writes is built by `Site::url()`** (#536): never a bare `/page`. In a script printed by PHP use `Router::urlForScript()`; in a script file, `Portal.url()`. `tools/audit-checks/check_site_aware_addresses.py` fails on a bare address anywhere it reads: the list of places still to sweep, `site_aware_addresses_pending.txt`, has been EMPTY since 13 October 2026 and nothing may be added to it (`--strict` fails if a line appears). `tools/site-addresses-selftest.php` reads the real pages. **A "come back to" address taken from a request goes through `Auth::safeRedirectUrl()` and nothing else (#620)** — no page judges one itself; `tools/audit-checks/check_redirect_targets_judged.py` is the hard gate (presence only, D-065), and the newsletter click counter forwards only to the address `Newsletter::linkTarget()` stored for that newsletter. `tools/audit-checks/check_registered_addresses.py` fails on an address written out whole in `Site::url()`, `Router::url()`, `Router::urlForScript()` or `Portal.url()` that nothing answers (an `api/` address also needs its `api.{app}.{action}.enabled` switch seeded), and on two built addresses glued together; a new address the router answers by itself must be added to the check (#612). Three things the sweep taught: never join two built addresses (`Site::url('a') . Site::url('b')` prints the organisation's part twice); a page that ACCEPTS a come-back address by a pattern must accept it with the organisation's part too, and still refuse one that leaves the portal; and a class used by its short name needs its `use` line in THAT file, which only fails when the line runs. `Site::FIRST_PART_MODE_READY` STAYS `false`: the empty list is not enough. Still to settle, written up beside #536: the service worker, the installable app, the chat box on `/live`, the return address of a Microsoft or Google sign-in, and links in a push or e-mail made by a scheduled job (a job has no organisation's part; those belong to `PortalAddress`).
- **The release channel is decided in ONE place, `Portal\Core\Channel`** (#499, #500; owner, 12 October 2026): the `PORTAL_ENV` setting, else the `.channel` file beside `_core`, else a web folder named exactly `public_html`, `public_html_beta` or `public_html_dev` (that last one is the `dev` channel), else ASSUMED LIVE with errors hidden. Do not write a second channel check, do not read `DOCUMENT_ROOT`, and do not bring back "fail closed" (it was built first and locked members out). `PORTAL_WEBROOT` is the web folder: never build a path with `public_html` typed in. Test: `tools/channel-selftest.php`.
- **Secret settings (#597, #497).** Secret settings: `Portal\Core\SecretSettings` is the ONE place that decides what a stored secret means. Do not write a second mark comparison or a second decrypt. `$SETTINGS` holds real secrets: never decrypt a value taken from it, and never print one. Test: `tools/settings-secrets-selftest.php`. The key file: any length is a working key; only empty or "not a file" is no key. Never refuse a short one. `Captcha::verify()` takes the form's name as its second argument; there is no default. A form with no widget shows `Captcha::closedNotice()` itself. An address that is recorded or sent goes through `Logger::withoutJobToken()`.
- **Text messages held for quiet hours (#610).** `Portal\Core\Sms` holds every statement that moves a held text; `sendHeld()` is called only by `/cron/mail-queue`, after the mail step, and can never stop it. `Sms::NEVER_HELD` is the ONE list of what quiet hours never hold (a critical alert, an emergency notice: the owner, 13 October 2026 — D-028). A held text is `status = 'queued'` with `errorMsg = 'quiet-hours'` and a use-by moment (`heldUseBy`, UTC); it is taken once, by filling `claimedAt` in one statement, and a claim is never undone. Never twice comes before always delivered. A text with no use-by moment is never sent. When a held text comes due, `Sms::whyNotThisPerson()` asks `Site::isOwnOrganisation()` whether the person may still be sent it: do not write a second membership test. The job page's answer stays a status word and plain numbers (the texts step's begin `texts`). A restore calls `Sms::holdRestored()`. The broadcast page's sentence is `Sms::broadcastSummary()`, and neither it nor the preferences page may say a held text will NOT be sent: `MailQueue::jobIsRunning()` means "the job ran in the last 15 minutes" and no more. Test: `tools/held-texts-selftest.php`, which reads the Notification preferences page.
- **The portal's own QR pictures (#623).** `Portal\Core\Qr` is the ONE place a QR picture is made (`QrEncoder` is its own helper); nothing under `web/` may ask another website to draw one, and `Totp::getQrUrl()`, which sent the authenticator key to `chart.googleapis.com`, is removed and must not come back. A picture of anything secret is shown with `Qr::dataUri()`, written into the page: a picture fetched from an address puts the text in that address, and addresses reach server logs and browser histories. The format marks were written back to front until 14 October 2026, so every picture the portal had ever drawn was unreadable by every reader and nothing noticed, because a wrong picture still draws — only reading one back catches it. Two pages made the same outside-request mistake and it was invisible on one and live on the other: a page that writes its own `<head>` (such as `calendar/public-landing.php`) sends no page rule, so its outside request really happens. **The rule had NO GUARD FOR JAVASCRIPT until 29 October 2026**, and that is how `web/public_html/assets/noticeboard/noticeboard.noeval.js` kept an `api.qrserver.com` fallback in `qrSrc()`. It is UNREACHABLE — `web/_apps/noticeboard/index.php` sets `NoticeboardHost.qrUrl`, which always returns the portal's own `api/noticeboard/qr` address, and `qrSrc()` takes that first — but nothing checked that, and the bundle is GENERATED from `dc-runtime`, which is NOT in this repository, so the fallback cannot be removed here. `tools/audit-checks/check_no_outside_qr_service.py` now guards both halves: no new outside QR service anywhere under `web/` (counted per file, so a second one in an already-listed file fails too), and **the `qrUrl` host function must still be there** — because the moment it goes, the fallback is live and nothing looks broken. **That guard was WRONG TWICE and planting caught both — keep this, it is the most useful thing here.** Version 1 searched for the plain string `qrUrl`, so renaming the function to `qrUrlRENAMED` left it as a PREFIX and the check passed on a guard that no longer existed. Version 2 required the name and the address as TWO INDEPENDENT patterns anywhere in the file, which passed on BOTH states it exists to catch — the function COMMENTED OUT (all the text still there) and the function kept but `return '';` (the address still in a note above it) — while going RED on `"qrUrl": function`, a quoted key any formatter may produce. **Missing the real fault and crying wolf, in one check.** Version 3 asks the question the guard is about: is there a LIVE `qrUrl` property whose body RETURNS the portal's own address, with comments stripped and the two facts in ONE place? It states plainly what it still cannot see (a QR host not on its list, and a host name built by concatenation — both measured). Tests: `tools/qr-selftest.php` with `tools/qr-test-reader.php`, which is proved against `qrencode`'s own pictures before it is pointed at ours.
- **What a shared link looks like, and the pre-push gate (#569).** `web/_core/templates/head-meta.php` is the ONE place the meta description, canonical address and Open Graph / Twitter tags are written. `header.php` requires it, and so does every page that draws its own `<head>`; set `$pageTitle` and, where the page can do better than the fallback, `$pageDescription`, before requiring it. **The canonical address and `og:url` are left out entirely while `PortalAddress::visitorCanChooseHost()` is true** — the fallback is a host name the requester chose, and a canonical built from it points search engines and preview services at a name an outsider picked. **They are also left out on any error answer (400 or above), decided by the response code in `head-meta.php` alone (#823)**; a page reachable at two addresses sets `$pageCanonicalPath` to the one the portal links to (`settings/index.php` names `settings`, the Dashboard `''`). `og:image` only where the brand in use has the square raster on disk (two of seven; #656), and `twitter:card` is `summary`, not `summary_large_image`, because the picture is square. **Do not count the own-`<head>` pages by searching:** three reasonable searches gave 27, 15 and 12, because a doctype may sit inside an e-mail body built as a PHP string and because a page can draw its own head for the main view AND include `header.php` for an error branch. The list that matters is the exemption list in `tools/audit-checks/check_page_head_tags.py`, which names each exempt file and why. That check was WRONG THREE TIMES and planting caught each: string literals stripped across the whole file (an HTML attribute's quote paired with one inside PHP and swallowed the `<title>`); comments not stripped before pairing quotes (an apostrophe in `// don't` put every later quote out of step); and asking only whether the text `head-meta.php` appeared anywhere, which a commented-out require satisfied. **Its most valuable guard is on `header.php`'s own require**, because ordinary pages write no `<title>` of their own and a page-by-page check would never notice its deletion. Behaviour is proved by `tools/page-head-selftest.php` over real HTTP, whose part E plants the fault. **`.githooks/pre-push`** (turned on per clone by `tools/install-git-hooks.sh`, setting `core.hooksPath`; per repository on purpose — a global hooks folder overrides every other project's) runs `php -l` on the PHP the push range touches **as it stands on disk, not as committed** plus **every script in `tools/audit-checks/`** (the hook globs the folder, so a new check is picked up with no change to the hook), about nine seconds. **The count is deliberately not written down here** — it said 38 while there were 40, because two packages added checks without updating this line; count them with `ls tools/audit-checks/check_*.py | wc -l`. It does NOT run the self-tests or the migration harness, and note `tools/api-spec-check-selftest.py` lives in `tools/` and so is NOT globbed by the hook, and `git push --no-verify` skips it.
- **Offboarding really erases now, and the sweep has three traps (#569, migration 226).** `_apps/offboarding/do.php` **files** an erasure request through `GdprEraser::fileRequest($siteId, $userId, true)` — a fourth way a request gets confirmed, now written into that method's docblock — and never erases inline: there is ONE audited erasure path and a second would have to agree about 35 tables for ever. `offboarding.erasureMode` is `on_request` (the default; it waits on Admin, Erasure requests) or `after_undo_window` (the daily sweep runs it once `offboarding.undo_window_days` has passed). **Neither erases at the moment of offboarding**, because `rehire.php` exists; immediate erasure is #663. **`rehire.php` cancels a request still waiting** — without that the default mode left a live request to erase somebody who had returned. In `run_offboarding_erasure_sweep()` (`_apps/cron/_retention-sweep.php`): **join to the NEWEST offboarding row per person, never to any row** (an old row made a request filed today due tonight, bypassing the window — measured); **claim the request** with the same conditional `UPDATE … WHERE status = 'pending_review'` the staff page uses, or the Execute button stays armed mid-run and GdprEraser's own put-back and still-going updates match nothing; and **never log the fault's own words**, which can quote the value being erased. It cannot honour a per-organisation mode: `tblOffboarding` has no organisation column. The form offers TWO options, not three, because the portal does one kind of erasure. **Nothing yet covers this end to end over HTTP** — the SQL was proved against a real database and the rest was read, so a page-level test is still owed.
- **A member's notification choices have one writer (#667).** `Portal\Core\NotifyPrefs::merge()` is the one writer of a member's choices (an erasure empties the column; a restore replaces it). It changes only the switches a form marks as drawn (`prefsShown[<key>]`, drawn by `NotifyPrefs::shownMarker()`), inside its own transaction with the row read `FOR UPDATE`, and keeps every other stored key; `NotifyPrefs::SETTABLE` is the one list of settable choices. Removing a switch from a page is now ONE change and loses nobody's stored value. Never write a choice into `tblUsers.notifyPrefs` anywhere else. Test: `tools/notify-prefs-selftest.php`. **Removing a feature must not remove the test guarding a past fault:** section F of `go-live-button-selftest.php` is the only guard for #612 and pins a named list of keys rather than a count. `||` is **logical OR** in MySQL, not concatenation. A **`disabled` control is not submitted**, so disabling the GoCardless option would have made Save switch the provider to Stripe silently.
- **One place hashes and stores a password (#728).** `Auth::setPassword()` and `Auth::setUnusablePassword()` are the ONE place a sign-in password is hashed and written; `storeHash()` behind them is the only writer. **EIGHT places did it with their own SQL** and every one was correct about hashing — what could not be done was enforce a RULE anywhere, and #586 was such a rule. They own the storing and **deliberately NOT who may set a password**: each caller keeps its own gate, and moving those would be a behaviour change dressed as a refactor. The gate is `tools/audit-checks/check_one_password_writer.py`, failing BOTH ways; test `tools/password-writer-selftest.php` (53 checks, real MySQL). **THE EMPTY STRING IS THE UNUSABLE MARKER AND IT IS ENFORCED** — `Auth::loginLocal()` refuses an empty hash BEFORE `password_verify()` is reached, `offboarding/do.php` writes it, the runbook reads `passwordHash <> ''` as "has a password", and `admin/users/save.php`'s #586 read-back treats zero length as nothing stored. A random hash is unmatchable but **UNRECOGNISABLE**, so `users/api/create.php` left every account it made reading as though it had a working password; the claim that an empty marker "could be made to succeed" is FALSE, measured on PHP 8.5 for `''`, `'0'`, `'x'` and `'*'`. Check `forgot-password/save.php` puts no condition on the hash before relying on this — it does not, so the reset route stays open. **REFUSING BEATS CHOOSING:** `userID` carries no unique key, so an account can have two rows, and a draft took `MIN(localID)` — deterministic, but it leaves the OTHER row's old password working, so a member who resets because they fear compromise is still signable-in with the password they just replaced. **`offboarding/do.php` is a STATED exception, not an oversight:** taking a password away must reach EVERY row (`WHERE userID = ?`), and it is one step in a uniform list of audited steps. **THE INSTALLER HAS NO AUTOLOADER** — it requires every class by hand, so the call needed a `require_once` or it was a fatal "class not found" on EVERY installation, which `php -l` cannot see; safe because `Auth.php` loads standalone and those three methods reach no class but `self` (section A pins it). **AND SECTIONS A-G OF THE TEST PASSED 49 OF 49 WITH THE READ-BACK WEAKENED** to "does a row exist" — the old `LENGTH(passwordHash)` question, the very thing they were written to prove was stronger: they all check the database before and after, so they prove the write LANDED, while the read-back only matters where the write lands as something ELSE. Section H makes that state with a `BEFORE UPDATE` trigger. **When a check exists to prove one question is stronger than another, build the one case where the two answers differ** — everything else passes on both. **FOUR places READ the empty marker and ONE WRITES it**, and a first version of this rule said "three places read" while citing `admin/users/save.php`'s #586 read-back — which #728 DELETED, so it cited as evidence the thing it removed, missed two real readers and called the writer a reader. READ: `Auth::loginLocal()` (before `password_verify()` is reached), `auth/account/change-password.php`, `auth/2fa/disable.php`, and the disaster-recovery runbook's `passwordHash <> ''`. WRITES: `offboarding/do.php`. **AND I CLAIMED A RACE WAS IMPOSSIBLE WHEN IT WAS NOT** — "nothing in this portal creates a second one" stood in five places and is FALSE, measured: two connections, each in its own transaction, both setting a password for the SAME account with DIFFERENT sign-in names both reported success and left TWO rows, because under REPEATABLE READ neither `COUNT(*)` sees the other's insert. The harm is what follows — that account can never have a password set again while BOTH old passwords keep working, so the member who most needs to change theirs cannot. The unique key is on `username`, so it catches only the case where the names MATCH. It locks the account's own `tblUsers` row before counting (the PARENT, because the row being guarded against does not exist yet), and that lock is real ONLY inside a caller's transaction (#697) — every caller that can create a row is inside one, and the one that is not passes no sign-in name so can only update. Making the lock real made a lock-wait timeout reachable, which throws and would escape with the transaction open (#694's round-3 shape): checked, every creating caller catches and rolls back. Section I needs TWO REAL PROCESSES — two connections driven one after the other from one process just deadlock. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "One place hashes and stores a password (#728)".
- **Every scheduled job records that it ran, and keyed by JOB not by token (#775, migration 253; D-070, D-071).** `Portal\Core\ScheduledJobs` is the ONE place a run is recorded and read; do not read `tblScheduledJobRun` anywhere else. Fifteen `cron/` addresses are called by a customer's scheduler and until now only ONE left a trace, so a portal could silently stop sending reminders, stop clearing personal data past its retention period, or stop taking backups, with no error and nothing on any page — and the retention sweep IS how the portal keeps its promise about deleting data. **`maintenance.cronToken` alone covers FIVE jobs**, so keyed by token "the backup has not run for a month" could not be told from "the mail queue ran a minute ago". **OVERDUE IS PER JOB** (D-070): 15 minutes for the mail queue, 2 hours for event reminders, 6 for feed imports, 8 days for giving statements, 36 hours for the jobs that really run once a day, and — since **#793 (D-076)** — every frequent job an allowance from its own timing (three times the longest gap its own page states, at least 30 minutes; seven jobs moved, `cron/health` among them) — one number would let the mail queue sit dead for a week or complain about the backup daily, and two tiers would make statements read overdue six days in seven (#483). **A job with no record is "nothing is known yet", NEVER a warning and never a backfilled time** (D-071): warning would give every correct portal a page of warnings the morning after an upgrade, and backfilling would invent evidence in the one place whose job is saying what is true; the never-configured case is still caught by the token check, which IS knowable. **RECORDING MUST NEVER STOP THE JOB IT RECORDS** — files reach a server before anybody presses "Run migrations" (#694), so `began()`/`finished()` answer false rather than throwing, and the cost is stated: a failed recording is silent and the page then under-claims. **THE FAULT IT SHIPPED FIRST IS THE LESSON: **EIGHT** pages EXIT EARLY when switched off, locked, or with nothing due, and those TWELVE paths recorded a start and no finish — ten are refusals and two are genuine failures, both in pages already among the eight; a thirteenth early finish is in `health.php`'s handled catch, which is not an early-exit path, so NINE pages record one in all. **Three documents first called all twelve refusals** — in the very comment written to stop a reader misclassifying one — **and the correction then said "nine pages" and "twelve paths" in one breath, which cannot both be true** — so a job somebody had deliberately switched off read as "started and never finished", as a PROBLEM, for ever.** Every such path records a finish now. **THREE outcome words, not two:** a first version had `refused` doing both "switched off" and "ran and failed", and the page words `refused` as switched off — so **a nightly backup that FAILED would have been reported as switched off**, untrue in the reassuring direction, about backups; an unrecognised word stores as `failed`, because `refused` is a reassurance nobody has earned. **A broken READ is its own answer too** (`COULD_NOT_READ`): both used to be NOT_KNOWN, so a half-applied migration got the reassuring "it starts being watched the first time it runs" when nothing was going to start. That also exposed the `SHOW TABLES LIKE` guard as doing nothing observable — **a guard nothing can distinguish from its absence is not a guard**; `state()`'s is load-bearing now and planted, `began()`'s still cannot be and that is SAID rather than pretended. Guards: the hard gate `tools/audit-checks/check_scheduled_jobs_recorded.py`, which asks PHP's TOKENISER because every page quotes its own gate in a docblock (a text search finds the prose), fails BOTH ways on the job list, and pins the security property that **the start is recorded AFTER the token gate** — otherwise anybody on the internet could make a dead job look alive; and `tools/scheduled-jobs-selftest.php` (real MySQL; **count the checks from the file, never from here**) which DRIVES all fifteen pages over HTTP with a wrong token and with none, and TWO of them through a real run including a switched-off one, because the gate states plainly that it cannot see whether the code RUNS, nor **one exit path losing its finish** (planted, green, recorded — requiring a finish before every `exit()` was tried and cries wolf, since the normal end is also an exit and ONE — `health.php` — ends in a re-throwing catch; **that said "two" in four places and the count was never run**: PHP's tokeniser finds `T_THROW` exactly once under `web/_apps/cron/`, the conclusion surviving on the first half alone, the fifth wrong count in two packages). **ROUND 7 FOUND THE NESTING CHECK ITSELF WRONG BOTH WAYS and closed review under D-064/D-066, since every finding was in tooling or documents:** `\hash_equals(` read as NO GATE and a bare block read as a fault (false REDs on a hard gate, #483), while `{$x}` inside a string popped the enclosing `if` and `Foo::try()` replaced it (false passes). All four are fixed, the rule is applied in ONE place (`nesting_problem()`, which the gate's own test now asks instead of holding a copy), and each fix was planted back out and caught by its own case. What it still cannot see — the alternative `if (): endif;` syntax, and a magic parameter folded INTO the gate's own condition or guarding its `exit` — is **#794**; the self-referential H0c3 pin and four wording faults are **#795**. **Count the plants from the suites, never from here:** the gate's and the test's plant lists each print their own total, and this sentence has carried a stale pair twice — one half updated and the other not. It drives a real run on TWO of fifteen; the other thirteen real runs are **#789** (the refusal half is driven on all fifteen). **THE INDEPENDENT CHECK FOUND FOUR PORTAL FAULTS AND THREE WERE REASSURING SENTENCES — this package's own headline fault reached from new directions.** (1) **The all-clear asserted a finish for a job still RUNNING:** a run with no finish yet, inside its threshold, answered `RECENT`, had no branch of its own, and was swept into "All 15 of them recorded a finish" when fourteen had — so a nightly backup dying part-way at 02:00, the state the design calls "worth reporting", read as an all-clear for 36 hours, and a busy portal's mail job is mid-run often enough to show it routinely. `RUNNING_NOW` is its own status and the all-clear counts only what it can speak for. **Nothing caught it because C4 pinned the CLASSIFICATION and section G drove the page only with everything finished or one job overdue — no check asked what the PAGE said in C4's state.** (2) **`refused` means THREE things and the page named one** (switched off, locked, nothing due): every clause was false in the commonest state, and it IS the commonest — `backup-run` refuses six nights in seven on a weekly schedule, 27 in 28 on a monthly, so the page told administrators their backup was switched off permanently; it now says what is known and lists the three reasons (#791 would record which). (3) **The mail-job WARNING was deleted while its evidence survives the upgrade** — `jobLastRanAt` has history, the new table is empty on upgrade day, so a portal whose mail job had genuinely stopped was downgraded from a warning with an action to a note saying "Nothing"; "one page, two answers" does not apply when the new source has NO answer, so the older reading is used for the mail queue alone while the new record is silent. (4) **The overdue sentence asserted a token is set while the finding above said the opposite**, because `state()` never looks at a token. **AND THE GATE'S ONE SECURITY PROPERTY WAS BYPASSABLE TWO WAYS, neither in its blind-spot list: a gate that EXISTS but does not REFUSE** (deleting `exit();` left the gate green while the self-test went 3 red with "NOTHING was recorded" — an unauthenticated request writing the record, **a lesson already recorded TWICE**, #668 section D and `gdpr-coverage-selftest.php`), and **`hash_equals(` inside an ordinary string** (`php_code_only()` kept string CONTENTS while its docblock said it removed them, so ONE line put `began()` above the real gate on `cron/retention-sweep` with everything green). **THAT GUARD HAS NOW BEEN WRONG IN BOTH DIRECTIONS FOUR TIMES ACROSS FIVE VERSIONS, AND IS NOW DELETED, and each version was "verified by planting" the shapes its author had thought of:** v1 passed an exit inside a braced closure; v2 cried wolf on a real refusal one level deeper AND passed `$log->exit()` (a METHOD named exit — the #763 trap verbatim); v3 asked the TOKENISER but **passed `fn() => exit()`, because an arrow function has NO BRACES**, so a brace walk never enters its body — **one line restored v1's fault, and worse: an unauthenticated request got its 403 header and then RAN ON, writing `began()` and executing the outgoing-webhook retry sweep**; v3 also cried wolf on a braceless gate. v4 marked `T_FN` apart from `T_FUNCTION` — and **v4 was wrong BOTH WAYS TOO**: it stopped an arrow body at the first `{`, so `fn() => match (true) { default => exit('x') }` passed (v1's fault, new spelling — five such shapes measured), and it decided "braceless" by `;`-before-`{`, so an ordinary `$matches = hash_equals(...)` above the gate went RED on a hard gate (#483). v5 steps OVER braces in an arrow body and locates the gate as the `if` whose CONDITION encloses the comparison, one hop through an assignment, passing rather than accusing where it cannot locate one. **AND ROUND 6 SETTLED THE DESIGN QUESTION BY FINDING THE SHAPE NEITHER HALF COULD SEE: a gate the REQUEST decides whether to apply** — `if ((\$_GET['debug'] ?? '') !== '1') { …the gate… }` — which left every guard green while `?debug=1` let anybody on the internet write the record and run the job's work. **Driving does not catch it either, measured: with it planted the driven test stayed at 196 of 196**, because a wrong token sent WITHOUT the magic parameter meets the gate and is properly refused, and no test can guess a magic parameter. So **`gate_refuses()` IS DELETED, all five versions**, and D-065's rule splits the job honestly: "does it REFUSE" is behavioural and is now DRIVEN on ALL FIFTEEN pages (section I-ALL, a wrong token and none, row read back — `&& false` on a condition fails it); "is it NESTED" is structural and is pinned by `gate_enclosures()`, which requires every enclosing block to be a `try`, because a `try` cannot decide whether its body runs while an `if`, a loop or a function can — fourteen gates sit at file scope and `health.php`'s in its own `try`, so that is a rule and not an exception. **The lesson is not that the fifth pattern was bad: four of five versions were wrong in BOTH directions, which is what a check tells you when it is asked a question of the wrong KIND.** Its replacement has a committed test of its own (eleven cases, eight refused and three allowed, **both counts pinned** — round 6 showed the previous one's were not: deleting the two cases that cost rounds 4 and 5 left it printing "20 of 20" and exit 0). **AND BOTH OF THIS PACKAGE'S TESTS RAN NOWHERE AUTOMATIC** — `scheduled-jobs-selftest.php`, named in four documents and in the gate's own docstring as the guard for everything the gate cannot see, was in no workflow and not in `tools/run-all-selftests.sh`; `portal-check-selftest.php` was in a workflow GitHub has never registered (#754) and absent from the runner, **which is why that runner could not see the two missing-dependency breaks that happened in it**. The runner's own one-liner found three more missing (`clock-change`, relied on for twenty-two #777 rounds, and the two i18n tests); all five are wired now. **A failed read was also still reported as "nothing is known yet"** — round 3's catch sent it to `$notKnown`, so a half-applied upgrade got the reassuring "each one starts being watched the first time it runs" with `todo: Nothing`, at a LOWER severity than the version it replaced; it goes to `$unreadable` now. **And `$mailStopped` was a STRING whose emptiness meant two things**, so an empty description made the warning vanish AND the all-clear return — the headline fault a fifth time. **THE RULE ROUND 3 WROTE INTO THE CODE WAS ITSELF THE NEXT FAULT AS AN INSTRUCTION:** `$refused` violates it correctly (a refused run IS a finish) and a reader applying it literally would delete the all-clear from almost every portal; the stated reason for dropping the subtraction was false too. **And the route list read the schema as RAW TEXT**, so one future comment quoting a removed `cron/` address would block every pull request (#483) — it reads `INSERT INTO tblRoutes` only, with comments blanked TRACKING QUOTED STRINGS, which is #788's lesson from this same package. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "Every scheduled job records that it ran (#775)".
- **The PHP version is judged in ONE place (#640).** `Portal\Core\PhpRuntime` is the ONE place that decides anything about the PHP version: whether the portal can run on it (`MIN_PHP`, 8.4.0) and whether it is older than the release that fixed the October 2026 flaws (CVE-2026-109618 / CVE-2026-109619, "PHP2Shell", fixed 21 October 2026 in 8.5.12 and 8.4.27). The installer's checks screen, the admin dashboard, Server Information and the health page all ask it — **do not write a second version check; change the constants in that file.** `support` and `security` are returned SEPARATELY and must stay so: 8.3.36 carries the fix and is still too old to run the portal. **A version number cannot tell a vendor-patched build from an unpatched one**, so the wording is always "older than the release that fixed it — ask your hosting company" and NEVER "you are vulnerable" (section G of the test fails on that wording), and an unpatched PHP never blocks an installation — that decision is the one constant `BLOCK_INSTALL_WHEN_UNPATCHED` (`false`), read by the installer. **A FINISHED release of a line newer than `NEWEST_KNOWN_SERIES` counts as fixed; a PRE-RELEASE of one does NOT — it is "cannot tell".** That distinction is not a nicety: the first version of this said any newer line must be fixed "because it was published later", and PHP 8.6 disproved it (pre-releases from 2 July 2026, RC3 on 8 October, both BEFORE the 21 October fix), so 8.6.0RC3 was told it was safe. 8.6 is pinned at `8.6.0RC4` and the comparison keeps the pre-release marker, because `version_compare` ranks RC below the release — never compare a tidied three-part number. Going stale must make this less informative, never wrong. Pin every fixed-release number by hand in the test: deriving "one below" from the constant proves only that the constant agrees with itself, and a wrong number passed 66 checks. The tripwire must match `phpversion()` as well as `PHP_VERSION`. Tests: `tools/php-runtime-selftest.php` (no database, which also proves the class has no dependencies) and `tools/audit-checks/check_php_version_one_place.py`. **DO NOT MOVE THE 8.6 PIN TO `8.6.0` WHEN 8.6.0 SHIPS (#705, measured 2 November 2026 against a real 8.6.0RC4).** #705 asks for exactly that and calls it a dated action with a security consequence; it is WRONG and doing it causes the harm. The finished `8.6.0` ALREADY counts as supported and patched with the pin left at `8.6.0RC4`, because `version_compare` ranks a finished release above its own candidates — so NOTHING falls due on 19 November. Moving it warns everybody on 8.6.0RC4 that they are unpatched, and RC4 is the FIRST build carrying the fix: planted, that one edit turns RC4 from `ok` to `warn`. The constant means "the first BUILD carrying the fix", never "the first finished release". What does fall due is unrelated to it: `PREFERRED_SERIES` (the owner's, 8.5 on purpose) and `NEWEST_KNOWN_SERIES` (bump it when an 8.7 appears). Section C4 of the test pins this and explains it at the point of failure. **Three more 8.6 facts, each measured rather than read:** a deprecation survey of FUNCTIONS misses a LANGUAGE change — the BACKTICK operator is deprecated, used by two audit fixtures on purpose and by no portal code, and the pre-push hook judges `php -l` by its exit code, which is 0 for a deprecation. **It is 8.5 that deprecated it, NOT 8.6, and my first version of this line said 8.6:** measured on all three, 8.4.27 is clean and 8.5.11 and 8.6.0RC4 both warn, so the notices were already printing on the RECOMMENDED version before this work. Seeing a notice on the new version tells you nothing about whether the old one was quiet — a version claim is only measured if you measured the versions it distinguishes; seven filesystem TEST functions (`is_file`, `is_dir`, `file_exists`, `is_readable`, `is_writable`, `filesize`, `filemtime`) THROW on a NUL byte in the path where 8.4/8.5 answered `false`, which is unreachable here only because every request-derived path is an `(int)` or passes a strict allow-list before any file call (the table is in DEV_NOTES.md; `strtotime()` does NOT reject a trailing NUL); and `zend.exception_ignore_args` still exists, so argument values still cannot reach a logged trace.
- **A member can add a password to their OWN account, and nobody else's (#731, migration 247).** `Portal\Core\SelfServicePassword` is the ONE place that decides whether somebody may, and the ONE place the proof that they have just signed in again is recorded, read and spent; it takes FACTS, so the page and the handler cannot disagree (#647), and the handler asks it TWICE — once cheaply, once on facts read inside the transaction that writes. **The proof may be recorded ONLY by a path that really re-authenticated somebody** (`Auth::loginLocal()`, the two single-sign-on callbacks, `_apps/auth/login/webauthn.php`): **never put a privilege grant behind nothing but an existing session**, because a session can be borrowed and that is the whole reason this gate exists. It is good for FIVE MINUTES, is SINGLE USE, is spent when the password is stored and not before, and is refused when it names somebody else, carries an unknown method, or comes from the FUTURE. It is recorded ABOVE the two-factor gate on purpose: that gate takes the sign-in away and redirects, so a proof written below it would never exist for a two-factor account and such an account could never add a password (for the password route that gate is in `_apps/auth/login/index.php`, NOT below in `loginLocalOneAtATime()` — a copied comment said "below" and was wrong). **`auth.password.selfAdd` is the setting and the STRICTER LEVEL WINS** (D-026, quoting D-013): `false` is stricter, so it is allowed only where the installation AND the organisation both say yes, read through `InstallationSettings::layer()` because `$SETTINGS` has already let the organisation's row win — a MISSING installation-wide value counts as NO, which switches the feature off between files landing on a server and somebody pressing "Run migrations", and that is the recoverable direction. It ships ALLOWED (`'1'`) because this is a RECOVERY route and #499's "fail closed" locked members out. **BUT AN ORGANISATION CANNOT EXPRESS ITS OWN "NO" THROUGH ANY PAGE, and three places said it could** — measured: nothing under `web/` writes the key outside the class, and `_apps/settings/save.php` REFUSES to add a per-organisation row for a key the installation already holds (the four `mail.queue.*` numbers are the only exception). That is #668's recorded shape, re-created; the rule is right, the control is #755, and section G of the test inserts that row with raw SQL — a state no administrator can produce. **The password is stored only through `Auth::setPassword()`** (#728), the sign-in name is the account's own e-mail address read from `tblUsers` and never the session, and the two refusals are worded by that method (#521: never whose name is taken). **An administrator STILL cannot do it for somebody else** (D-018, #586). **It is an ADDITION — the identity-provider link and every passkey survive, pinned by a test rather than intended** — recorded as `PasswordAdded` and NEVER `PasswordChanged`, with an e-mail to the account's own address so a stolen session cannot add a sign-in method quietly; sent AFTER the commit in its own try/catch, because a portal with a broken mail route must still have a recovery route. **`/login` cannot be used for the re-authentication** — it redirects away when `Auth::check()` is true, so a signed-in person bounces back having proved nothing — which is why the page offers the methods itself and carries a THIRD copy of the passkey browser plumbing (not of the ceremony: `login/webauthn.php` is still the one place an assertion is verified). **`Auth::comeBackAfterSignIn()` is the ONE place `login_redirect` is taken out of the session**, and it had to exist: FOUR places WROTE that key, each only in its two-factor branch, and only `_apps/auth/2fa/verify.php` read it, while the ordinary single-sign-on path ended `safeRedirectUrl($_GET['redirect'] ?? '/')` and a provider sends the person to the ONE address registered with it. Establish that with `git grep -n login_redirect 5f37e4f -- web/`, which answers thirteen lines — four writes, one read with its `unset`, SIX in comments and one in CODE (the `SESSION_KEYS_SAFE_TO_KEEP_OFFLINE` entry); an earlier note said "two in comments" and was wrong. **THREE THINGS WERE PINNED BY NOTHING AND THE INDEPENDENT CHECK FOUND ALL THREE.** Deleting the handler's whole `Auth::verifyCsrf()` gate left 88 of 88 GREEN — every other check fetches a real token first and E03's point is the opposite case, so the one thing standing between a member and a page that posts this form FOR them inside the proof window was guarded by nothing (E04b/E04c). Making the page RENEW the proof's moment each time it is drawn also left 88 green — a sliding window turns five minutes into "five minutes after you stop looking" (F02b/F02c) — and **my own first version of that check could not fail either**, because the run finishes inside a second so a renewal rewrites the same `time()`: the proof must be AGED first, inside the window, for a renewal to be visible. And deleting the SECOND `whyNotAllowed()` inside the transaction left it green too; **never say "the transaction and the lock protect it", which is untrue in the reassuring direction** — the lock makes two simultaneous adds SEQUENTIAL, it does not refuse the second, and `storeHash()` would simply UPDATE and answer success, so what refuses it is that second ask. Tests: `tools/self-service-password-selftest.php` (93 checks, real HTTP; seven plants, each proved applied and each caught by its named check — **count them from the commit message, never from here**, because the drafts claimed seven while six existed and the page had no scratch copy to plant into). **Three things it cannot do, said plainly: no test can drive a real Microsoft, Google or passkey sign-in**, so each person is given a password, signed in through the real page and then has the row deleted — the state a Microsoft-only account is really in; **five minutes cannot be waited for**, so the proof is aged inside the session's own FILE (the server runs with `session.serialize_handler=php` so the format is known, and the editor REFUSES rather than failing quietly); and **one worker means two people adding a password at once is untested**, and such a test would pass on the broken code (#647).
- **A session id the portal never issued is REFUSED (#705).** `Auth::prepareSessionStart()` sets `session.use_strict_mode` and is the ONE place it is set for the portal (the installer sets its own, because it cannot load `Auth`). Up to and including PHP 8.5 PHP ADOPTS any well-formed session id a request sends, even one naming no session, and starts a session under it. 8.6 turns this on by default; setting it ourselves keeps the behaviour the SAME on 8.4, 8.5 and 8.6, which matters more than the default. **DO NOT CALL IT A FIX FOR THE CLASSIC SIGN-IN FIXATION ATTACK — my first version did and the independent check killed it.** All six grants of `$_SESSION['user_id']` are ALREADY preceded by `session_regenerate_id(true)` (since #900 every one goes through `Auth::beginSessionFor()`, which calls it and also decides what of the session survives — a DIFFERENT person starts with only the browser's chosen language; the two-step code step now rotates too; `tools/audit-checks/check_sign_in_begins_session.py` (#902) fails when a grant has no `Auth::beginSessionFor()` before it in the same function — presence and order only; part K of `two-step-selftest.php` drives all six sign-ins, the passkey included), so the id somebody is signed in UNDER was always one PHP minted. **THEREFORE THIS SETTING DOES NOT MAKE THOSE ROTATIONS REDUNDANT:** strict mode refuses an unknown id, it does not rotate one at a privilege boundary, and removing them believing otherwise opens the real hole. What it DOES close is session state with no rotation in front of it — above all the kiosk hand-over, `_apps/assets/kiosk-action.php` line 208, which puts a REAL MEMBER'S account in the session in a file that rotates nowhere. **It must be set BEFORE `session_start()`**, which is why it lives there and not in `bootstrap.php`. It is NOT the same check as #509's, which drops a MALFORMED id; this one is about a well-formed id that names nothing. **A green suite proved only that nothing broke** — section L of `tools/sign-in-limits-selftest.php` is what proves it works, by sending such an id and finding, with the setting removed, `sess_<the id the request chose>` really written to disk. Its third check passes either way on purpose: it guards a hardening that breaks the page.
- **A file that could not be CLOSED is not written (#705).** `BackupArchive` discarded `fclose()`'s answer when finishing an archive and when writing each file out of one during a restore; `DbBackup` already did it right and is the pattern. A close fails on a full disk, so the old code left a truncated backup on disk named like a good one, and counted a truncated restored file as written. Two traps: **`fflush()` is NOT a substitute** (it pushes PHP's buffer at the OS, which can still fail at close — the old code checked `fflush` and threw the close away), and **a second `fclose()` THROWS a `TypeError`** on both 8.5 and 8.6 (measured; the resource closes either way), so the extraction path nulls the handle before throwing or the enclosing `finally` masks the honest message. `fclose()` has ALWAYS returned `false` here; 8.6 only reports more of it — that was NOT measured, so the claim to rely on needs no version at all.
- **The money pages: all four writers lock the claim, and a claim is editable only before anybody decides (#647, #651).** The FOUR writers of an expense claim — `approve/save.php`, `withdraw/save.php`, `treasury/save.php` and `edit/save.php` — each open a transaction, re-fetch the claim with `SELECT … FOR UPDATE`, and refuse when what they expected has changed. The payment page was the ONLY one without that until 28 October 2026, so **a claim could be paid twice**: two treasurers, or one double-click, each wrote a payment reference, each set `Reimbursed`, each sent the claimant an e-mail. A unique key on `tblExpenseClaimPayments(claimID)` is deliberately NOT the fix — `payAmount`'s own comment promises partial payments (#650). **`Portal\Core\ExpenseRules::whyNotEditable()` is the ONE place that decides whether a claim may be changed**, and it takes FACTS rather than a connection because the handler asks it TWICE: once cheaply, once on facts read inside the lock. Do not write a second status test in a page. The rule is "a claim NOBODY HAS DECIDED ON", never "a Pending claim": a claim in a department needing several approvals STAYS Pending after the first approval, so editing it then would leave an approval sitting against figures nobody approved. `$approvalCount !== 0`, never `> 0` — `approvalCount()` answers -1 when it could not read, and "we could not tell" must never become "nobody has, go ahead". Ownership is answered BEFORE the status, so a stranger is never told a claim exists or where it has got to. `/expenses/submit` lists the claimant's own claims (#834); editability is still asked only on the claim's own page. **Receipts (#649): once a claim's COMMIT has been sent, the submit handler never deletes a receipt file** — the server may have committed with only the reply lost; files are kept and logged by path, and a fresh connection only chooses the claimant's MESSAGE. Two earlier designs that let that look delete a saved claim's receipt are recorded in DEV_NOTES.md. **The HTTP harness cannot test the payment race**: `access-pages-selftest.php` runs the built-in server with one worker (`unset($env['PHP_CLI_SERVER_WORKERS'])`), so two requests never overlap and such a test PASSES ON THE BROKEN CODE. `ExpenseRules::paymentReferences()` is the ONE place payment references are read — it was inline on the page, and the page's test held its own COPY, so a change to the page alone passed every check and would have written an EMPTY payment reference against a real claim. **A test holding its own copy of the logic it tests is not testing anything.** Three more traps, all found by planting and all worth knowing: a check that searches a page for a CALL can be satisfied by an HTML COMMENT mentioning it (`codeOnly()` keeps inline HTML on purpose, so the `<script>` check can fail — use `phpOnly()` when you mean code); a brace-matching walk must step over STRING LITERALS, or one `{` in a log message swallows the rest of the function and the check passes on a removed gate; and `substr_count` of a shared SQL prefix cannot tell three statements apart, so name each one. Tests: `tools/expense-payment-race-selftest.php` (two real connections), `tools/expense-edit-selftest.php` (section D walks the fraud sequence at the level of the rule), `tools/expense-rules-selftest.php`. **And run the audit scripts and READ THEM: `check_schema_seed_parity.py` names a missing migration mark while exiting 0, which is how migration 223 nearly shipped unrecorded.** The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "The four money writers all lock the claim".
- **A unique key is POWERLESS over a column that can be empty (#487, migration 224).** In MySQL and MariaDB a unique key STANDS ASIDE for any row where any part of it is empty — an empty value is not even equal to itself — so the key is a rule only for rows that fill in every one of its columns. **`INSERT IGNORE` and `ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE` lean entirely on the key**, so where it stands aside they stop meaning "do nothing the second time" and start meaning "insert another row". That is how `tblEventAttendance` came to hold duplicate rows: `uq_attendance_unique` covered `(eventID, userID, walkinName, dayDate)`, a row names its person by EITHER `userID` OR `walkinName`, so no row ever filled in all four and the key refused NOTHING, for any row, ever — under a comment reading "idempotent via unique key", which stated the guarantee the schema had forgotten to implement (the #645 shape). **Do NOT say it "counted people twice in every figure": three code comments and two documents claimed that and it was false, caught by the independent check.** The grid keys its display by person and day and lists walk-ins with `SELECT DISTINCT`, and the two `Discipleship` joins feed an `INSERT IGNORE` whose key has no empty part, so nothing double-counted and nothing on screen was wrong. The cost was a duplicated "who marked this" trail and a figure that WOULD be wrong the moment anything counted rows rather than distinct people — which is the reason to fix it rather than trust every future reader to de-duplicate. The cure is a worked-out column, as `tblSettings`/`tblEmailTemplates` already do with `siteScope` = `COALESCE(siteID, -1)`; `VIRTUAL`, never `STORED`, because MySQL refuses a STORED generated column on a table carrying an ON DELETE CASCADE foreign key (ERROR 1215). **`attendeeRef` is deliberately EMPTY when the person is unknown, and that is the point, not an oversight:** `GdprEraser` ANONYMISES attendance rather than deleting it so the aggregate survives, two erased people cannot be told apart, and a key that collapsed them would delete the counts erasure exists to preserve — so the de-duplication's `WHERE` clause is load-bearing. Fourteen of the fifteen such keys in the schema are RIGHT, including every token column, and `tblWebhookEvent` is deliberately left alone (coalescing its reference would make every unparseable webhook collide and be silently dropped, losing audit rows; the money path is guarded by `WHERE status = 'pending'` instead). **Read these out of a loaded database, never from the SQL: `information_schema` calls a generated column nullable because that is DECLARED rather than worked out, so `siteScope` looks like a fault and is not.** The query, the full fifteen-key table and the one question to ask of each are in DEV_NOTES.md, "A unique key is powerless over a column that can be empty".
- **Scripts written into a page (#625).** Every inline script carries the answer's own nonce from `App::cspNonce()`; a script or an element handler without it is REFUSED by the browser and nothing is logged, so the page looks finished and the control does nothing. Never add an element handler (`onclick`, `onchange`): use `data-portal-submit-on-change` / `data-portal-select-on-click`, read by `web/public_html/assets/js/portal-actions.js`, or a listener carrying the nonce. The tripwire is `tools/audit-checks/check_inline_scripts_have_nonce.py` (its exemption list is explained file by file, and its header names the three shapes it cannot see); the page test is `tools/page-scripts-selftest.php`. Never loosen the rule itself to make a script run. **A page that writes more than once without reloading must be handed the replacement anti-forgery token and must keep it**, because `Auth::verifyCsrf()` replaces it on every acceptance — the house way is `ApiAuth::requireWrite()` with `ApiResponse`, whose `meta.csrfToken` exists for this; return it on the success AND on every refusal reached after the rotation. Such a page sends ONE request at a time (two in flight can never both succeed), arms its deadline across the BODY read and not just the wait for headers, never lets its own polling pile up, and SAYS when a command fails — and says only what is known, never "not sent" when the answer was merely lost.
- **The activity log and sessions (#622).** `Logger::sessionFingerprint()` is the ONE thing stored in place of a session identifier and `Logger::sessionFacts()` the ONE copy of a session a record keeps (six named facts; add one there on purpose, never "everything except a list"). Never store `session_id()` or the whole `$_SESSION`: `tools/audit-checks/check_session_id_not_stored.py` fails on it. The fingerprint is plain SHA-256 because the clean-up must make the same value in SQL, and the monthly sign-ins report joins on that column. Records written before #622 are cleaned by `Portal\Core\ActivityLogCleaner`, in pieces, from the Upgrade page; it is NOT part of the upgrade gate and never closes the portal. Migration 220 deletes old backup codes once, and its "already done" mark is its own row in `tblMigrations`, which the install script writes only where no backup code exists. **A one-table restore of `tblMigrations`, `tblTotpBackupCodes` or `tblSettings` moves that mark and can lose data — a whole-database restore is safe, and ANY migration whose "once" test is its own row has the same exposure.** Test: `tools/activity-log-selftest.php`, which reads the records back from the database rather than believing the cleaner, and refuses to search the log for an empty text.
- **The rows a restore cannot put back are KEPT now, and two of my own mistakes here are the lesson (#694, migration 233).** A snapshot from before 27 October 2026 does not say it covers the whole database, so `restoreAll()` holds `tblSettings` and `tblTotpBackupCodes` back — while still replacing `tblSites` and `tblUsers`, their `ON DELETE CASCADE` parents. Every per-organisation settings row went — EVERY row with a `siteID`, not just the address ones #677 added — and every sign-in code, spent ones included, and the restore said success. They are now read BEFORE anything is emptied and written to `tblRestoreHeldRows` AFTER every table is back — **that order is the design**, because the holding table may itself be in the snapshot, and both halves sit inside the restore's own transaction so a rolled-back restore keeps nothing. `DbBackup::CASCADE_RESCUE` is the ONE allow-list of tables ever kept (exactly two), and it is what makes the table name safe to put in a statement; `CASCADE_RESCUE_MAX_ROWS` is 20,000, past which **NOTHING** is kept and the count is said out loud, because keeping some would read as keeping all — **that path is exercised by no test and proof 27's header says so.** The rows wait until an administrator clears them, with **no timer** (the owner, 31 October 2026 — D-029): a clock that deleted them mid-recovery would be this same silent loss one step on, so the Backup page's card is the only thing stopping them being forgotten. **`EXTRA LIKE '%GENERATED%'` IS NOT HOW YOU FIND A GENERATED COLUMN** — MySQL puts `DEFAULT_GENERATED` there for any `DEFAULT CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` column, so my first `putHeldRowsBack()` dropped `updatedAt` and let the database re-stamp it: the row went back with the right key, the right value and the wrong "last changed" date. `columnFacts()` already reads `GENERATION_EXPRESSION` and its comment already warned against exactly that test; **read the class before adding a helper to it.** Only `brs_checkSame()`, which compares every value of every table, caught it — every row-exists check passed. Second mistake: the "put back" mark was written `if ($put > 0)`, marking EVERY row as returned the moment ONE succeeded, so a row that could not go back would have stopped being offered — the one thing this feature exists to prevent. **The new warning is an ADDITION, not a replacement**, and a draft of the code comment claimed otherwise: the older "N table(s) that are NOT in this snapshot were changed as well" still runs and covers every table, so proof 6 pins BOTH sentences. **A one-table restore cannot cause any of this** — `restoreTable()` refuses a table another points at — and I said on the issue that it could before checking. A row that cannot go back has more than one cause (its parent is missing, OR it is already there), which cannot be told apart from the insert's failure, so the page says "the likeliest reason is…" and never asserts one. The holding table does not exist until migration 233 has run and files reach a server before anybody presses "Run migrations", so every reader asks `holdingTableExists()` first (`SHOW TABLES LIKE`, never a blanket try/catch) and the restore then proceeds with an honest warning — refusing it would block a recovery at the one moment somebody is recovering. A kept code row is a second copy of authentication material, so `GdprEraser` DELETES this table by `userID` (whole rows: a kept row with its person removed could not be put back anywhere) and it must never join the pinned "left behind on purpose" list. Proofs 6 and 17 cause the cascade, so they call `brs_clearHeldRows()` and **CHECK its count was not nothing** — a silent sweep would pass the day the rescue stopped working. Tests: proof 27 of `tools/backup-restore-selftest.php`, section P of `tools/backup-page-selftest.php`, section 18 of `tools/gdpr-eraser-selftest.php`. **The independent check found five real problems in this, and three are shapes to recognise.** (a) `CASCADE_RESCUE['tblSettings']['describe']` said "its postal address, map position, what3words address and support e-mail" — UNTRUE: the rescue takes EVERY row with a `siteID`, whatever its key (`attend.detailRetentionDays`, `giving.retention.years`, the four `mail.queue.*` numbers), and **SEVEN** files under `web/` can write such a row. The sentence is general now and `rowsWaitingToBePutBack()` returns a `names` field — the settings KEY, lifted out by `nameInsideHeldRow()`, which NEVER returns the JSON, because a key says what a row IS and `settingValue` can be anything. (b) **A row put back kept its whole copy FOR EVER**: nothing deletes a marked row, so a code hash and a stored setting value would travel into every later snapshot and off-site copy — exactly the unbounded second copy of authentication material the owner was warned about. `rowJson` is now emptied IN THE SAME STATEMENT that sets `putBackAtUtc`, and the column is nullable for that; the test counts `COUNT(rowJson)` against `COUNT(*)` AND checks the other columns survive, because emptying the whole row would destroy the audit trail instead. (c) **The rescue asked "is this table HELD BACK" when the question is "is it being PUT BACK"** — `$heldBack` is built only from names the MANIFEST holds, so a snapshot that simply did not CONTAIN `tblSettings` (imported, or hand-edited) restored `tblSites`, let the cascade wipe every per-organisation row, rescued nothing and printed no #694 warning. NO EXISTING CHECK COULD CATCH IT, because every fixture was a genuine old-form snapshot, which holds all three tables — the I7d lesson (#645) mirrored. Section F of proof 27 is decisive because its snapshot still SAYS it covers the whole database. Also: the waiting-rows read takes `FOR UPDATE` (#647's rule — without it two administrators are told "1 could not be put back" about a row that is back); `discardHeldRows()` answers **-1**, not 0, for a table it keeps no rows for, because one answer for two facts made the page say "0 kept row(s) for <anything posted> discarded" and log that name; `DbBackup::keepsRowsFor()` is the ONE public test of the allow-list. **Two comments invented an e-mail column on `tblTotpBackupCodes` and two privacy clauses could never fail** — a clause that cannot fail makes a check LOOK stronger than it is. **#697 is FIXED (31 October 2026): a restore CARRIES the waiting rows through** — read before anything is emptied, written again after every table is back, the same order as the rescue and for the same reason. It used to replace them and merely warn, which asks somebody mid-recovery to act on a sentence. **A SECOND round found five more, and the two worst were mine twice over.** **I wrote "32 files" and it is SEVEN** — 32 was not even the count of the thing I cited (`grep -rln "INSERT INTO tblSettings" web/_apps web/_core | wc -l` gives 30, and counts files writing ANY settings row, nearly all binding `siteID` as literal `NULL`).
