This very file already said "seven per-organisation writers" from #668 and I contradicted it. **Establish it by reading each INSERT's column list against its VALUES, never by counting files that mention the table.** Same shape as the fault round one existed to repair, two rounds running. **And the `FOR UPDATE` TOOK NO LOCK**: it was on a SELECT running BEFORE `begin_transaction()`, and nothing in `web/_core/` touches autocommit, so the implicit transaction around it committed and released every lock at once — while an eleven-line comment and both instruction files claimed it stopped two administrators reading the same rows. **A lock outside a transaction is decoration.** The transaction starts first now. **"Because this snapshot does not cover tblSettings" is FALSE in the commonest case** and the restore contradicted itself in ONE answer: the held-back list is built from the MANIFEST's own list, so a table must be IN the snapshot to be held back, and the same answer already said "tblSettings … were left exactly as they are". An administrator mid-recovery was told their settings were not in their backup. The warning and the card now say the RESTORE could not put it back, which covers held-back and simply-absent alike; two new checks pin it, one of them checking the contradicting sentence is still present. **`put_held_back` still wrote a posted table name into the activity log** under an event type saying a put-back failed — the discard branch had just been fixed for that and the put-back branch had not; both route an unknown name to `BackupActionRefused` with no name. **Section 18 of the eraser test gave an untrue reason 25 lines below its own correction** ("still holds their sign-in codes" — a marked row has `rowJson` emptied, and never held an e-mail address); the real reason it must go is that it still NAMES THE PERSON, and its fixture now nulls `rowJson` as the portal does, because **a fixture in a state production can never reach is testing nothing**. Also: "UNUSED" two-factor codes was wrong in five places — the cascade takes every row with a `userID` and `verify.php` KEEPS a spent code with `isUsed = 1`, so the record of which codes were spent goes too, which is what `WHOLE_DATABASE_ONLY` exists to protect. **A THIRD round found three more, and two were failures to APPLY a correction I had already written down.** Round 2 recorded that "unused" two-factor codes was wrong in five places and that "address rows" understated the loss; round 3 found both wordings still live in the in-app help page, migration 233's own header, section 18 of the eraser test, `CHANGELOG.md`, `.claude/HANDOFF.md` and — on the very line recording the correction — this file and its mirror. **A document asserting a fix while stating the thing it fixed is worse than one that says nothing**, because the next reader stops looking. The help page mattered most: it is the one sentence a customer reads BEFORE deciding whether to accept the loss, and it understated it. **And making the lock real made a new path reachable that the broken version never could:** `prepare`/`bind_param`/`execute`/`get_result` sat OUTSIDE the `try`, and under `MYSQLI_REPORT_STRICT | MYSQLI_REPORT_ERROR` all four throw — **it takes BOTH** — so with the lock genuinely held, a second administrator now BLOCKS, `TimeLimit::atLeast(900)` means PHP will not cut it off first, and at `innodb_lock_wait_timeout` (50s) `execute()` throws ERROR 1205 and escapes with the transaction OPEN. That gave a bare 500 instead of the honest message — and `Logger::db()` returns the SAME connection, so the `tblErrors` row was written inside the open transaction and rolled back on disconnect: **the fault left no record anywhere.** The read is inside the `try` now with a `finally` that frees and closes on every path. **A check made only of ABSENCES passes when the thing stops happening at all** — section P asserted the posted name was NOT logged and the event type was NOT a failed put-back, and deleting the `$record()` call left it green under a name claiming the refusal IS recorded; the positive clause comes first now. **A FIFTH round found three more. IT TAKES BOTH `MYSQLI_REPORT_STRICT` AND `MYSQLI_REPORT_ERROR`, AND I GOT THIS WRONG TWICE.** Measured on the STATEMENT path (`prepare()` on a missing table): `ERROR` alone returns false and warns, `STRICT` alone returns false SILENTLY, only both together throw. My first "correction" said `STRICT` does it — because I had measured a CONNECT error, which is reported whatever `ERROR` says, so `STRICT` alone really does throw there. **Measuring the wrong code path and then writing "measured, not reasoned" beside the conclusion is worse than reasoning, because nobody has cause to doubt it.** I had corrected the two code comments a round earlier and left four documents naming the wrong flag: the fifth time in this package a correction was recorded and applied in one place. Also a pronoun first defined later in its own sentence, and a REFUSED discard logged as `BackupHeldRowsDiscarded`, the same type as a successful one, so a log search by type counted a run where nothing was discarded (now `BackupHeldRowsDiscardFailed`, also added to the page test's `BPS_OWN_LINES`). **THEN THE DESIGN CHANGED, and this is the part worth keeping:** the warning sentence had reached ninety lines and nine nested ternaries, and **four of the six wording faults rounds 2-5 found were inside it**, every one a ternary in the middle of a clause, and NOT ONE findable by any check — nothing judges how an assembled sentence reads, and a test pinning a substring will pin a substring of nonsense. `DbBackup::cascadeWarning()` now holds **EIGHT COMPLETE SENTENCES** keyed by (ahead/done, kept/lost, one/many). **When a sentence is built from branches, spell out the whole sentence for each branch**, and PRINT the outputs by calling the real code rather than transcribing them. **A SIXTH round found four more, and the most useful lesson in the package: I MEASURED THE WRONG CODE PATH.** My round-5 "correction" said `MYSQLI_REPORT_STRICT` makes mysqli throw, with "measured, not reasoned" beside it — but I had measured a CONNECT error, and the sentence is about four STATEMENT calls. Measured properly (`prepare()` on a missing table): `ERROR` alone returns false and warns, `STRICT` alone returns false SILENTLY, only BOTH throw. `ERROR` makes a statement error get reported; `STRICT` turns a reported error into an exception; a CONNECT error is reported whatever `ERROR` says, which is why `STRICT` alone throws there. **"Measured, not reasoned" is only worth writing if the measurement is of the thing the sentence is about** — written against a conclusion from a neighbouring path it is WORSE than reasoning, because it removes the reader's reason to doubt. `bootstrap.php`'s own comment carried the same untruth and is corrected (one line outside this package's file list, declared not buried). **A hedge that covers one condition and ASSERTS the other is not a hedge:** the "what your backup may still hold" sentence was wrong BOTH ways round — first hedging table presence and ignoring row currency, then hedging currency and asserting presence, which is false for the not-in-the-snapshot case and false in the REASSURING direction. All three places state both conditions now, and the CAUSE is "most likely" because a hand-dropped table looks the same. **A FOURTH clause that cannot fail** (`discardHeldRows('tblSettings') >= 0`, when the method ends `return max(0, $gone)`) — the same shape I deleted forty lines below it in the same edit. **And HALF the eight sentences were exercised by NOTHING:** the whole `canKeep === false` branch, which proof 27's own "what this cannot show" list had not mentioned. Section G reaches all four by renaming the holding table outside `tbl%` AND taking the snapshot afterwards — an un-upgraded database has no holding table and no snapshot containing one — and `brs_tidy()` puts it back if that section is killed mid-rename. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "The rows a restore cannot put back (#694)".
- **The waiting rows survive a restore now (#697, 31 October 2026).** `tblRestoreHeldRows` was an ordinary table, so a whole restore emptied it and filled it from the snapshot — and a snapshot taken BEFORE some rows were kept holds none of them, so the waiting rows went and the only copy of them outside that snapshot went with them. `readWaitingHeldRows()` and `carryWaitingHeldRowsThrough()` are the ONE place a waiting row survives a restore; do not write a second one. **`WHOLE_DATABASE_ONLY` now has FOUR names** — the holding table joined it, which refuses a ONE-table restore of it (that really would throw the rows away, with no carry to save them). **I ALSO claimed a "second effect", that an OLD-FORM snapshot now leaves the table alone, and it is a NO-OP for every snapshot a customer can have** — the independent check restored a genuine one and got THREE tables held back, not four: a snapshot with no `whole_database` flag predates that flag (`b7496b7`, 27 October) and the holding table arrived with migration 233 (`ac1b19e`, 31 October), so it cannot contain it. **The claim survived because `brs_legacySnapshot()` read every live `tbl%` table and so built a snapshot production cannot produce** — the #645 lesson again — and it cost a TRUE sentence in the customer-facing help page ("leaves three tables alone") being turned into a false one, in the one place a customer reads before accepting a loss. The addition changes a WHOLE restore only for a manifest somebody has edited the flag out of (section G of proof 28); an old-form snapshot is safe for the simpler reason that the table is not in it, as is any snapshot that does not contain it. **TWO SAFETY NETS WERE INERT ON A SERVER WITH `lower_case_table_names = 1`, AND ONE WAS #694's.** `SHOW TABLES` reports lower-case names there, and both this carry and #694's cascade rescue compared them with `in_array(..., true)` against a name typed in the source — so neither ran and a waiting row was lost with the restore reporting SUCCESS and NO warning. Measured on `mysql:8.0.36 --lower-case-table-names=1`. **AND FIXING THE FIVE `in_array` SITES WAS NOT ENOUGH — I WROTE "all five sites use it" IN FOUR DOCUMENTS AND IT WAS FALSE.** An ARRAY SUBSCRIPT is the same comparison written another way: `$facts['tblRestoreHeldRows']` and `REMOVED_COLUMNS[$table]` went on matching exactly, and `$restored` was a FOURTH server-reported list holding two more (`tblMailQueue`, `tblSmsMessage`). Making the carry RUN without fixing the subscript turned the silent loss into a BLOCKED RECOVERY: the carry threw "the shape could not be read", so every whole-database restore AND rehearsal was refused while any row was waiting, naming the wrong cause — and the only way to reach it was #694's rescue succeeding. `namedIn()` compares names; `forTable()` is for arrays KEYED by table name; `isWholeDatabaseOnly()` had always used `strcasecmp()` for this reason, so the two halves of one method had disagreed about what a table IS. The two mail/text faults were PRE-EXISTING and cost double sends (`holdRestoredMail()`'s own docblock: "three waiting messages came out as six copies"). **Every self-test here runs ONE server set to 0, so NOTHING here can catch this coming back** — the #646 shape — which is why `tools/audit-checks/check_table_name_comparisons.py` is a hard gate in `pr-security.yml`. **AND THE CEILING USED TO LOSE EVERYTHING IT WAS MEANT TO PROTECT:** past `CASCADE_RESCUE_MAX_ROWS` the first version carried NOTHING and let the snapshot's older copy replace them — 20,001 rows in, ZERO out, measured — while the card and the help page both promised that could not happen. The ceiling is now decided ONCE, in `restoreAllWork()` BEFORE the transaction, and past it the holding table is not put back AT ALL: nothing is lost and the answer says so. Two places deciding one ceiling is #668's lesson. **And never promise anything about EVERY waiting row:** one branch legitimately ends with a row no longer waiting (the snapshot records it as already put back, and the restore honours that history), so the sentence speaks only of the rows it wrote again. **Identity is `tableName`, `siteID`, `userID` and `heldAtUtc`, NEVER `rowJson`**, and both reasons are load-bearing: a row already put back has had `rowJson` emptied, so comparing it would call that a different row and re-offer something the restored database says is finished; and MySQL REWRITES a JSON column on storage (keys reordered, a space after each colon) while MariaDB's `JSON` is an alias for `LONGTEXT` and rewrites nothing (measured on both), so a text compare means two different things on the two engines — the same trap as the `rowJson LIKE` clause that could never fail in #694's eraser test. **THREE OF THOSE FOUR COLUMNS WERE GUARDED BY NOTHING, and this is the most useful thing here:** narrowing the comparison to `tableName` alone left ALL THIRTY of proof 28's checks green while a real waiting row was SILENTLY LOST — success reported, no carry warning, row gone — because the only fixture reaching the clash branch made its two rows differ in `tableName`, the one column a narrowing keeps. **A fixture that differs in several ways at once tests only the first thing the code looks at.** Section E now has one sub-case per column. A row ALREADY PUT BACK is deliberately NOT carried (it is history, and a restore puts history back); what goes with it is the audit trail of the return, which is a cost, not nothing. A carried row keeps its own number where that number is free and takes a new one where a DIFFERENT row holds it. **The REHEARSAL carries too**, on purpose: it writes inside the transaction and rolls back (`holdRestoredMail()` already did), so one that skipped the carry could not tell you the carry would fail — but a rolled-back INSERT still consumes an AUTO_INCREMENT number, so say "no value survives it", never "it changes nothing". **And proof 26's refusal loop could not notice a deleted name** — it iterates the constant, so removing `tblRestoreHeldRows` just stops testing it; the explicit `array_keys($list) === […]` pin is what catches that, proved by planting. Three wording faults worth recognising, each a repeat: "All N" of a number counting only a SUBSET (the rows written again, not the rows waiting); a hedge covering one condition and ASSERTING the other, for the third time in two packages; and a clause that CANNOT FAIL in section P, forbidding a phrase that was never on that page — in the package that wrote that lesson down. Nothing exercises the over-20,000-rows path or the could-not-read path. **AND THE CLASS TOOK FOUR MORE ROUNDS TO CLOSE, which is the real lesson.** Round 3 found a SUBSCRIPT (`$facts['tblRestoreHeldRows']`) still exact, so making the carry RUN had turned the silent loss into a BLOCKED RECOVERY — every restore refused while a row waited. Round 4 found a THIRD site (`columnFacts()[$table]` in `putHeldRowsBack()`), where the Backup page offered a kept row and **only the button that throws it away worked.** Round 5 found that the RULE I had written to close the class was wrong BOTH ways. **The true rule: a table-keyed lookup is safe when BOTH SIDES COME FROM THE SAME PLACE** — keys typed here indexed by a name typed here is safe (`CASCADE_RESCUE[$table]`, where capitals matter by design); keys from `information_schema` indexed by a name from `SHOW TABLES` is safe on one server (#698 is the cross-server residue); one of each is the fault. My wrong version — "the fault is an index that came from the SOURCE" — CLEARED `REMOVED_COLUMNS[$table]`, one of the very faults it was generalising, and CONDEMNED three correct sites. **A rule written from one fault's symptoms describes that fault, not the class: test both halves against the other known sites before writing it down.** `forTable()` is the helper for arrays keyed by table name, `namedIn()` for lists of names, and neither is "the ONE way" — that phrasing, written while correcting "all five sites use it", is the same mistake again. The gate was bypassed FIVE times across versions 1-4 (the word rather than a call; `strcmp` in the unpinned helper; a two-argument `in_array`, which is no safer; a one-hop local; and a real `strcasecmp` call with `&& $table === $name` after it), so version 5 pins BOTH helper bodies WHOLE — the literal form of "pin the WHOLE condition" — and any edit to them fails it on purpose. Tests: proof 28 of `tools/backup-restore-selftest.php` (61 checks), proof 26, section P of `tools/backup-page-selftest.php`, and the hard gate `tools/audit-checks/check_table_name_comparisons.py`; thirty-one plants over five rounds, each caught by the one check that should catch it. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "Carrying the kept rows through a restore (#697)".
- **Who may be sent a text, and a one-table restore (#610, #622).** `Sms::whyNotThisPerson()` is the ONE place that decides whether a person may be sent a text, asked at BOTH moments — by `send()` for a text going now (AFTER the decision to hold, never before) and by `sendHeld()` when a held text comes due. It answers a REASON, and `heldPersonWords()` / `atOncePersonWords()` put that reason into the right words; never record the reason itself. JUDGE A PERSON AT THE MOMENT THEIR TEXT WOULD GO: asking before the hold was built first and silently refused a text for anybody suspended and reinstated inside two days. A refused text carries no "sent at", and `record()` is the ONE place that keeps the row and drops the NAME when the database refuses a person who has gone. Test: `tools/held-texts-selftest.php`. **`DbBackup::WHOLE_DATABASE_ONLY` is the ONE list of tables a restore of ONE table refuses** (`tblMigrations`, `tblTotpBackupCodes`, `tblSettings`, and `tblRestoreHeldRows` since #697), compared only by `isWholeDatabaseOnly()` and worded only by `wholeDatabaseOnlyReason()`, ignoring capitals because `lower_case_table_names` is a server setting; the Backup page asks both and keeps no copy. A WHOLE-database restore is untouched and is the supported route. Before adding a table, ask whether the rest of the database would still make sense beside it. Tests: `tools/backup-restore-selftest.php`, `tools/backup-page-selftest.php`.
- **A check can name the right file and still guard nothing (31 October 2026).** `tools/gdpr-coverage-selftest.php` pins which pages may file an erasure request as ALREADY CONFIRMED — `_apps/account/erasure-request.php` (not confirmed), `_apps/auth/account/delete-confirm.php` and `_apps/offboarding/do.php` (both confirmed). **Callers are named by their PATH under `web/`, never by `basename()`:** moving the call into a new, completely ungated `zzplant/do.php` was indistinguishable from the gated `offboarding/do.php`, and `do.php` is a generic name. **Every gate must REFUSE, not merely exist:** matching `if (App::isAdmin() === false) {` and nothing else passed with the `exit` inside the braces deleted — #668's section-D lesson again — so the walk requires an `exit` before the next `}`, and the three gates (administrator, form token, `AccountGuard`) must come IN ORDER, which a comment had claimed while nothing checked it. The source is MASKED, not just stripped of comments: every string literal becomes a run of `x` of the same length, because a `}` or the word `exit` inside a message would answer the walk falsely either way. Cut the relative path with `strlen($webBase)`, never `strlen(realpath($webBase))` — the iterator returns the path as given (`tools/../web/…`) and realpath is shorter. Seven plants, all caught.
- **The password-reset page is compared with itself, and the nonce is noise (31 October 2026).** `landing()` in `tools/mail-pages-selftest.php` and `tools/sign-in-limits-selftest.php` proves a stranger cannot tell which addresses have accounts. Both tests sat RED because #625's asset-fallback script put `nonce="…"` on the page and `App::cspNonce()` is `bin2hex(random_bytes(16))` — **32 hex characters, where the helper stripped 64.** The portal was right; the test was comparing noise. **Strip the nonce BY NAME; never widen the pattern to 32 hex**, which would blind the comparison to any real difference of that length. And a clause reading "the link is not used up" could never fail: it looked the row up by `hash('sha256', $link)` while the portal stores `hash('sha256', 'reset-link-utc:' . $token)` via `Auth::resetLinkFingerprint()`, so `null['usedAt']` was null and it passed on a row that did not exist. Ask the portal's own function; never keep a second copy of the rule in a test.
- **Two self-tests ran in NO workflow until 31 October 2026** — `tools/setting-limit-selftest.php` (#668) and `tools/offsite-settings-selftest.php` (#671), the #646 shape again. Both are now in `portal-selftests.yml`, **which SAYS it runs on every pull request with no path filter and has never run on GitHub at all (#754) — the wiring is right and is not yet a live gate**; `backup-selftest.yml` is path-filtered and does not list the off-site files, so a change to them would have run nothing. The off-site test REFUSES without `bash`, `openssl`, `rclone` or `tar`, and GitHub's image has no `rclone`, so the job installs it. **Nothing runs the whole set locally — that is #695**, and running it by hand is how all three of 31 October's red tests were found.
- **Erasure and the activity log (#626).** Every activity record must NAME ITS PERSON in `userID`:
  `Logger::activity()` fills it from `sessionFacts()['userID']` when the caller passes none (never
  `waitingForCodeUserID`, and never `kioskUserID` — which is a real member's account, not the machine's, and is left
  out because a kiosk session outlives the person at it; a kiosk record therefore still names nobody). It never
  overrides an explicit number. That is what makes the ONE
  hand-written instruction in `GdprEraser::catalogue()` enough — an erasure matches one column. **If you are ever
  tempted to add a second way of finding a person's activity records, the fault is that something wrote a record
  without naming them: fix that instead.** Matching on the session fingerprint was rejected because a shared
  machine means one fingerprint is not proof of one person. Older records are brought into line by
  `ActivityLogCleaner::linkAccounts()`, which MUST stay inside the existing walk and BEFORE its blanking step (the
  old whole-session copy is the only place such a record names anybody), and which CLEARS rather than links a
  record whose account has been erased — linking it would undo that erasure. Migration 221 resets
  `activityLog.cleanedThrough` to send the cleaner back through the log. The pinned list `$leftBehindOnPurpose` in
  `tools/gdpr-coverage-selftest.php` is **EMPTY and pinned empty** since #635 (2 November 2026) — it used to name
  35 tables and this line used to say so, which is now the stale shape #678 warns about. The decision for every
  one of those tables moved into `web/_core/personal-data-catalogue.php`, beside the column, as `notEmptiedHere`.
  Nothing may be added to the pinned list. Tests: `tools/activity-log-selftest.php`, section 17 of `tools/gdpr-eraser-selftest.php`,
  `tools/gdpr-coverage-selftest.php`, phases 1 and 7 of the migration harness.
- **Every table has a catalogue decision now, and the check that guards it asks about a column's SHAPE (#706).** `web/_core/personal-data-catalogue.php` had SIXTY-SIX tables missing entirely and THIRTY-SEVEN columns holding a person's details named in no entry. **The hard gate that exists to catch exactly this was GREEN over all thirty-seven**, because `check_personal_data_coverage.py` decided what a personal column is from a hand-written list of LITERAL names — it knew `phone` but not `phoneNumber`, `address` but not `addressLine1`, `visitorIP` but not `createdIP`, and its own comments record ELEVEN occasions on which somebody had to extend it. **It was somebody's memory wearing a schema-driven check's clothes — the #645 shape one level up.** It now ALSO tests the SHAPE of the name: whole words split out of it, a contact word, or `name` where the qualifier says WHOSE. **The literal list STAYS** (no shape catches `actorUserID`), and **the judgement stays in the catalogue** — the check only insists somebody wrote one. **Keep it NARROW:** a first draft flagged 283 columns across 150 tables because "contains the word name" matches `categoryName`, `deptName` and `tuneName`; a check that cries wolf gets switched off (#483). Free-text columns are an INVENTORY, not a gate (the owner, 2 November 2026 — D-030), so **a green run means every contact-shaped column is named, NOT that every personal one is.** Three rules the catalogue enforces and I had to learn: a `notEmptiedHere` key must ALSO be in `columns` (#635); an entry may not promise an action with no column to act on, so `tblExpenseClaimItems` and `tblReadingPlanProgress` are `retain` with the truth in the reason (#535); and **`tblErasureAudit` must NOT be in the catalogue at all** (#510, rule 7d — a `retain` entry is still an instruction), so a comment marks that deliberate gap because an absence looks exactly like the #706 fault. **None of the sixty-six carried a `userID`** — they are lookups, settings and child tables keyed by a parent, so they were unreachable BY CONSTRUCTION, which is why nobody wrote them down; #709's parent matching reaches some, but **NOT `tblExpenseClaimFiles`**, whose parent is deliberately kept for HMRC, so there is no deletion to cascade. **Writing an honest reason is what found the faults:** #712, #713 and #714 all came out of refusing to write a comfortable sentence. Tests: `tools/gdpr-coverage-selftest.php` and `check_personal_data_coverage.py` (a hard gate). **ROUND 1 OF THE CHECK FOUND THE GUARD ITSELF BROKEN, and it is the best lesson here: a BRACKET-COUNTING parser is broken by a COMMENT, and the comment that broke mine was a note about this very parser.** One unbalanced `[` inside `tblAnonymousCheckins`' comment made that entry's "block" run 144,494 of the file's 153,340 characters, absorbing every other entry's `notEmptiedHere` keys — so a contact-shaped column on that table was INVISIBLE if its name appeared anywhere else. Live on the day it was written. **Stripping comments is the WRONG fix** (a reason may contain `//`, as any URL does): delimit each entry from its own header to the NEXT header and parse no structure at all. Same cause, second fault: the decision regex wanted `'decision'` immediately after `[`, so it MISSED the two entries carrying a comment there — 226 seen where PHP sees 228, and the success line printed the wrong number as coverage. Four more: **"nothing erased them" conflated two faults** (about a dozen of the 37 were ALREADY erased and merely undocumented — `git show HEAD:` proves it — while the rest are genuinely unreached); **naming a column does NOT add it to the data copy** (`inventory()` reports table, count, action and reason, never a value); **a `period` must be checked against the purging code** (`tblAssetFoundReports` IS cleared at 180 days by the nightly asset job, and that method's docblock records the same untruth being deleted once before); and **a reason may not cite the issue being closed** (four said "Tracked as #706" and now say #715). Also: listing a column under an `erase` table READS as erased — `externalName` means "not a portal user", so that row has no account number and the delete never reaches it.
- **A RESERVED WORD USED AS AN IDENTIFIER MUST BE BACKTICKED OR QUALIFIED (#718, #276).** MySQL ignores case in keywords, so **`fullText` IS `FULLTEXT`** — a bare `fullText = ?` does not parse, `php -l` cannot see it, and that is how the statement that SAVES a transcript shipped unable to run, throwing AFTER the outside provider had been called and charged. Until #718 no audit script could see it either, exactly as the #276 note said. `tools/audit-checks/check_reserved_words_quoted.py` is now a hard gate: every reserved identifier in `full_schema.sql` must be in `RESERVED_ON_PURPOSE` with a reason (fails BOTH ways), and every reference to a listed one must be backticked or qualified by an alias — **both work, because after a dot a reserved word is allowed**. **EXACTLY ONE of 229 tables needs it: `tblTranscript.fullText`.** **THE ALIAS RULE WAS WRITTEN AND DELETED ON PURPOSE** — `AS <word>` cannot be told from English ("as long as", "as not", "as if") and `CAST(x AS BINARY)` is valid SQL; it produced fifteen reports and not one was real (#483). **And "does this string look like SQL" had to be strict:** asking for select/set/where/from matched ordinary prose in the help pages, so it now requires a `tbl[A-Z]` table name or a leading SQL verb. What it cannot see: a continuation fragment like `. 'fullText = ? '` alone — in the real fault the bare reference sat in the same string as `UPDATE tblTranscript SET`. **And the list is CHECKED AGAINST A REAL SERVER now (#719).** It has to be hard-coded, because an audit script must run with no database — that is what lets the pre-push hook run every one of them in about nine seconds — and a hard-coded list goes stale SILENTLY. `tools/reserved-words-live-selftest.php` reads the `RESERVED` set OUT OF the Python (never a second copy) and compares it with what the server says: on MySQL, `INFORMATION_SCHEMA.KEYWORDS WHERE RESERVED = 1`; on MariaDB, which has `WORD` and no `RESERVED` flag, by TRYING each candidate as a column name, because membership does not mean reserved. **Only "the server reserves a word we LACK" fails** — the other direction means the list is conservative, which is safe and happens in both directions as versions move, and failing on it would make the test impossible to run on two MySQL versions. It earned its place on the first run: 8.0.36 reserves `master_bind` and `master_ssl_verify_server_cert`, which the list lacked, and **MariaDB 11.4 reserves seventeen more including `offset` and `returning`** — both thoroughly plausible column names. The list is now the UNION of all FIVE engines the matrices run on (MySQL 8.0, 8.4 and 9.7; MariaDB 11.4 and 12.3). **It went STALE for the two newest the moment #475 part 1 added them to the harness and nothing was pointed at either** — measured 4 November 2026, the committed list FAILED on both: MySQL 9.7.4 reserves `external` and `library`, MariaDB 12.3.3 reserves `conversion`, `current_path`, `to_date` and `vector`, and none was in it. None is a column name here, so nothing broke — luck, not coverage. `vector` is the one to notice, since both families are adding vector types. The fix is two changes together: the six words, AND `calendar-selftests.yml`'s matrix taken to those same five engines, because adding a word without pointing the test at the server that reserves it leaves the next version just as blind. **A warning from doing it: a probe loop reported `phase` and `recover` as reserved on MariaDB too, and tried ONE AT A TIME both are ACCEPTED — the generated table names collided, and a collision looks exactly like a refusal. Probe one word at a time and read the error, never the exit code** (#483: a check that cries wolf gets switched off). `DbServer::TESTED_LINES` is NOT touched and must not be — it describes the MIGRATION HARNESS alone. Wired into `portal-selftests.yml` (8.0.36 and 8.4.11) and `calendar-selftests.yml` (8.0.36, 8.4, 9.7, MariaDB 11.4 and 12.3). **What it cannot see, measured rather than assumed: a MariaDB reserved word containing a DIGIT.** The probe tries only `^[a-z_]+$`, so `float4`, `float8` and `int1`-`int8` are never tried there — all seven are already in the list and no column here is named any of them, so it costs nothing today, but a NEW digit-bearing word MariaDB reserved would slip through, and that is the one direction this test fails on.
- **A person's own words about themselves are emptied on an erasure request (#715, migration 240; the owner, 3 November 2026 — D-016).** A prayer request's `subject`, `body` and `partnerNote`, and `tblCareCase.summary` — which is a third party's description of the erased person's circumstances and sat beside visit notes #709 already empties. The rows stay. **Migration 240 makes three of those columns nullable, and the hard gate `check_erasure_columns_nullable.py` is what found it: a NOT NULL column CANNOT be emptied and the failure is QUIET** — the erasure records a line saying so, carries on, and the request can still close as completed with the person's words in place. **KEPT on purpose, because the words are about the organisation:** `presenterText` (twice), `deviceName`, `seatLabel`, `meetingNotes`, and the `tblErrors` columns, which expire on that table's own timer — with the honest condition stated, that the sweep is a scheduled job a customer must set up. **NOT reached, each recorded rather than forced:** a salvation card's `prayerRequest` (nothing on the card says whose it is) and a room booking's text, which `GdprEraser`'s own guard refuses because `bookedByID` is in `ACTOR_COLUMNS` — **#723 IS DONE (3 November 2026): `bookedByID` has LEFT `ACTOR_COLUMNS`** on the owner's decision that a booking is made BY the person it is about, so `purpose` is emptied. **BE EXACT: `purpose` AND NOTHING ELSE.** `tblResourceBooking.notes` is ALSO the booker's own words — `_apps/resources/book.php` is its only writer and reads `$_POST['notes']` from the booker's own form on the same row — and the catalogue claimed it was "the organisation's own note … rather than anything the booker wrote", which was FALSE; the owner was told that and chose to KEEP it, so the reason is now that decision. And `declineReason` is written by NOTHING (the decline branch sets only status, approver and approved-at), so its place in `nullCols` can never match: the limit is recorded in the INSTRUCTION's reason and not in `notEmptiedHere`, because the #635 check fails a column explained as not-emptied while an instruction empties it — the two mechanisms cannot both describe one column. #708's precedent. **`approvedByID` STAYS an actor column, and that pair is the whole point of the two lists** — emptying text on an approver's number is a blanket `= NULL` over every booking they approved. The guard was NOT weakened; the classification was the fault. **The eraser test now holds a booking somebody ELSE made and this person only APPROVED, and without it the reclassification could have been widened to both columns with every check still passing** (planted). No migration: both columns were already nullable. A case-by-case review role is #724, built next. **Two lessons from the independent check.** `prayer-requests/index.php` printed `$req['subject']` with NO cast, twice, and the congregation feed is not filtered by submitter — so the page would really have thrown `htmlspecialchars(): Argument #1 must be of type string, null given`, mid-render, giving an HTTP 200 ending in an ordinary `</html>` with the feed missing; migration 240's own header had claimed every reader cast, and said three writers where there are five. **And #715's whole behaviour was pinned by NOTHING:** dropping `body` and `summary` again, each with a plausible catalogue excuse, left 263 eraser checks, 93 coverage checks and both hard gates green. `MUST_BE_EMPTIED` in `gdpr-eraser-selftest.php` exists for exactly that and its own comment says so; #715 had added four columns to the instructions and not to the list.
- **The erasure review's two races are exercised now, and keep BOTH kinds of check (#724, #753, #735).** `ErasureReview::record()` is the ONE place a decision about a line of free text is recorded; it opens its transaction and THEN reads the cell with `FOR UPDATE` (#697: a lock outside a transaction is decoration). `GdprEraser` asks `ErasureReview::whyNotReadyToClose()` TWICE — at the top of `execute()`, which is the refusal an administrator meets, and again inside `closeRequest()`'s transaction, which covers a reviewable row being WRITTEN while a run is already going. **"No test can reach this" was a wrong CONCLUSION from a true fact, and that is the transferable part:** the comment said no end-to-end test could reach the second ask "because the test harness runs one request at a time", which is true and does not follow — ONE run is SEQUENTIAL (top check, every step, then the close), so a row appearing anywhere in the middle of that one run reaches it, with no second request, connection or process. **Ask which two events have to be ORDERED, never how many actors there are.** A trigger on `tblErasureAudit` provides the write (every step writes a line there), the same technique as `password-writer-selftest.php` section H. Measured with the second ask deleted: 233 steps, `failed: 0`, the request **`completed`**, and the person's own words still in the row. **And section R provably could NOT catch the lock being deleted** — R1-R4 take the lock IN THE TEST, so they prove the database locks rather than that `record()` asks it to; section T never takes it on the method's behalf (this connection holds the row, the real `record()` runs in its OWN PROCESS and blocks on it, which one process cannot do to itself). The harm, read out of `record()` rather than guessed: without the lock the second decider's in-transaction read is a consistent-snapshot read taken before the other committed, so it still sees the words, writes `keep`, and because the decision is written `ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE` that `keep` lands on top — the record then says the words are there over a cell somebody else emptied. **`uq_errd_item` is NOT a substitute for the lock:** no part of it can be empty so it really binds (unlike #487), but `ON DUPLICATE KEY UPDATE` makes it MERGE the second decision rather than refuse it, and a reader who checked only the key would conclude otherwise. Measured three of three with ` FOR UPDATE` deleted: R1-R4 green, T2 and T3 red. **Do not delete R5 as redundant now T exists:** T would still pass if the lock were taken some other way, and R5 would still pass if it were taken outside the transaction. Sections S and T drive the CLASSES — nothing presses two buttons on `review.php` at once. Test: `tools/erasure-review-selftest.php` (145 checks, real MySQL; needs `CREATE TRIGGER` and `proc_open`, each refused loudly rather than skipped). **AND THE SECTION WRITTEN BECAUSE R WAS VACUOUS WAS ITSELF VACUOUS AT THE MARGINS — the same shape one level up, found by the independent check.** `record()` refuses an already-empty line in TWO places — before its transaction (`oneItem()`: not a review item) and inside it under the lock — and T2 matched only the word `REFUSED`, so it could not tell them apart while its own label asserted the second. Measured: with a short sleep the worker returns the FIRST refusal, so **at a sleep of ZERO the whole of section T was green in BOTH states**, and nothing asserted the 1.5-second sleep had been long enough (with the lock removed T2 went red at 50ms and green at 20ms — a 40-times margin here, narrower on a loaded runner, failing in the FALSE-GREEN direction). T2 now requires the in-transaction wording and T1b asserts the worker is still blocked. **Be exact about which closes it: at sleep zero T1b still PASSES** (the worker is not yet exec'd, so it counts as running) and T2 is what goes red — a first comment claimed the opposite, which is a claim about a measurement never made. **When a test's verdict depends on a sleep, ASSERT THE STATE THE SLEEP WAS MEANT TO PRODUCE:** a sleep is a hope, a `proc_get_status` is a premise. **And S2 was a clause that could not fail:** `MYSQLI_REPORT_ERROR | MYSQLI_REPORT_STRICT` makes mysqli THROW (it takes BOTH — #694 rounds 5 and 6), so `query()` never returned the `false` it tested for, while four documents claimed the CREATE TRIGGER requirement was "refused loudly" — it gave an uncaught exception, no red check and no summary. **A check whose failure mode is an uncaught exception is a crash with a label, not a check that reports.** The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "The erasure review's two races (#735)".
- **Nothing asks for a reply it would decode with BROTLI (#716, CVE-2026-111497 "HTTP2Shell").** An out-of-bounds write in Google's Brotli decoder (1.0.3-1.2.0; fixed 1.0.10 / 1.1.1 / 1.2.1), remote, unauthenticated, CVSS 9.8, on CISA's actively-exploited list since 27 October 2026. The portal holds NO Brotli code, so its whole exposure was ONE LINE. **SETTING `CURLOPT_ENCODING` TO *ANY* NON-NULL VALUE SWITCHES LIBCURL'S CONTENT DECODING ON, AND LIBCURL THEN PICKS THE DECODER BY NAME FROM THE *REPLY*'S `Content-Encoding`** — so what you advertise is irrelevant. **`'gzip, deflate'` WAS COMMITTED AS THE FIX (`8dc848a`) AND STILL DECODED BROTLI**; an independent check caught it and it was reproduced before acting. Measured against a server declaring `br` unasked (libcurl 8.22.0, brotli/1.2.0): unset → 33 raw bytes NOT decoded; `''` → decoded; `'gzip, deflate'` → STILL DECODED. **SO THE RULE IS: DO NOT SET THE OPTION AT ALL.** `SafeFetch` sets nothing now, which is what every other curl site here already did — and why every one of them was already immune, while the one "configured" site was the only vulnerable one. **THE THREAT IS THE SERVER ITSELF** (an outside feed at an address somebody typed), so "we did not ask for it" is worth nothing. The cost is compression on the wire, accepted deliberately; if bandwidth ever matters more, switch libcurl's decoding off and gunzip in PHP, which would also make the size cap count COMPRESSED bytes. **FIX IT HERE, NEVER WAIT FOR A HOST:** this is a product on hosting we do not control, we cannot know which Brotli a customer's PHP links, and a version number cannot prove a vendor-patched build (#640's rule). **THE SIZE CAP IS NOT A GUARD** — its own comment says it counts bytes AFTER decompression, so the decode has already happened; and SafeFetch's other defences are all about WHERE it connects, not what the reply is decoded with. Every other curl site leaves the encoding UNSET, which is safe (libcurl then sends no `Accept-Encoding`); `.woff2` fonts are decoded by the BROWSER; Apache's `mod_brotli` COMPRESSES, which is the other direction. Two guards. **The first version of BOTH pinned the WRONG INVARIANT** — they checked what we ADVERTISE, and the self-test's label said "so no reply is handed to the decoder", the comforting half being false (the repo's own "a check that pins a reassurance" shape). The guard now fails on the option being SET AT ALL; the self-test serves a reply DECLARING `Content-Encoding: br` unasked and proves the bytes come back untouched — no Brotli library needed to write the fixture. Planted, the withdrawn `'gzip, deflate'` FAILS it, which the old check passed. **Two parsing faults in the guard too:** a `//` inside a string (a URL) ate the rest of the line, and a `/*` inside one swallowed to the next `*/` — then MASKING strings, the repo's usual remedy, BROKE BOTH DETECTORS because the value read IS a string. It walks the source and steps over literals now, and replaces block comments with their own newlines, because deleting them put reported line numbers 370 out. **Not the product, and still outstanding:** this workstation's Node is v26.11.1 against a fixed 26.12.1 and its libcurl links the affected brotli/1.2.0; no workflow pins a Node version, so CI uses its runner image's. Recorded on #716, not changed.
- **The change history ABOUT the erased person is emptied now, and the EASY WAY WOULD DESTROY THIRD-PARTY RECORDS (#707).** `tblAuditTrail.userID` is the ACTOR; what was CHANGED is the pair `(tableName, recordID)`, and NO column says who a row is ABOUT — so an entry reading "somebody edited tblUsers id N" was reachable by NOTHING, while `Logger::audit()` records a creation as the WHOLE new row and a deletion as the WHOLE old one, so a change to somebody's own account wrote their NAME AND E-MAIL ADDRESS in there — **measured, and NOT the telephone or postal address my first version claimed**: the two call sites that audit that table pass a name, an address and three flags, and nothing audits a deletion of it. `GdprEraser::eraseAuditTrailAboutPerson()` empties the three value columns; ANONYMISE, not delete (the owner, 2 November 2026 — D-031) — who acted, when, which table and which record all stay. **DO NOT "SIMPLIFY" IT BY ADDING THOSE COLUMNS TO THE ORDINARY INSTRUCTION'S `nullCols`:** that instruction matches `userID`, so it would empty the before-and-after of every change this person made to SOMEBODY ELSE'S record AND still reach no row about the person — wrong in both directions, #709's lesson in a new table. One check in section 20 of `tools/gdpr-eraser-selftest.php` exists purely to fail on that, and does. **And it is NOT in `DEDICATED_STEP_TABLES` on purpose, but MY FIRST REASON FOR THAT WAS FALSE and an independent check disproved it by measurement:** I wrote that registering it would make `fromPersonalDataCatalogue()` SKIP the table and lose its `ipAddress` instruction. It cannot — that instruction is HAND-WRITTEN, and the deriver already skips every hand-written table via `$alreadyHandled`. Registering it was tried: 222 instructions before and after, unchanged. **The real reason is better because it is enforced:** with it registered, `gdpr-coverage-selftest.php` fails its check that a dedicated-step table is erased, in time, AND EXPORTED — and this step empties three columns of a KEPT row, so the constant would be claiming something untrue. It is called directly from the run, as `eraseProfileImages()` is. **THE ORDER IS LOAD-BEARING, #709's trap again:** rows about the person's ORGANISATION memberships are found by looking their `tblUserSites` rows up and the walk DELETES those, so the step runs BEFORE the walk; moving it after turns four checks red. A column a dedicated step empties is recorded in `notEmptiedHere` beginning "Emptied elsewhere, on purpose", the `tblUsers.avatarPath` precedent. Not reached: a row about some OTHER record whose values quote the person — nothing on it says they are in it, and searching the values would match a namesake. Two fixture traps, both of which failed loudly: `ge_user()` does NOT make a membership row, and `ge_insert()` answers `{table, key}` with the key BY NAME, so `['id']` is 0. Adding a step shifts SIX hard-coded counts in that test; the file keeps a running note at each one, which is what makes them findable. **#708:** `tblCareVisit.followUpAssignedToID`'s instruction is correct and can NEVER MATCH — nothing writes the column — so the limit is recorded beside it rather than the instruction being deleted or the page changed (the owner, 2 November 2026); whether a visit should have an assignable follow-up owner is a product question. `tblKidCheckins.pickupName` was already recorded on 1 November 2026 and needed nothing.
- **An erasure can match a row through its PARENT table, and the shortcut is the destructive one (#709).**
  `GdprEraser::whyParentMatchUnusable()` is the ONE test of whether an instruction may do this, and it is public so
  `tools/gdpr-coverage-selftest.php` asks it rather than keeping a copy; `GdprEraser::whereForEntry()` is the ONE
  place the "which rows are this person's" clause is written for BOTH shapes, asked by the probe, the statement and
  `inventory()`. Only ONE instruction uses it: `tblCareVisit.notes`, through `tblCareCase.personUserID`, because a
  visit's note is about the person being CARED FOR while the only account numbers on the row are the two WORKERS.
  **`personCol` must be in `LINK_COLUMNS` and NOT in `ACTOR_COLUMNS` — two of the eight refusals, and the pair that
  stops real harm:** pointing it at `tblCareCase.openedByID` empties the notes of every case the erased worker
  opened — a third party's safeguarding records — and reaches none of their own. `personUserID` was on NEITHER list
  until #709 although the database links it to `tblUsers`. **THE ORDER IS THE TRAP AND IT WAS LIVE:** the parent's
  own step empties the column the look-up uses, and in the list as it stood the parent came FIRST, so an instruction
  added in the obvious place would have found nothing, changed nothing, and written "nothing held for this person"
  into the sealed trail. Two guards, both needed — the coverage test pins the POSITION (it judges the list, before
  anybody runs an erasure) and `whyParentLookUpUnsafe()` judges the RUN. **THAT GUARD WAS WRONG TWICE, IN OPPOSITE
  DIRECTIONS, AND THE SECOND IS THE LESSON.** Version 1 read the whole request's trail, so it refused the step on
  every SECOND run — a run with a failed step is put back and run again, so the request could never be completed
  (section 6 of the eraser test found it). Version 2 therefore read only the CURRENT run's lines, **and that
  reopened the very fault the guard exists to stop, one step further on:** on a second run the parent's change is
  not "from this run", so the step was ALLOWED, the link was already gone, and it wrote "nothing held for this
  person" while the request closed as COMPLETED with the note still in it. **No mistake by anybody was needed** —
  one transient error on that one step in run 1 is enough, because the walk carries on and the parent's step still
  cuts the link. **The docblock is what let it stand:** it claimed a refusal "leaves every note in place for a
  second run, whereas a miss is silent and permanent", which was false in the reassuring direction — the #645 shape,
  a comment stating the guarantee the code had forgotten to implement — and four documents carried the same words.
  **Worse, the self-test ASSERTED the fault as correct** (`$saysClearAfterwards === false`), which is why nothing
  caught it. Version 3 asks all three questions: parent untouched (safe); changed EARLIER IN THIS RUN (an ordering
  mistake, recoverable); changed BY AN EARLIER RUN (the link is gone for good, and the answer turns on whether this
  step had already answered while it was intact — any line of its own with a LOWER `auditID` than the parent's
  first change, because **a `none` from before the change means the person held nothing, while a `none` from after
  it means only that the look-up could no longer see**). Where it never answered in time the step FAILS and says
  the rows must be checked by hand: **a request needing a human look beats one reporting success it has not
  earned**, and that is a product decision, not a tidy-up. `self::$runStartedAfterAuditId` (`MAX(auditID)` at the
  start) exists ONLY to tell those two "changed" cases apart.
  **And the child side was coarse too, found by reading:** the "had this step already answered" question was asked
  by TABLE NAME, and `tblCareVisit` carries THREE instructions, so another instruction's line counted as this one
  having run. The pinned order makes them agree TODAY, but nothing pins where the other two sit relative to the
  parent, so moving one above it — harmless in itself — would have made the guard allow a step it must refuse.
  Only a parent-matched step writes HOW it reached its rows, so that sentence is the discriminator (`details
  LIKE`), and it is deliberately a clause that CAN fail: case 3 reads a REAL via line back through it, so a
  pattern that stopped matching goes red rather than refusing everything. `childCol` is NEVER emptied (a visit detached from its case is
  rubble with the note still in it), and an instruction naming no `nullCols` is refused because without an account
  link to drop it would change nothing while reading as coverage. The audit line says HOW it matched, FIRST and
  untruncated — written last and cut to 250 characters it sliced `personUserID` in half, losing the one fact nothing
  else records. **THREE THINGS WERE PINNED BY NOTHING AND PLANTING FOUND ALL THREE:** replacing the run-time guard's
  whole CALL with `if (false)` left both self-tests and all 46 audit scripts green; reverting the `inventory()`
  half was equally invisible, although "what we hold about you counts these rows too" is listed as delivered; and
  swapping two of the four names in the clause builder could not be seen, because the one real instruction uses
  `caseID` for BOTH `childCol` and `parentKey` — **a pin whose fixture uses one value for two names cannot tell
  them apart.** Section 8d closes all three (the whole condition, a refusal that must return `STEP_FAILED`, and a
  made-up instruction whose four names all differ), and `$mustRefuse` is now pinned with
  `array_keys(...) === [...]` because ITERATING it meant deleting a case stopped testing it while the label still
  said eight (#697's remedy). **Nothing covers the LIVE path through `processEntry()` with a real refusal** — the
  audit line it writes and what an administrator sees — because reaching the ordering half needs a list the
  coverage test refuses and the earlier-run half needs a trail a correct run never produces. **`$where === ''`
  there is a clause that CANNOT FAIL** and is a backstop, not a tested one. Tests: sections 8c and 8d of
  `tools/gdpr-coverage-selftest.php` and section 19 of `tools/gdpr-eraser-selftest.php`; count the plants from
  the commit message, never from here (#671).
  **ROUND 2 FOUND FOUR MORE PINNED BY NOTHING, AND THE FIRST IS THE ONE TO REMEMBER:** `if (false && isset(...))`
  on the line ABOVE switched BOTH guards off in ONE WORD with all 336 checks green, because the inner pins matched
  text that never ran — **when you pin a call, pin the gate that decides whether its block is entered at all**, or
  you have pinned a label on a door nobody opens; the rule's own call was pinned by NOTHING. **A CHECK CAN NAME
  THE RIGHT FAULT AND MEASURE THE WRONG VALUE:** the check labelled "not a 250-character slice" measured the
  guard's RETURNED sentence, a literal in the same file, so putting `mb_substr(..., 0, 250)` back around the AUDIT
  LINE — where the fault was — left it green; the cut is applied by the CALLER and no value inside the guard
  changes when it is (measured: 261 and 317 characters as stored). **And widening a list of "good" actions put the
  whole fault back:** adding `'failed'` to the actions counting as "this step already answered" left both tests
  green and is LIVE HARM, because a probe failure writes "could not look for this person (matched through ...)" —
  carrying the very discriminator the `LIKE` clause matches — below the parent's change; `'skip'` is out for a
  different reason (the rows WERE found and left). Also: the comment justifying the unescaped pattern claimed the
  identifier rule allows neither `%` nor `_`, and it ALLOWS `_`, a single-character wildcard — harmless today only
  because neither name contains one, which is why it went unnoticed. **And error 1146 was the silent-success shape
  again:** a parent-matched step names TWO tables, and a missing PARENT was logged as `skip` under the CHILD's
  name, so the child's rows kept every word of the note while the request closed as completed; the parent's
  absence is a FAILED step now, asked of the database rather than read out of an error message. **The cost of all
  this was #710, now FIXED (3 November 2026):** a step that refuses for ever is right, but `closeRequest()` ran
  only when nothing failed, so such a request could never be closed and could not be cancelled either.
  `GdprEraser::finishByHand()` is the ONE other way to reach `closeRequest()`, and it RUNS the request one final
  time rather than asking whether a step *would* refuse — because that question says nothing about whether some
  OTHER step still has work left, so it would close a request with real work outstanding. It closes only when
  every failure of that run is the permanent kind, and `self::$onlyHandWorkLeft` is the ONE place a step is
  recorded as that kind: do not add a second, and do not match on the refusal's WORDS — an older run's line
  would be found too, and the request would be closed although its latest run failed for a quite different
  reason. There is no force-close and no free-text box (a description of what somebody did by hand to a
  safeguarding note would put that note's content into `tblErasureAudit`, which is deliberately absent from the
  catalogue by #510 rule 7d and is never erased). Cancelling such a request is STILL refused, correctly, because
  earlier steps have already changed records. Two things that were pinned by NOTHING and an independent check
  found both: the guard that leaves the administrator's number out when the request is THEIR OWN (every fixture
  used an administrator who was never the person being erased — a fixture in which a guard's condition cannot be
  true cannot test the guard), and the surface of `$onlyHandWorkLeft` itself, where the check counted ONE
  SPELLING of the write, so the same fault written `$x = $x + [...]` in the could-not-read branch passed every
  gate — and that branch needs no second mistake, one failed read of `tblErasureAudit` would close the request
  and write into the sealed trail that no further run could help. Section 22 of `gdpr-coverage-selftest.php`
  pins the whole surface now (exactly four mentions, comments removed by `token_get_all()`, never a regular
  expression). **Two fixture lessons worth keeping:** every case in the
  first version of that world was opened by the control person, so the actor-column plant matched NOTHING and went
  red only on the wrong check while the third-party check passed vacuously — a fourth case, opened BY the erased
  person and ABOUT somebody else, is what makes it fail for the right reason; and `['personUserID' => null]` does
  NOT give a null column, because `ge_insert()` reads the people list as `$people[$name] ?? $other` and `null ?? D`
  is D, so a null must go through `$fixed`. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "Matching an erasure through a
  parent table (#709)".
- **Every two-factor gate takes the sign-in away FIRST (27 October 2026).** There are FOUR gates that demote a session to "waiting for a code" — the two sign-in callbacks in `Auth.php`, `_apps/auth/login/index.php` and `_apps/auth/login/webauthn.php` — and in each, `unset($_SESSION['user_id'], $_SESSION['2fa_passed'])` comes BEFORE anything else, `login_redirect` included. All four had it the other way round: a request chooses the TYPE of what it sends as well as the value, so `?redirect[]=x` made `Auth::safeRedirectUrl()` throw while `user_id` was still set, PHP wrote the session at shutdown, and the next request saw somebody signed in who was never asked for a code. **On the password form that needed nothing but the person's password.** `Auth::safeRedirectUrl()` therefore takes `mixed` and refuses a non-string with the fallback: never narrow it back to `string`, and never put a call that can throw between granting an identity and the check that earns it. A gate found by searching for `$_GET` will be missed — the passkey path reads the posted JSON. Checks, and BOTH are needed because neither catches the other's half: `tools/audit-checks/check_2fa_gate_order.py` (the order) and section Z of `tools/two-step-selftest.php` (the behaviour). Reversing the order alone is invisible to the self-test; that was measured, not assumed.
- **An OAuth `state` must be a non-empty STORED value (27 October 2026).** Both callbacks compared `$_SESSION['oauth_state'] ?? ''` with `$_GET['state'] ?? ''`, so an address carrying an empty `state=` passed when no sign-in had been started: `hash_equals('', '')` is true. That signed a visitor into whoever's authorization code was supplied. The stored value is now required to be non-empty before anything is compared, and a non-string is refused so `?state[]=x` cannot reach `hash_equals()`. Do not simplify that condition back.
- **Two-factor authentication (#613).** `auth/2fa/setup.php` is the ONE place backup codes are made, and it shows them only in the answer to the request that made them: never put a code, or the authenticator key, readable in `$_SESSION`. A code is single-use by `… AND isUsed = 0` plus "one row changed" in `auth/2fa/verify.php`, and is looked for among the person's own codes only. The old set is removed row by row after locking the person's own account row, never by `DELETE … WHERE userID = ?` (two people at once deadlock). Test: `tools/two-step-selftest.php`, alone on its server.
- **The installer asks for an ownership code (#499, #500).** `web/_install/owner_code.php` is the ONE place a code is written, counted and compared (`hash_equals()`); nothing is shown or acted on without it, and the finish asks again. Read the code file only through `installCodeRead()` (it waits for the writer's lock). The installer refuses when opened directly, and when the address has the web folder's name in it. **A choice for a database that is not empty ("carry on" or "wipe") belongs to ONE database:** read it only through `installChoiceHeld()` in `web/_install/db_state.php`, never from the session. The private folders' "refuse everything" file and the words for the channel are in `web/_install/install_common.php`, shared with the upgrade page. A missing `.channel` file never stops an install. Test: `tools/installer-selftest.php`.
- **The installation package and upgrades (#499).** `tools/package/build.sh` makes the package from git, `check-package.py` guards it, and `docs/INSTALL.md` (shipped as `INSTALL.md`) is the customer's guide to installing AND upgrading. **The portal is closed for an upgrade only while a database upgrade is really waiting:** that is decided in ONE place, `Maintenance::isActive()`. **The portal never records the version by itself**: `Maintenance::recordVersion()` is the one statement that records it and only the Upgrade page's button calls it; until then every request asks `Migrator::waiting()` once (about half a millisecond), and with the versions equal nothing is asked. A page that only asks uses `Maintenance::versionIsBehind()` or `upgradeNotice()`. Admin, Migrations runs nothing while the version is behind, and is for a global administrator only. The recorded version is read from the installation's own row only: start-up passes over one organisation's row of that name, and Admin, Settings refuses the setting (`Maintenance::lockedInGeneralEditor()`). When the portal cannot tell, `Migrator::waiting()` gives the cause. **A package carries NO `.channel` file:** the installer's finish writes it from `VERSION.txt`, only when there is none (`installWriteChannelFile()`), so an upgrade can never replace it. Never commit `web/.channel`. The domain guard's names come from `OWN_DOMAINS` in `tools/audit-checks/check_no_hardcoded_domains.py`: that is the ONE list. Tests: `tools/upgrade-pages-selftest.php`, `tools/package/package-selftest.sh`.
- **Time zones (#604).** `Site::timezone()` is the ONE place that answers an organisation's zone, and `Site::isZoneName()` the one test of what a zone name is. Do not read `tblSites.timezone` anywhere else, and do not bring back a `site.timezone` setting. `Site::zoneFor()` gives the zone to read a stored event time in. A stored event time is a reading of the event's own clock: never pass it through `strtotime()`/`date()`. Every `INSERT INTO tblEvents` names `timezone` (the column has no default). Migration 217's mark (`site.timezoneKeptAt`) is written by the install script only into a database that never held the old arrangement, so the installer's "carry on" choice keeps every organisation's zone. A column removed by a migration goes on `DbBackup::REMOVED_COLUMNS`. Giving retention deliberately does NOT follow the organisation's zone. Test: `tools/time-zones-selftest.php`.
- **A reminder e-mail now obeys the member's switch, and the morning summary belongs to ONE organisation (#666, #616, #683).** `WebPush::notifyPrefAllows()` is the ONE test of a notification preference and is public for that reason: the idiom is implemented FOUR times already (`WebPush::notifyPrefAllows()` public, `Workflow`'s private copy, `cron/user-reminders.php`'s function, `Giving::isOptedOutOfStatements()` inverted) and `sendReminderBatch()` in `_apps/cron/event-reminders.php` asks it rather than making a fifth. **`ImportNotifier` is NOT a copy — it already ASKS `WebPush`, which is the precedent this follows** — and `auth/account/save.php` only writes the column; counting those two gave "six copies", which reached four documents and two comments before the independent check killed it. Count function bodies, not file names. **A missing key, empty text or unreadable JSON all mean YES**, so nobody who has never opened the Notification preferences page loses a reminder. **The switch covers the DAY-BEFORE and ONE-HOUR reminders only** (both go through `sendReminderBatch()`): the DAY-OF SUMMARY is not filtered by it at all and never was — it goes to the event's coordinators and the organisation's administrators, not to members who answered "going", and the switch's own words are "Get notified ahead of events you have RSVP'd to". Never write "it stops the e-mails" unqualified; silencing the summary too would be a decision, not a tidy-up. Until 1 November 2026 the reminder query asked only who had answered "going". **Be exact about how little the switch did: it was saved and read by NOTHING** — `git grep -n eventReminders 8810a06 -- web/` finds only the two account pages that write the key and draw its tick-box (each a view and a handler, so four files), plus two schema column comments — no reader. Quote the command WITH its `-- web/` filter: without it the citation returns more than the sentence claims. The push is gated on a DIFFERENT key, `pushServiceReminders` ("Service reminders"), so switching this one off changed nothing anywhere, e-mail or push. Saying it was "honoured for the companion push" is false, flatters the old code, and replaced a comment on the page that had been RIGHT; it reached five places before the independent check caught it. A consent failure on a live mail path, under a standing notice on the page admitting the e-mails were not filtered. **ONE ADDRESS, TWO ACCOUNTS:** the recipient map is keyed by e-mail and two accounts can share a mailbox, so a refused row is dropped BEFORE the merge — an address is written off only when every account reading it has said no, and a refusing account can never upgrade somebody else's e-mail to the one naming the location. The morning summary joins `tblUserSites` on the EVENT's organisation (#616): the portal-wide `isAdmin` flag still counts, because that is how a single-organisation install marks its administrator, but it now reaches only the organisations that person actually belongs to. `Mailer::renderBase()` takes a site id and `Mailer::send()`/`sendTemplated()` read it from `$options['siteId']` (#683) — a scheduled job sends across every organisation with `$SETTINGS` merged once, and `Site::forceContext()` does NOT re-merge it, so without that the footer's name and support mailbox stayed with whichever organisation the job resolved to while the LINK followed the context, which is the hardest kind of half-right output to notice. **The issue blamed the reminder job and could not have been right:** all three reminder bodies are HTML and `renderBase()` only wraps a body that does not look like HTML, so no reminder e-mail has ever carried a footer; the live instance was the giving statement. **Three traps in testing this, all measured.** `tblEventReminderLog.recipientCount` counts only a message a mail service really TOOK, and part T of the time-zones test deliberately points the portal at an empty SMTP mailbox, so it is 0 for every message that went out perfectly well — read `tblEmailLog.toRecipients` through `mailTo()` instead, which says WHO. **Part T switches every account but one member OFF**, and the summary's query asks `u.isActive = 1` where the reminder's does not, so the first #616 check PASSED ON THE PRE-FIX QUERY planted back from `git show HEAD:` — the administrator was unreachable, not excluded. And the plant tried before that one, loosening the join to `LEFT JOIN`, ALSO passed: **a plant that will not go red means the fixture is dead just as readily as it means the code does not matter, and the passing run cannot tell you which.** The check now asserts the positive half too — the same administrator IS told about their own organisation's event — because "not told" that could mean "unreachable" is the fault it just had. **AND IT STILL PROVED NOTHING ABOUT THE QUERY, which the independent check found and is the better lesson:** that administrator holds all THREE ADMINISTRATOR grounds at once (the fixture loop passes one `$isAdmin` into `isAdmin`, `isRootAdmin`, `isSiteAdmin` and `isSiteRootAdmin` alike, though the query never reads `isRootAdmin`), so **each of those three `OR` clauses could be deleted on its own with every check green** — measured, one at a time. **THREE, NOT FOUR, and I wrote four in five places:** the COORDINATOR clause was never deletable-green, because that administrator coordinates nothing. **Claiming a measurement that was never made is worse than claiming none**, since the next reader has no cause to re-run it. **The REFUSING conditions were worse and all three were INERT:** `AND us.isActive = 1`, `WHERE u.isActive = 1` and `AND revokedAt IS NULL` could EACH be deleted with all 134 checks green, because nothing in that world could trip any of them — three separate production leaks (a person removed from an organisation, a closed account, a coordinator stood down, each still sent the morning diary). **A condition no fixture can trip is not a condition**, and two of those three were only found in the THIRD round, because rounds 1 and 2 looked at the clauses that ADMIT. **One account holding several flags cannot tell them apart; give each ground a person who holds it and nothing else, and each refusing condition a person who trips it.** Seven people now stand in that world. `tools/mail-queue-selftest.php` also calls this job over HTTP, but its day-of check is satisfied by the portal-wide flag alone, so do not count it as cover. Test: the three #666/#616 checks in `tools/time-zones-selftest.php` (134).
- **The "We're live" push (#612).** In `web/_apps/admin/livestream/index.php` the action `notify_live` is the ONE action open to an event's coordinator: the posted event decides, and must belong to the organisation (`HostConsole::eventBelongsToSite()` and `Auth::isCoordinatorOf()`, the same two calls the Host Console uses to draw the button). `WebPush::sendToChannel()` addresses only a signed-in person's device, and `api/push/subscribe` refuses a request that is not signed in. The counts on Admin, Integrations, Push copy the sender's rule: change both together. Test: `tools/go-live-button-selftest.php`.
- **A download that is not whole must never be trusted to remove anything (#645).** `IcsReader::parse()`'s `complete` flag is the ONE thing `FeedImporter` asks before it lets a refresh remove events, and its own comment says so. It is false unless the calendar started, the calendar ended, nothing was dropped, we did not stop early, AND **nothing was left open** — that last term was missing until 28 October 2026, so a download cut off INSIDE an event was called whole and the importer removed every event after the cut. The test is `count($stack) > 0`, NOT `$current !== null`: the stack catches an unclosed `VALARM` inside an event that ended, and `END` pops only on a match so one unclosed nested block keeps everything above it on the stack. **A comment claimed the flag was "false anyway" — it stated the guarantee the code had forgotten to implement**, which is why the fault survived. Check I7d guards the opposite direction: if this rule over-reaches, the portal silently stops tidying any calendar, for ever. **A repeat landing in the hour that does not exist moves FORWARD, keeps its exact length and still counts against `COUNT`** (so `COUNT=3` gives three dates) — right, deliberate, matching Google, Apple and Outlook, and now written beside `generateStarts()` with check C13 on it; changing it is the owner's decision, not a tidy-up. **The clocks going BACK is now settled too (#653, 28 October 2026).** `IcsReader::laterOfARepeatedReading()` is the ONE place that decides what a reading in the hour that happens TWICE means: the LATEST moment carrying it, worked out from the zone's own transitions. **Never go back to `new DateTimeImmutable($text, $zone)`: PHP does NOT give the later one** — it gives standard time in Europe and Australasia but DAYLIGHT time across the Americas, so that version was a no-op in New York, Chicago, Los Angeles, Havana, Santiago and the Azores and #653 stayed live there, with every check passing because every fixture was London. Testing "a zone that really changes" was not enough; it must be one that resolves THE OTHER WAY (I34i, plus Australia/Lord_Howe for a thirty-minute change). **Nor is `setTimestamp()` safe for building a candidate: on a zone-carrying object PHP re-resolves through the wall clock and hands back a DIFFERENT instant when the one asked for is inside a repeated hour, so it snapped back to PHP's pick in 81 zones — every one a PERMANENT base-offset move, standard time both sides (I34j, America/Metlakatla 2015).** Build such a moment as `new DateTimeImmutable('@' . $ts)` then `setTimezone()`. This ONE fix shipped as a no-op THREE times, each time with every check green; the committed checks pin four shapes (ordinary change, resolves-the-other-way, thirty-minute, permanent move) and the all-zone sweep that found the third is NOT committed. It is called from `buildOccurrence()` and **must stay there** — the recurrence key is the start turned into UTC and is STORED as `externalRecurrenceKey`, so resolving any earlier renames every such date and the importer deletes and re-adds it (check I34b). **It takes the zone the reading was WRITTEN in, never the organisation's:** a time ending in `Z` is an instant, not a reading, and the first version judged it in the organisation's zone and moved a real event an hour late — every reader check passed and only part G of `feed-importer-selftest.php` caught it (now I34h, the sole catcher). The symptom was wider than the issue said: a FOUR-hour series read as THREE: zero length is just the one-hour case. **Two leftovers that look alike and are not:** a date whose END falls in the repeated hour LOOKS an hour short but the stored data is right and reads back correctly (I34f, appearance only); a date whose FILE stated instants in `Z` can read end-before-start and its true length is NOT recoverable (I34h, a real limit needing a stored offset). Tests: I7b, I7c, I7d, C13 and I34–I34h of `tools/ics-reader-selftest.php`. **Two lessons about CHECKS from this package, both found by planting:** I7d guards the over-reach direction and guarded NOTHING, because not one fixture that existed before this work held a correctly closed nested block — no `END:VALARM`, no `VTIMEZONE` anywhere — so narrowing the stack pop to `VEVENT`/`VCALENDAR` passed all 345 checks while nearly every real calendar became "incomplete" (fixed by `nested-blocks-closed.ics`); **when a check exists to prove something ORDINARY still works, check that any fixture is actually ordinary.** And C13's comment claimed it pinned the exact-versus-nominal duration rule when its occurrences never cross a transition — I26 and I29 are what pin that. An overstated guarantee is worse than none.
- **Transcription, and the reserved word that could not save a transcript (#276, migration 229).** `Portal\Core\Transcription` is the ONE place anything about transcription is decided: `whyNotQueueable()` answers whether a recording may be queued (the page draws the button from it, the handler accepts from it — never a second test in a page), `refusalWords()` and `statusWords()` are the only words, `capFrom()` is the monthly ceiling, and `queue()` / `processQueue()` are the only writers of the queue state. **`fullText` IS THE RESERVED WORD `FULLTEXT`** — MySQL ignores case in keywords, so a bare `fullText = ?` does not parse; it must be qualified (`t.fullText`) or quoted. The statement that SAVES a finished transcript was written bare and therefore could NEVER RUN, throwing after the outside company had been called and charged; nothing noticed because no page could queue a recording in the first place. **Ask of any camel-case column whether its name is a keyword: `php -l` cannot see it and no audit script here can either.** The two writers of the queue state BOTH take the RECORDING's row lock (`FOR UPDATE`) — the transcript row may not exist yet — and the provider call is made only after the transaction is committed, never inside it. Refusing a `processing` row outright was the WRONG fix and wedged any recording whose request the host killed, so the refusal is "processing AND started recently" (`startedAt`, `STUCK_AFTER_MINUTES` = 120, on the server's own clock to match `queuedAt`). **One API key pays for every organisation**, so everything is scoped to one organisation and the budget follows #668: the installation's figure caps the organisation's, read from `InstallationSettings::layer()`. **Nothing drains the queue on its own (#672)** — do not describe transcription as automatic. Three traps worth keeping: a `<form>` inside a `<form>` is DROPPED by the parser and its button is owned by the outer form (that is how "Process next" saved the settings and transcribed nothing — ask the PARSED document with `Dom\HTMLDocument`, never the markup); `recordings/view.php` had NO flash block and nothing here prints one centrally (141 pages print their own), so a handler's message is swallowed unless the page prints it; and a check searching for the text of a guard passes when `&& false` is added to its condition, so pin the WHOLE condition. Test: `tools/transcription-pages-selftest.php` (153 checks, section Q states plainly what a single-worker harness cannot prove; section S guards #674). **Two gates, not one:** `recordings/transcribe` AND `admin/transcription/run` must EACH ask `providerIsReady()` — the run page had only the button's `disabled`, and a posted request claimed every waiting row and marked the lot FAILED. **`processQueue()` must never re-throw** (one unwritable row gave a bare 500 and abandoned the rest). And a check that looks for the TEXT of a guard stays green when `&& false` is added to its condition — pin the WHOLE condition; two checks here failed that way and planting caught both.
- **The API description is checked against the code (#641).** `web/_core/api-spec.json` is what a customer's developer reads, and until 30 October 2026 **nothing had ever compared it with the handlers** — 17 reachable endpoints were absent from it, two more were described only under their `api/v1/` address, and three operations denied a legacy address that works (19 addresses documented as a result). `tools/audit-checks/check_api_spec_matches_code.py` is now a hard gate. **It takes reachability from the router's own `^[a-z0-9\-]+$` pattern, so there is NO exemption list for helper files** — `_coerce.php`'s leading underscore is what both makes it unreachable and excludes it, and a hand-written list would need a line per helper and go red the day somebody forgot. It resolves the `api/v1/` facade through the four tables in `ApiRouter` (comments stripped first, so a commented-out resource is not read as live) and counts a `**Legacy alias:**` line as documentation. **That last part is NOT load-bearing, and the first version of its comment claimed it was** — measured, `aliases - targets` is EMPTY, because an alias sits inside the v1 operation already resolving to the same pair, so removing the parse leaves the check green. The "28 versus 19" figure that justified it came from a different model entirely and was false. The facade resolution IS load-bearing: without it `noticeboard/save` reads as a missing handler.
