Its FOUR named lists (`UNREACHABLE_ON_PURPOSE`, `UNSEEDED_ON_PURPOSE`, `ORPHAN_SEEDS_ON_PURPOSE`, `PAGE_ROUTED_ON_PURPOSE`) **fail BOTH ways** — an unlisted fault, and a listed entry that has stopped being true, which is how the migration harness's exceptions went stale unnoticed. **Two of those four are now EMPTY and the reason is the most useful thing here (#675, 30 October 2026).** `seeded_switches()` was ONE REGEX over `full_schema.sql`, which both seeds AND DELETES settings — it folds old migrations in — so a key written inside a `DELETE` counted as a seed. Four `api.expenses.*` rows were reported as orphan seeds that **no installation has held since migration 186**; loading the install script into MySQL 8.0.36 gives exactly three `api.expenses.*` rows. **Crying wolf was the harmless half. The dangerous half is check G:** a handler whose switch is seeded and then deleted is 403 to everybody, and the old reading called it seeded and PASSED — measured by appending a `DELETE` of `api.events.create.enabled` and watching the old regex still answer True. The two statement kinds are now applied IN FILE ORDER, and the function states what it still cannot see (a `DELETE` by `LIKE`, by subquery, or from application code). **And a file in an `api` folder is NOT necessarily an API endpoint:** `tblRoutes` holds `expenses/api/export` → `expenses/api/export.php`, and `Router::dispatch()` resolves a target under `PORTAL_APPS` first, so that address reaches the same file with **NEITHER** the `api.{app}.{action}.enabled` row **NOR** the app's API-folder switch in front of it — two fewer gates than the folder name implies. New **check I** (`PAGE_ROUTED_ON_PURPOSE`) names every such file; there is exactly one. `api.expenses.export.enabled` stays UNSEEDED on purpose (owner, 30 October 2026): seeding it publishes a second address for the same CSV that a bearer token still cannot use. **`seeded_switches()` was then WRONG AGAIN and the independent check caught it:** it ended a `DELETE` at the next `;` character, so a semicolon inside the statement's own `--` comment ended the span early, the key fell outside it and was read as a SEED — the SILENT direction, on the exact fault the rewrite existed to catch, and its docstring had claimed that case was merely over-reporting. One character decided it. `read_install_script()` now scans the file once, blanking comments and tracking quoted literals, which also fixed a second finding: a route target left quoted in a comment was being read as a live route. **`tools/api-spec-check-selftest.py` is the test OF both checks** — 33 plants, each required to be caught by the expected check AND BY NOTHING ELSE (comparing the whole set found two impure plants a looser test had hidden), plus 35 cases run against the schema validator directly in both directions, because an end-to-end plant cannot reach a keyword the file does not happen to use. Wired as its own hard gate because `and False` on any of eight conditions across the two checks leaves the check green — measured. **What it cannot do: judge whether a description is TRUE** (a documented parameter the handler ignores passes), or work out which verbs a handler accepts — `PORTAL_API_V1` relaxes `requireMethod()` at run time. **Five endpoints answer with their own flat JSON, not the portal's envelope** (`worship/state`, `worship/advance`, `livestream/ping`, `push/subscribe`, `push/unsubscribe`), and **`worship/advance`, `push/subscribe` and `push/unsubscribe`** return the replacement form token as a **top-level** field, never `meta.csrfToken` — a console drawn once and posting repeatedly must carry it forward or every control works once. **`livestream/ping` asks for no form token and returns none**, so it is in the flat-JSON five but not in that trio; getting this wrong once already put two memory files out of step. **`web/_core/api-spec.schema.json` is the shape the description must have**, executed by `tools/audit-checks/check_api_spec_schema.py` (a hard gate). Its validator is **hand-written and dependency-free** because there is no JSON Schema library on this machine and a check that cannot run before a push is the #646 failure again; `UNDERSTOOD` in that file is the keyword list, and **the check FAILS when the schema uses a keyword it cannot see**, because a keyword it ignores would make that part of the schema decorative. Every property and parameter now carries a `description`, and writing them found what matters: **asset money is in PENCE and expense money is in POUNDS**, many limits **quietly shorten or clamp rather than refuse**, `licenseKey` is **write-only and cannot be cleared**, an attendance breakdown and the noticeboard poster list are **whole-state** (an empty list wipes them), and leadership's `assignmentID` is the assignment and not the role. `PaginatedListResponse` describes a shape **no handler returns** and now says so. #676 (the events `status` allow-list not matching the column) is FIXED in `28f56cb` with migration 230, and it was FIVE writers not three — `calendar/manage/save.php` had no allow-list at all and `calendar/submit-save.php` supplies the value as a literal inside its INSERT. #675 is DONE and all three of its headline claims were wrong — **with one qualifier that matters: its narrower wording, that the `api/expenses/export` ADDRESS has never worked, is TRUE and still is on purpose.** What was wrong is the conclusion drawn from it: `expenses/export` answers a real CSV at `expenses/api/export`, a live button links to it, and the four "orphan switches" do not exist. What WAS wrong is that the Export button was drawn for everybody who could open `/expenses/approve` — a page with no gate beyond sign-in — and gave four of six account kinds a bare `Forbidden`. `ExpenseRules::mayExportClaims()` is now the ONE place that decides, asked by the button AND the handler; `tblUserDepts.isApprover` (one department) is NOT the `approver` ROLE (the whole organisation) and is deliberately not enough — widening it scoped is #681. Behaviour is pinned by section L2 of `tools/access-pages-selftest.php`.
- **A form that SAVES installation-wide must not PREFILL from the merged array (#674, #668).** `$SETTINGS` is already merged by the time a page runs, so a box filled from `App::settings()` shows the OPEN ORGANISATION's value while a Save writing `siteID IS NULL` writes the INSTALLATION's. **A global administrator opens the page while in an organisation that has its own row, sees it, presses Save without touching anything, and it is now everyone's — no mistake required.** Read such a field from `InstallationSettings::layer()`. **Several admin forms had this shape** — count them from the check's own output, never from here (it said fourteen were outstanding while twelve were, #637): `tools/audit-checks/check_install_wide_prefill.py` is a hard gate whose pending list has been EMPTY since #943 (`--strict`; nothing may be added to it), and `tools/install-wide-prefill-selftest.php` drives every such page over HTTP, because the static check skips any page that reads the layer at all. Two things about that check: it requires a `<form>`, because a runner, callback or webhook in the same folder reads the merged value *because it needs the effective one* (five such files would otherwise be false positives out of nineteen); and it skips a page that reads the layer **at all**, because **it reported the #674 fix itself as a fault** before that term existed — a fixed page often reads both deliberately, one for its boxes and one to REPORT a difference. The cost, in its header: a page fixed for one field and not another passes. Section S of `tools/transcription-pages-selftest.php` demonstrates the harm rather than arguing it — the old read put back changes the installation's provider on a Save that touched nothing.
- **One organisation's address is NOT every organisation's (#677, migration 231).** `admin/settings/organisation/save.php` writes at TWO levels and `$upsert()` takes the level as an argument **with no default**: the nine `org.*` keys and `portal.support.email` go to the organisation's own row, `site.url` alone goes installation-wide (#500) and is a global administrator's. **The support e-mail falls back to the installation's**, which is why migration 231 deliberately does NOT copy that row down — copying it would mean changing it later reached nobody. **An empty box DELETES the organisation's row:** the merge falls back only where there is NO row, so a stored `''` wins and would mask the installation's address for ever. `Mailer` needed no change FOR A WEB REQUEST — `renderBase()` runs BEFORE `MailQueue::add()`, so a queued copy keeps the sending request's footer — but **a scheduled job sends across every organisation with `$SETTINGS` merged once, so one organisation's support address reaches another's members (#683)**, and no page sets the INSTALLATION's value any more (#684). **Three faults of mine here, each caught by a test and none by reading:** the box prefilled from the MERGED array while Save wrote the organisation's row, so one press of Save on an untouched form pinned the installation's value as the organisation's own — #674 mirrored, and `check_install_wide_prefill.py` cannot see that direction; the inherited value was read from `$SETTINGS_INSTALLATION`, which **a page does not inherit** (only `$mysqli` and `$SETTINGS`), so the help text told administrators the opposite of the truth; and **I did not run `portal-address-selftest.php`, which covers this exact card and is wired into `portal-selftests.yml` (a file that has never run on GitHub — #754) — five of its 93 checks were red** while the package's own new test passed throughout. **Two traps, both real.** First, **ask whether a field ARRIVED, never what it contains**: the page disables the portal-address box for an ordinary administrator, a disabled control is not submitted, and the first version wrote `?? ''` anyway — which `PortalAddress::check()` reads as "no address", so saving a SUPPORT address **cleared `site.url` for every organisation** (the #667 shape, twice in a month). Second, **the check went blind to its own fix**: binding the level as a parameter stopped `check_install_wide_prefill.py` matching the literal `NULL` it looks for, so the whole folder stopped being examined — and binding the level is exactly what the fourteen pages still on its pending list should do, so the list would have emptied while the coverage did. Such savers are now named in `LEVEL_CHOSEN_AT_RUN_TIME`, failing both ways; measured first, only two files have that shape. Be precise about the old harm: **nothing outside that page reads the nine `org.*` keys**, so no address was ever shown to the wrong people — the support e-mail was the one that really was shared. Test: section O of `tools/access-pages-selftest.php`.
- **A LIMIT is capped by the installation, and `Portal\Core\SettingLimit` is the ONE general place that decides it (#668 part 1).** Three tiers, each able to tighten the one above and none to loosen it: a hard maximum in code, then the installation, then the organisation. **The rule is `min(merged, installation)` and needs NO query** to discover whether an organisation has its own row — the merged value already IS the organisation's own where it has one and the installation's where it has not; the independent check tried to break that and could not. `EventRecurrence::limitsFrom()` stays as the two-limit reader for repeating events. **ZERO IS THE TRAP AND IT WAS WRONG IN BOTH DIRECTIONS:** where 0 means "for ever" or "no limit" it is the LARGEST value, not the smallest, so as a number it passes every limit. Converting it in the HANDLER was worse than not converting — an installation that chose "for ever" refused EVERY organisation save including a tighter one (and blocked the page's other two settings, because the refusal exits before all three writes), while an installation that chose 3650 let an organisation store 0 and keep data FOR EVER. Pass `$zeroIsLargest` and let the class read it at BOTH levels; the rule is **"for ever" is allowed only when the installation itself allows for ever**, which is not expressible as arithmetic. A page must ask TWO questions (`installationAllows()` and `installationAllowsForEver()`), because `min="0"` offers for-ever whatever the max says, and must never prefill a value its own `max` forbids — the browser then refuses to submit the whole form. **The live breach fixed:** `/admin/settings/attendance` took 0–3650 days per organisation with no cap, so attendance personal data could be kept ten years on an installation intending ninety. It refuses with the reason and RECORDS the refusal. **#685** carries what part 1 does not fix: an organisation that ALREADY stored more keeps it, because the clear-out job reads the uncapped value. **The issue's 31-key list is a starting point, not the answer:** four shapes no key-name grep can see — nested-array reads, a `LIKE 'mail.queue.%'` query, keys behind CONSTANTS (how `attend.detailRetentionDays` hid), and four settings seeded but read nowhere. Only ONE dedicated page names a limit key directly, so part 2's tripwire must resolve constants. Test: `tools/setting-limit-selftest.php` (47 checks). **Its section D passed on FOUR disabled gates** — `if (false)`, `&& false`, a refusal that logs "nothing was saved" then writes, and one that silently clamps — so pin the WHOLE condition, require the block to `exit()`, and require it not to assign to the number; note `/\$days\s*=/` matches `$days === 0`, so use `=(?!=)`.
- **The giving-records ceiling is OPT-IN, and it is the one limit where capping is the DANGEROUS direction (#687, migration 234).** `giving.retention.years` is how long a donor's name and home address are kept, 1 to 50, per organisation. `GivingRetention::capInForce()` is the ONE place that answers the ceiling and it asks `Portal\Core\SettingLimit` — **0 means NO ceiling, so 0 is the LARGEST value**, #668's zero trap, passed as `$zeroIsLargest` rather than converted by hand. **It ships with no ceiling and nothing changes until somebody sets one** (the owner, 31 October 2026 — D-032: "can have the option to enforce such a limit - but can also choose not to. so customisable"), because the figure is a STATUTORY retention period and forcing it down could make a customer destroy records their own law requires. **TWO save-time gates are what make it a real cap and not #668's "a save-time cap is not a cap":** a figure above the ceiling is REFUSED with the reason, and a ceiling BELOW a figure an organisation has already chosen is also refused, NAMING the organisations (`organisationsAboveCap()`, which THROWS rather than answering an empty list it cannot stand behind). **BOTH GATES RE-READ THE CEILING LIVE AND LOCK THE INSTALLATION'S OWN ROW (`capFromDatabase($db, $siteId, true)`), inside the transaction that writes — #647's rule, and the first version did NOT.** It compared against the request's settings SNAPSHOT, and an independent check measured two ordinary web requests storing 50 YEARS UNDER A CEILING OF 7 with both saves successful, in 60 of 60 simultaneous pairs — while FIVE places claimed the only way in was hand-written SQL, which was the stated reason the job need not cap. **A claim that the portal "cannot" reach a state is the sentence that stops the next reader looking: measure it.** `whyYearsNotAllowed($years, $cap)` TAKES the ceiling rather than fetching one, so both gates ask the one rule (and an earlier version that fetched its own was left DEAD when the gate was inlined — a public method nothing calls, with an authoritative docblock, is worse than none). **The gate is on the figure the save really STORES, never on `$years`:** `$years` is the typed number OR the country suggestion, and gating it refused saves that store nothing — on a fresh installation with a ceiling, SWITCHING THE HOLD ON WAS REFUSED, the one control that STOPS records being cleared, with a message about "that number" when the box was empty. An organisation stores a figure only by typing one or by ticking "confirmed"; both are gated. **The one hole left is a figure written in SQL or restored from a backup, where the job keeps the records LONGER and the page says so**, because destroying a statutory record early cannot be undone. **The lock was then PROVED, which the first version never was:** 116 simultaneous pairs ended 0 of 116 above the ceiling, both orders were forced with an external session holding the lock (each path blocked ~5s, woke, saw the committed value, refused), and removing just ` FOR UPDATE` brought the fault straight back — so the LOCK closes it, not the live read. **THREE MORE FALSE REFUSALS followed, and the gate was never too WEAK, only too EAGER** — worth knowing because each looked like safety: gating before the UN-TICK branch was decided refused an administrator who cleared the years box with the tick left on (how the page DRAWS it), so the gate now runs AFTER rules 1 and 2 on `$yearsOwn`, which is by then exactly what will be stored; judging the organisation's own ceiling when the save did not CHANGE it blocked the hold, the year end and a plain Save over a number the page itself had prefilled (#667/#677: ask whether the field CHANGED); and **the YEARS box was prefilled above its own `max`** when a stored figure exceeded the ceiling — #668's named fault, in the same edit that fixed it for the ceiling box five lines below, under the comment citing the rule, and in headless Edge the form would not submit at all, so the only way to use the page was to SHORTEN a statutory period. **`max` is the hard maximum on both boxes; the handler is the boundary.** A pre-existing deadlock on two organisations' first simultaneous save is #700 (proved pre-existing against `git show HEAD:`). Three more from the earlier check: the activity log said "Nothing was different" because the old ceiling was read AFTER the commit (`settingsAt()` has no cache); the own-ceiling box was PREFILLED ABOVE ITS OWN `max`, #668's named fault reproduced five lines under the comment citing it; and a comment justified the "did the field ARRIVE" design with a protection that does not exist. An organisation that has chosen NOTHING follows the country suggestion, which may exceed the ceiling harmlessly: nothing is cleared until "confirmed" is ticked, and ticking it WRITES the figure through the same refusal. **Set on the giving-records page, never the general settings editor** — the issue said `lockedInGeneralEditor()` "has to change" and it does NOT: that editor asks this class about the LOCK and nothing else (checked at `_apps/settings/save.php`), so a ceiling written there would skip the refusal. The refusal an organisation reads NEVER says who set the ceiling (the two levels may be different parties, as `SettingLimit::refusalWords()` already decided); naming organisations to the INSTALLATION's administrator is the other direction and is their own business, and the activity log carries only a count. The exemption in `check_limit_settings_capped.py` is DELETED, so that hard gate now requires the cap — it reported its own stale entry. Tests: section 6 of `tools/giving-retention-selftest.php` (9 of 63), the words only; **the two save paths are covered by NO automatic test and that is #699.**
- **The limits sweep, part 2: what is actually reachable, and the tripwire (#668).** **All 32 limit-shaped candidates are seeded INSTALLATION-WIDE, and `_apps/settings/save.php` refuses to ADD a per-organisation row for a key the installation already has** — so almost none of them is overridable whatever its name suggests. The complete reachable set was `attend.detailRetentionDays` (its own page, part 1), the **FOUR** `mail.queue.*` numbers in `MailQueue::NUMBERS` (the one deliberate exception to that refusal, via `$onlyOwnRowClashes`), and — found by the independent check — **`giving.retention.years`**. The mail-queue four are fixed: `ownNumberCheck()` tested only the hard range IN CODE and merely MENTIONED the installation's value, and the page needs `App::isAdmin()`, so an organisation's own administrator could keep a failed message's body on the installation's disk for a year where it chose seven days. **A SAVE-TIME CAP IS NOT A CAP:** `numbers()`, which the JOB uses, now caps too — a row that predates the check, was written by SQL, or came back from a whole-database restore was otherwise still obeyed, and the comment justifying the design said the two readers must not disagree while they did. **`giving.retention.years` is DELIBERATELY NOT CAPPED (#687):** 1 to 50 years of a donor's name and home address, per organisation, with no installation layer at all — but capping is the DANGEROUS direction there, because the figure is a statutory retention period and forcing it down could make a customer destroy records their law requires. The owner decides; the tripwire names the file in `NOT_A_LIMIT` so the judgement is on record. **The noticeboard** (the owner asked specifically) is written by no page at all. **`check_limit_settings_capped.py`** is a hard gate: which files can create a per-organisation settings row, and do any write a limit-shaped key without asking. **It must read `web/_core` as well as `web/_apps`** — two of the seven per-organisation writers are in `_core`. **It RESOLVES CONSTANTS**, without which it would pass on the very fault it guards (`AnonymousCheckins::RETENTION_KEY` appears nowhere as a literal). **It must JOIN ADJACENT STRING LITERALS** or it finds nothing, because the house style builds `'INSERT … ' . 'VALUES …'` in pieces. **And "does this name look like a limit" is the LAST segment, split into words, matched WHOLE** — a narrower rule missed five keys including `keepFailedDays`, a wider one found `min` in "reminder", `count` in "countryCode", and the namespace `retention` in six settings that are not limits. Four of its findings were false alarms across three versions, and #668 says a check that cries wolf gets switched off. Tests: `tools/setting-limit-selftest.php` (47) and section F8 of `tools/mail-queue-selftest.php` (159).
- **Content translation IS reachable now, and the allow-list is the part to leave alone (#485, #761, migration 248; the owner, 4 November 2026 — D-049).** `Portal\Core\ContentTranslation` is the ONE place that decides what may be translated and for whom: `SOURCES` is the allow-list, `whyNotOffered()` takes FACTS so the page and the handler cannot disagree (#647), `readSourceText()` is the ONE place the words are read, and `readerLanguage()` the one place the reader's language is decided. `isSwitchedOn()` is the one question whether the feature is on (D-075, #792), and `api/translation` follows the Translation app's own switch like any other app — it must not join push, tours and users. `_apps/translation/api/content.php` is the ONE caller of the engine, at `api/translation/content`; `_core/templates/translate-control.php` is the ONE place the control is drawn. **THREE things may be translated, all of them the ORGANISATION's own published words:** an announcement's `body`, a project's `description`, an update's `content`. **A PRAYER REQUEST IS DELIBERATELY NOT TRANSLATABLE AND MUST NOT BE ADDED** — D-049's own requirement is that the control "must not appear where it would send a third party's words", a prayer request is the table this portal already treats as a person's own words about themselves (D-016, #715), and it is routinely about somebody else ("pray for my mother") who can never agree; **the READER's opt-in cannot stand in for the WRITER's**, and a congregation-visible request is read by every member. `tblNewsletter` is out for a duller reason: no page shows it, and a surface nothing reads is #671's "a lie with a Save button". The allow-list is pinned WHOLE by the hard gate `tools/audit-checks/check_translation_text_from_db.py`, so adding a table fails on purpose. **IT WAS A REWRITE, NOT A RE-ROUTE, AND THAT IS THE LESSON FOR #277/#658:** `_apps/api/translate.php` took the text from `$_POST['text']` and looked nothing up, so its allow-list constrained the LABEL and not the words — a member could have spent the customer's money translating anything, and the answer was cached against a real row's number, so the next reader of announcement 42 would see text that was never in announcement 42; `lookupCached()` asks nothing about who is reading, so a posted row number read back any organisation's words; and nothing checked the caller could see the row (#761). **Making an unreachable handler reachable is the act that makes its faults live.** Money and consent: `Translation::capPence()` holds the organisation to what the installation allows (D-013, `zeroIsLargest` because 0 means no limit — #668's trap), enforced in `translate()` where the money is spent and not only at the save; a member must switch it on for their own account, and an API key is refused because a key cannot hold a person's permission. **#762 IS BUILT (D-059, migration 254): a second limit counts the NUMBER of requests, `translation.monthCallCap` (default 1,000 per organisation per month, hard maximum 100,000, 0 = none, capped by the installation through `SettingLimit` exactly as money is).** Both limits are counted in `tblTranslationUsage` just before a request is SENT (a request that cannot be sent — no key, unknown provider — is refused as `not-set-up` and not counted, by `Translation::providerReady()`) — **never from `tblContentTranslation`, which is a cache with one row per piece and language and so counts pieces, not requests** (and since #799, migration 256, a cached copy is served only while the source's words still match the fingerprint `sourceHash` it was made from, so an edited piece is translated and paid for again; a row with no fingerprint is STALE, never fresh, and the upsert's update clause must write the fingerprint or every press after an edit pays for ever, E3-06 — and `persist()` had bound the provider as a number since `8625b9e`, so every row said `0`). `Translation::reserveCall()` takes the slot BEFORE the call in one conditional UPDATE, so it holds under concurrency; a request the provider refuses still counts; money is asked first so a money refusal uses no slot; an unreadable record REFUSES (`limit-unknown`) — never "nothing used". `Translation::lastRefusal()` is the ONE answer to why `translate()` said no; do not re-derive it from a fresh read. The member gets the same words for either limit; the ADMIN page says which (`data-limit-reached`), and that raising the other will not help. Tests: sections F, F2 and J2 of `tools/content-translation-selftest.php`. On Google and DeepL the number limit is the one reached first (~£10 at 1,000); on Anthropic and OpenAI the money limit is. A reported cost reaches the record only through `Translation::settle()`, which also returns the words — do not split them again. **And the engine's code is pinned WHOLE by the hard gate `tools/audit-checks/check_translation_engine_pinned.py`** (one PHP token per line, comments and spacing free), with every built-in written fully qualified (`\ceil`, `\CURLOPT_POST`) because inside `Portal\Core` an unqualified name is looked up in the namespace first — eight review rounds, each defeated by one line just outside what was pinned until the whole file was, and the gate refuses an unqualified built-in in every position the grammar does not prove harmless. Change the engine, then re-read it against the bypass table in DEV_NOTES and `--repin` deliberately. **#763 IS FIXED (5 November 2026) — a member's saved interface language is applied now, so `readerLanguage()` reading `tblUsers.locale` rather than `I18n::locale()` is no longer a work-around for a broken system but simply the right source for a translation made FOR one person; the two can still differ.** Three traps worth keeping: an HTML comment in `footer.php` naming the control's attribute put that name in EVERY page served, which both leaked a developer note to visitors and made four checks wrong (#647's shape again — a comment answering a check); the gate's "no language" test normalised the value first and `normaliseLocale('')` answers `'en'`, so it was a clause that could never fire; and `Translation::detect()` is a cheap guess that read "money for a new boiler" as WELSH, because `' a '` is one of its Welsh markers. The provider's words are written with `textContent`, never `innerHTML`. **THE FIRST INDEPENDENT CHECK FOUND FIVE REAL PROBLEMS and the worst was on a page that takes money: `Auth::csrfToken()` is ONE token per session, so every control on a page AND every ordinary form share it and the first accepted press kills the rest — measured, pressing Translate on `/projects/view` then pledging gave a bare "Bad request" with the member's typing gone, and SEVERAL refusals carry no replacement (not signed in, a wrong method, an API key, a refused form token, the endpoint switched off — measured; only the form-token one is cured by reloading), so `shareToken()` keeps it for the WHOLE PAGE.** "Nothing has been charged" stood in three places and CANNOT be known (a 200 with an unexpected shape, or a timeout after the provider accepted, is still billed); an empty or non-numeric monthly limit silently became "no limit", the most expensive outcome, because `(float) ''` is 0 and 0 means no limit; the organisation test and `isPublic` on the two project statements were **pinned by nothing** (the world had B's announcement but no project of B and no unpublished project — a condition no fixture can trip is not a condition); and the gate's own "direct guard" read only `$body['…']`, so ONE planted line reading a request HEADER put #761's fault 2 back live with 61 gates and 66 checks green. **TWO OF MY OWN FIXES THEN FAILED THE SAME WAY WITHIN THE HOUR: a pattern spanning code and a STRING (`$_SERVER['HTTP_`) can never match in a copy whose strings are masked, and `shareToken(` was satisfied by `function shareToken(` — A DEFINITION IS NOT A USE.** And the script was read by NOTHING: deleting the token sharing and swapping `textContent` for `innerHTML` left every check green, so check F now reads both rules as text; no test here drives a browser. **A FOURTH ROUND FOUND TWO MORE AND THE PATTERN IS THE LESSON: four rounds, four times closing the SPELLINGS I was handed and calling it the class.** A reassignment (`shareToken = function () {...}`) killed the whole token fix with the gate green, because round 3 had closed the second-DECLARATION spelling; and `&&` → `||` in the reload guard, ONE CHARACTER, restored a fault round 2 found, because that narrowing was pinned as two string literals and not as a condition. **The remedy was not a fifth cleverer pattern but to pin the WHOLE FILE** — its code, comments stripped and whitespace normalised, must equal `tools/audit-checks/portal-translate.pinned.js`, so any change to a STATEMENT fails and `--repin` regenerates it as a separate visible act. **A FIFTH ROUND FOUND THE PIN'S OWN HOLE AND IT IS WHITESPACE: IN JAVASCRIPT A NEWLINE IS A SEMICOLON** (automatic semicolon insertion, after `return`, `throw`, `break`, `continue`, `++`/`--`). The first pin collapsed ALL whitespace, so one added newline after a `return` changed what the script does with the gate green — `outputBlock()` answered `undefined`, so a translation that had ARRIVED AND BEEN PAID FOR was thrown away while the member was told nothing came back, and it still PARSES, which is the dangerous kind. `normalised_code()` keeps line breaks and frees indentation, blank lines and comment text, and is used by the comparison AND by `--repin` so the generator and the checker cannot disagree. **The transferable rule: when you normalise before comparing, enumerate what the normalisation throws away and ask whether the language cares about any of it** — for JavaScript that is yes for line breaks and no for indentation; for Python the opposite; for SQL, case. **A SIXTH ROUND DEFEATED THE PIN WITH ONE LINE AND THE CHARACTER WAS INVISIBLE: JAVASCRIPT ENDS A `//` COMMENT AT FOUR CHARACTERS** — LF, CR, U+2028 LINE SEPARATOR and U+2029 PARAGRAPH SEPARATOR — and the blanker ended one at `\n`. Python's read folds a lone CR by accident; U+2028 came through, the blanker ran past it, blanked the real code after it, and `splitlines()` then dropped the line as blank — so ONE line put `innerHTML` live with the gate green and every audit check green (executed in node: the planted markup ran), defeating the pin AND the `innerHTML` rule at once. **The ACCIDENT is worse than the attack:** U+2028 is what text pasted from a PDF carries, and a comment containing one makes the WHOLE FILE fail to parse, so the control silently does nothing on every page while the gate says OK. **The rule, round 5's sharpened: when you blank or split on a delimiter, use the delimiter set THE LANGUAGE uses, not the one your own language's library offers.** Also: the stated residue was understated in the direction that bites — a tab and a non-breaking space collapse too, and `'CSRF check failed'` DOES depend on its single space (it is matched against `ApiAuth`'s exact words), so "no string depends on that" was a reassurance the check could not support; that one comparison is now checked un-normalised. **A SEVENTH ROUND FOUND THE SAME CLASS TWO MORE WAYS AND ONE IS AN ORDINARY ACCIDENT: an unterminated `/*` after the last statement** (Python blanks it to the end of file, JavaScript refuses the whole file, and the normalised code is unchanged so the pin matched) **and U+001F in a statement** (the one C0 control Python strips WITHOUT splitting, so U+0085/1C/1D/1E failed by accident and it did not). `js_will_not_parse()` now asks FIRST whether JavaScript would refuse the file at all. **The rule in its third form: of any normalise-then-compare guard ask not only "what does the language care about that I discard?" but "could the input be something the language REFUSES OUTRIGHT, which my reader accepts?"** — a file that does not run cannot be guarded by comparing its code. Also: `repin()` was the one read not folding U+2028, so generator and checker could disagree (it failed SAFE, but the failure message's advice could never work on that input); and `\v`/`\f` as whitespace made the pin go red over legal JavaScript, so both are folded to spaces — crying wolf gets a check switched off (#483), so both directions must be checked together. That is right here and nowhere else for one reason: NOTHING RUNS THIS SCRIPT. Every bypass from all four rounds now goes red, and so does a harmless rename, by design. **And the self-test was changing two settings it never named:** the save handler upserts six installation-wide keys from its own defaults when a field is absent, so section J's eleven presses reset the two provider MODEL rows and nothing put them back — round 3's `libre_base` finding one key over. **It is not enough to track what a test WRITES; track every setting anything it PRESSES can write** (`rememberSetting()`). **A SECOND ROUND FOUND FIVE MORE AND THREE WERE THE SAME FAULT ONE STEP ON. THE FORM TOKEN HAS THREE CARRIERS, NOT TWO:** `header.php` puts `<meta name="csrf-token">` on every page and five scripts read it from there, two of them loading everywhere — measured, after a press a guided-tour post answered 403 on the very page the pledge fault was measured on, and `event-hub-upload.js`'s `syncCsrf()` was already the right pattern, so this was a THIRD copy of one rule with a half missing. **THREE MORE VISIBILITY CONDITIONS WERE STILL PINNED BY NOTHING** (the project-UPDATE's `isPublic`, and the announcement's `isDeleted` and `publishAt`) while four documents said the class was closed — FIVE clauses, not two, and the enumeration is mechanical: every clause in every `SOURCES` statement needs a fixture that fails it. **THE MONEY FIX WAS PINNED BY NOTHING** — deleting the whole refusal left every check green, because nothing had ever posted to `/admin/translation/save`; a fix's own guard is part of the fix. **AND CHECK F PASSED ON CODE WHERE THE TOKEN FIX WAS GONE, TWO WAYS:** a call inside `if (false)` (#709: pin the gate that decides whether the block runs) and a write loop changed to `index < 0`. `shareToken()` is pinned WHOLE now, body and call line, as `check_table_name_comparisons.py` pins its helpers. **A new message was untrue the moment it was written:** "please reload" was shown for every 403 carrying no replacement, and `ApiRouter` refuses a switched-off endpoint before any rotation, so a member was told to reload for ever while the portal's accurate words were discarded. Tests: `tools/content-translation-selftest.php` (88 checks, real HTTP, a stub provider on its own port so nothing reaches the internet; twenty-two plants over three rounds, twenty-one caught by the check meant to catch each and the twenty-second recorded as uncatchable because the handler's gate refuses before the engine is reached) and the hard gate above.
- **Changing a repeating series' pattern (#669, migration 232).** `Portal\Core\SeriesRepattern` is the ONE place that decides what a pattern change does: it takes FACTS and returns a plan (re-time, add, remove, leave alone with a reason), opens no connection and has no clock of its own, so the preview page and the handler that applies it cannot disagree. **A date that stays is UPDATED IN PLACE — only its start and end — and that is the whole design:** it keeps a hand-changed title, venue or description safe without having to detect those changes at all, and re-creating instead would destroy every attached record through the CASCADE rules. **Pairing is by the DAY, never the time**, which is why one rule covers all four operations. `patternStart` AND `patternEnd` (migration 232) record where the pattern put each date, so "moved by hand" is WORKED OUT rather than remembered; **both are compared, and comparing the start alone let a hand-lengthened service be silently shortened again** while the docblock promised it caught "a changed TIME" — and a move must write BOTH, or every date reads as hand-edited next time and the page stops working after one use. `updatedAt` cannot stand in, because `ON UPDATE CURRENT_TIMESTAMP` means the bulk-edit page already moves it. Neither is backfilled — claiming every existing date is untouched would move exactly the dates they protect — and an unrecorded date falls back to re-reading the series' current rule, which judges the START only and is the only reason the feature works on an installation that already has series. **The recorded origin wins over the rule when both could answer.** `SeriesRepattern::ATTACHMENTS` names all **34** tables that NAME an event and is the only thing stopping a change hiding a date that carries a register; it LOOKS complete whatever is in it, so `tools/audit-checks/check_series_attachments_complete.py` is a hard gate failing BOTH ways. **A FOREIGN KEY IS NOT HOW EVERY TABLE SAYS IT, and building the list from the schema's keys is how the two most sensitive tables were missed:** `tblEventRSVPs` (`eventID INT NOT NULL`, no key, holding every member's going/maybe/no answer — the thing the owner's decision names out loud) and `tblZoomMeeting`. The check now looks for the `eventID` COLUMN, excluding a table whose own auto-increment key is called that (`tblWebhookEvent`). Its docstring had asserted "nothing in this schema does that today (checked)" — written WITHOUT checking, and false. Two parsing traps too, each got wrong first: a key can be declared by `ALTER TABLE` (that is how `tblAttendanceSessions` gets its, and a walk-back-to-CREATE parser blamed `tblEvents` AND missed it), and comments must be stripped before matching. **With no foreign key the schema never stops an event row being deleted out from under these two. Be precise about what that costs, measured rather than reasoned: this page SOFT-deletes, no ON DELETE rule fires on an UPDATE, so all three values behave IDENTICALLY here — every attached row survives pointing at a date nobody can see, for all 34 tables. `'no rule'` is the worst of the three only if something later really DELETEs the row.** A date the pattern ADDS **arrives as a DRAFT** (#692, the owner, 31 October 2026 — D-033) — it does NOT inherit the template's status, and the template's status is not read at all. It has no registration window of its own (the template's `registrationClosesAt` is a moment just before the TEMPLATE's date) and nothing beyond the series' description, so publishing it at once puts a half-finished entry in front of members; and a hidden date cannot be brought back (#691), so the recoverable mistake is the one to make. **The preview AND the message afterwards must BOTH say so** ("added as drafts"), pinned by S15b and S15c of `tools/event-repeat-pages-selftest.php`. Publishing a whole change's worth at once is #696. The dates it adds are **published from the series' BULK EDIT page**, which the handler redirects to — it lists every date including drafts with a tick-box each and a bulk `Status` control, so a whole change's worth goes in one step. **Do not claim it is one at a time** (#696 was raised on that assumption and closed). **And the message must NOT say subscribers see one repeating entry while any date is a draft:** `calendar/export.php` reads a run with `AND e.status = 'published'` and collapses only when the PUBLISHED dates match the rule, so the sentence has THREE answers (all published / rule written but drafts remain / no rule), pinned by R12b and S15d. The rule row itself is still decided on the dates alone. A date the pattern ADDS takes the series' name, venue and settings and NONE of one date's own history: never born cancelled, and carrying no cancellation reason, moderation note or submitter name and e-mail — the copy took every column but four, and `GdprEraser` clears neither `submitterName` nor `submitterEmail` on `tblEvents`, so those copies could not have been erased on request. **Its address is asked of the database, never built from the date's position in the add list:** `uq_event_site_slug` covers SOFT-DELETED rows, so a day removed and re-added collided and the whole change was rolled back for ever, intermittently. The remedy link for a date left alone is `?edit=`, because `calendar/manage/index.php` reads that and reads `eventID` NOWHERE. The old rule row is ALWAYS removed and a new one written only when the series then holds exactly the pattern's dates — otherwise subscribers see each date on its own (#670), which is the usual outcome and is said in the success message. **`(string)` on a numeric array key is load-bearing:** PHP makes `'1' =>` an INT, and under `strict_types` `htmlspecialchars(int)` is a TypeError — the page rendered most of its form, threw, and the error handler printed the footer, so the answer was HTTP 200 ending in an ordinary `</html>` with the preview silently missing, invisible to `php -l` and to every audit script. **And the gap worth keeping: removing the "did they press confirm" test failed NOTHING**, because the only request carrying a plan fingerprint was one that also confirmed — yet the hidden fingerprint sits in the same form, so a real browser's second "Show me what would change" would have applied the change. A guard never exercised alone is not covered. A date the pattern ADDS also takes NO registration window — the template's `registrationClosesAt` is a moment just before the TEMPLATE's own date, so a date added weeks later arrived with registration already closed, and both register pages treat that column as a hard gate. `registrationEnabled` is given its DEFAULT and not NULL, because it is `NOT NULL` and binding null fails the whole INSERT. The added date's `timezone` is the zone the READINGS were made in, never the template's. **And never say a hidden date can be put back: there is no restore path anywhere in this repository (#691), only `FeedImporter` ever clears `isDeleted`.** Tests: `tools/series-repattern-selftest.php` (72, no database) and sections R and S of `tools/event-repeat-pages-selftest.php` (247 over HTTP; it needed a third, non-administrator account because both existing ones are admins). **SIX of its checks were VACUOUS and planting found every one:** two passed on the rule row written at CREATION time; one pinned the recorded finish on a change that re-timed nothing; the slug check removed dates the SAVE HANDLER had named, whose addresses cannot collide with the page's; the "last entry in the constant" claim was false (`tblEventRSVPs` is 33rd of 34, so a loop stopping ONE table short still passed); the zone check could not fail while every event in the test world shared one zone; and the Zoom check attached a record to a date an earlier check had already removed. First, 33rd and LAST entries are exercised now — a loop skipping a MIDDLE table is still not caught, and the wording says so. Nothing covers two administrators racing on one series; the attachment list is re-asked inside the transaction, which closes the administrator-versus-door-steward window only. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "Changing a repeating series' pattern (#669)".
- **A part-written PDF is reached by an erasure and swept (#721), and a part-written upload is never recorded (#720).** `Pdf::PART_MARK` is the ONE place the temporary mark is named — the writer builds the name from it and `Pdf::sweepPartFiles()` looks for it, so two literals would be two places to change and **the half that broke would be the SWEEP**, which would simply stop finding anything: no error, no log line, and a folder with nothing to sweep looks identical. The hard gate `check_pdf_write_verified.py` WENT RED on that change, which is the gate working; it accepts the constant form now AND fails when the constant is missing or empty, and both forms still require the name to be built FROM `$filePath` (the temporary must be in the SAME folder, because `rename()` is only atomic within one filesystem). **#721 was a cost of #711's own fix, recorded at the time:** a process killed between the write and the rename leaves `statement-7-2026.pdf.part-…`, and the eraser's glob was `statement-<donorId>-*.pdf`, which does not match it — a giving statement holds the donor's name, home address and every gift. **Before #711 the same crash left a TRUNCATED `…-2026.pdf`, which DID match and was erased**, so it was a narrow regression in erasure reach taken in exchange for not destroying good files. **THE PRIVACY HALF TOOK TWO CHANGES AND THE GLOB ALONE WAS NOT ENOUGH:** widening it made the erasure FIND the file and `statementFileIsKept()` then KEPT it — "every gift" answers kept for anything, and under "receipted only" the `-4` chop for `.pdf` left an unparseable period so the "no period means keep" fallback applied to rubble; measured, the genuine statement came back NOT kept and the part file KEPT, and the step then COUNTED it and told the administrator it was tax evidence. A file carrying `PART_MARK` is a write that never finished, so it is refused before the scope is looked at. **Nothing caught it because the test world never wrote a kept-donor row for the erased person, and the proof was inside the test** — its own fixture period does not parse either, so if that branch had run the EXISTING check would already have failed. **AND THE SWEEP DELETED A MEMBER'S OWN UPLOAD:** the first version walked all of `_uploads` for any name CONTAINING the mark, under a docblock saying "nothing else uses this mark today" — false, because `_apps/documents/upload.php` and `_apps/recordings/upload.php` take the stored extension STRAIGHT FROM THE CLIENT FILENAME, so `report.part-x` was stored as `…aabbccdd.part-x` and deleted an hour later while the row survived and the portal went on offering it. **`Pdf::PART_FOLDERS` is the ONE list of folders it may touch (the four `Pdf::create()` writes to; `documents` and `recordings` DELIBERATELY ABSENT) AND the name must END with the mark plus exactly twelve hex characters** — the owner chose both, because this method deletes and a one-condition guard rests on nobody editing that condition: removing the name test alone left all 31 checks green while a day-old statement, an expense PDF, a document and a recording all went. Symbolic links are refused TWO ways, both needed (no `FOLLOW_SYMLINKS` for a linked FOLDER, `isLink()` for a linked FILE), and `CATCH_GET_CHILD` is there because one unreadable subfolder otherwise makes the walk throw and the sweep remove nothing. **FOUR of that section's checks could not fail:** the "real PDF left alone" fixture was never aged so the age guard did the mark test's work; the symlink check planted a link to a FILE, which `isLink()` skips whatever the flags are, so `FOLLOW_SYMLINKS` passed 31/31; and nothing exercised the folder list or an unreadable folder. **Never describe the sweep as what makes an erasure complete** — a request run between the crash and the next sweep would still miss the file. ONE HOUR is the owner's choice (3 November 2026) and **the check that matters is the one that must NOT fire**: section H gives the sweep a file written THIS SECOND and requires it to survive, because a sweep that took a write in flight would be worse than the litter it removes. A symbolic link is NOT followed: the method deletes what it finds. **#720 is #711's fault in a second file:** the documents API's base64 branch tested `file_put_contents(...) !== false`, which accepts a SHORT COUNT, so a part-written document got a `tblDocuments` row and was offered for download. The multipart branch was already right (`move_uploaded_file()` answers a boolean), and **the two sharing one `$written` variable is how the wrong test looked like the right one** — they are judged separately now. It does NOT need temp-and-rename, checked rather than assumed: `$storedFilename` is `bin2hex(random_bytes(16))` plus an allowlisted extension, fresh every request, and the only other writer of that folder always has underscores in its names — **if stored filenames ever stop being freshly generated, that reasoning goes with them.** No behavioural test is possible (a short write needs a quota, the page refuses anything but an authenticated POST, and the basename is a fixed 36 characters so the long-name trick is unavailable), so the guard is static and **the guard is itself tested**: fifteen plants, each required to be caught by the expected complaint AND BY NO OTHER, with one case that must stay GREEN (a closing brace inside a string, because a brace walk that cannot step over literals cries wolf). Both are hard gates in `pr-security.yml` rather than left to the pre-push glob (#646). Open: **#725** (nothing exercises the documents API over HTTP at all) and **#726** (whether `move_uploaded_file()` can report success after a partial write — not established either way).
- **One place writes the form token, and it writes EVERY carrier (#768, D-050).** `Auth::verifyCsrf()` REPLACES the token on every acceptance and `Auth::csrfToken()` is ONE token per session, so after any accepted post every carrier on the open page is stale TOGETHER. A page carries it in **THREE** places: `<meta name="csrf-token">` (`header.php`, every page), every `input[name="csrf_token"]`, and every `[data-csrf]` element (the Translate control). `Portal.writeCsrf()` in `portal.js` is the ONE place all three are written — extend THAT, never write a carrier elsewhere. **FIVE copies existed and NO TWO AGREED how many carriers there are:** `rememberCsrf()` meta only; `event-hub-upload.js`'s and `report-builder.js`'s `syncCsrf()` meta + fields; `api-docs/index.php`'s `storeToken()` meta only; `portal-translate.js`'s `shareToken()` all three. **THE CAUSE IS READABLE IN THEIR OWN COMMENTS AND IS THE TRANSFERABLE PART:** `report-builder.js` says "(event-hub-upload.js precedent)" and `api-docs` says it follows "the event hub upload widget" — **each new page copied the NEAREST existing version rather than a shared one**, so the INCOMPLETE rule propagated and the complete one never did. The shared helper existed throughout and its own comment told callers to use it "without writing their own copy of this": **a published helper is a single source of truth only while it is the BEST copy; the moment it is the worst, its documentation is an instruction nobody can follow.** **The measured cost:** on the Host Console, publishing a chat prompt goes through `ApiAuth::requireWrite()` and rotates, so pressing "Send 'We're live'" (#612) afterwards was REFUSED and the press lost — and `go-live-button-selftest.php` never touches livechat at all, so **a test that exercises one control at a time cannot find a fault needing two controls in sequence** (established: `grep -rln prompt-publish tools/` returns NOTHING — that endpoint is exercised by no test, #769). **The return value changed on purpose:** `rememberCsrf()` used to answer false whenever the page had no meta tag EVEN WHEN it had found a token, so a page carrying only forms stored nothing and said nothing was there to store; it now answers "a token was found", which is safe for the two callers that branch on it (`admin/host-console/event.php`, `admin/live/chat.php` — each re-reads `Portal.csrf()` and keeps its old value if that is empty). **`api-docs/index.php` is a STATED exception, re-tested rather than trusted:** it is served directly by the web server, draws its own `<head>` and loads no portal.js, so it cannot reach the helper — safe ONLY because the meta tag is its single carrier, which `check_one_csrf_writer.py` re-checks every run, failing the day that page grows a form AND failing the other way when a listed exception stops writing a carrier. **IT IS GUARDED BY A REAL BROWSER TEST, and that matters beyond #768 — though it is NOT the first here: `tools/layout-audit.mjs` has driven one since `0186531` (30 October, #569), established by `grep -rln "remote-debugging-port" tools/`, a command the first version of this claim did not run. It IS the first wired into a workflow.** the fault was invisible to every HTTP self-test because the page SOURCE was correct and only what JavaScript did to it afterwards was wrong — the same gap that drove #485 to pin a whole script as TEXT after four rounds of one-edit bypasses. `tools/csrf-carriers-selftest.mjs` needs NOTHING installed (Chrome or Edge is already present; node has had a built-in `WebSocket` since v22; it serves its own fixture from node's `http` module, so no PHP either) and REFUSES rather than skipping when no browser is found. Four traps, each measured: **`fileURLToPath`, never `new URL(...).pathname`** (a URL percent-encodes, and this repo's path holds spaces and an `&`, so every copy failed blaming a missing file); **read the port the browser CHOSE** from `DevToolsActivePort`, never name one (a collision looks identical to the browser failing to start); **wait for the browser to EXIT before deleting its profile** (killing and deleting raced its cache writer and threw `ENOTEMPTY` AFTER every check passed, so a successful run reported a refusal); and **THREE of each carrier in the fixture, not one** — one of each cannot tell a loop writing every element from one writing only the first, which was planted and caught only by the three-element fixture. **And a clause that CANNOT FAIL got in anyway, in the file written to prove behaviour:** `check('E2 …', evaluate('return 1;'), 1)` asserted nothing while reading as the strongest check there; it now requires the browser's own exception and console-error stream to be empty. Guards: the hard gate above (six plants, five caught by the intended check, one must-stay-green case proved green) and the browser test (22 checks, real browser; every plant caught by the check meant to catch it). **SIX CHECK ROUNDS, 6+4+5+4+6+4 findings, NONE in the original build** — every one in a previous round's fix. The detector took SIX versions: four enumerated syntax or statement shapes, the fifth followed the VALUE but scoped it by BLANKING function bodies out of a top-level view (so a top-level lookup with a write in a `function` callback — ES5 house style, on any of the 34 pages with a non-IIFE inline script — was invisible), and the sixth uses LEXICAL CONTAINMENT: a lookup is judged inside the SMALLEST function containing it, or the whole file. **Each earlier version was "verified by planting" the shapes its author had thought of.** 208/208 field and 21/21 meta combinations, four false-positive cases clean. **And the brace walk had to step over string and template literals** — CLAUDE.md already recorded that trap for the money pages, and without it 52 files under `web/` had a function the walk could never close, `portal.js` among them; one remains, the generated noticeboard bundle's outer IIFE, failing in the SAFE direction. Three habits of mine the rounds exposed: a multi-edit script that dies on its first assert having written NOTHING, then "verified" with a line-based `grep` that cannot see a phrase spanning two lines (four occurrences); a correction applied to one docblock and not its twin 120 lines away; and five patterns left as DEAD CODE under authoritative comments, where anyone closing a hole would have edited them and changed nothing.
- **A member's chosen language is applied now, and the trap is a DEFAULT that looks like a choice (#763, migration 251; the owner, 5 November 2026 — D-060, D-061).** `I18n::reconsiderNowSessionExists()` is the ONE place the language question is asked once a session exists, called from `index.php` straight after `Auth::ensureSession()`; `I18n::switchLocale()` is the ONE writer of `tblUsers.locale`, and the `?lang=` block that calls it MOVED out of `bootstrap.php` into `index.php` for the same reason. **NOTHING WAS BROKEN AND NOTHING WAS UNREACHABLE IN THE ORDINARY SENSE — the question was asked too early.** `resolveLocale()`'s first two sources are behind `session_status() === PHP_SESSION_ACTIVE`, `init()` runs inside start-up, the session starts in `index.php` AFTER bootstrap is required, so both were skipped on every request since March 2026 and `init()` latches so nothing asked again; bootstrap's own `tblUsers.locale` block was behind the same test. `php -l` cannot see it and no audit script could. **The switcher half was worse: `switchLocale()` stores only when a session is active and its ONE caller sat in bootstrap** (`git log -S"switchLocale" -- web/` answers `fe2b3fb` and `bd1ef12`), so `?lang=cy` applied to the request that carried it and died in its own redirect, and the column was never written on any installation. **WHICH IS WHAT MADE THE FIX DANGEROUS, AND IS THE PART TO REMEMBER:** the column was `DEFAULT 'en'`, so every row held `'en'` put there by the database — obeying it would have PINNED EVERY SIGNED-IN MEMBER TO ENGLISH, with nothing on any page explaining why (a draft added "and no control anywhere to change it", which is FALSE — see below), and `isSupported()` reads a built-in list of THIRTEEN languages rather than a setting so browser detection is live on every installation (drafts said twelve; count them from the code). **A default value and a deliberate choice are indistinguishable once stored, and the moment a column starts being obeyed is the moment that stops being harmless.** Migration 251 empties the rows still holding `'en'` and the default is NULL; the measured ground for calling that safe is the `git log -S` above, NOT reasoning. **AND "NOTHING ANYBODY SEES CHANGES" WAS UNTRUE IN TWO WAYS, ONE OF WHICH COSTS MONEY — THE SECOND-WORST FINDING OF TWELVE.** The globe language menu in the top bar, printed from `nav.php` on EVERY page since March 2026, had never done anything and starts working. And `ContentTranslation::readLanguage()` reads the same column and falls back to the BROWSER's language when it is empty, so emptying it turns the Translate button from "asks for English, sees English, charges nothing" into a real, charged translation for anybody on a non-English browser — accepted deliberately (D-063). **Ask of any column you are about to empty: who else reads it, and what does their fallback do?** Three smaller lessons: the probe must BUFFER its output or `session_start()` refuses and source 1 is skipped for the WRONG reason, giving a false red on correct code; **a check that masks string literals cannot look at an array key** — masking turned `$_GET['lang']` into `$_GET['xxxx']`, so the clause requiring the `is_string()` gate (#509) could never match, a clause that cannot fail dressed as the strictest check in the file, so it reads a comments-removed/strings-kept view for that one question; and section F's "an unknown language is refused" check is only worth anything because the row ALREADY holds `cy` — from an empty column, "nothing was written" passes either way. **AND THE WORST FINDING WAS A CLAIM I PUT TO THE OWNER: I SAID THERE WAS NO CONTROL FOR CHOOSING A LANGUAGE ANYWHERE IN THE PORTAL, AND THERE IS ONE ON EVERY PAGE.** `I18n::languageSwitcher()` is printed from `nav.php` twice — signed in and signed out — and `header.php` always requires `nav.php`; one `grep -rn languageSwitcher web tools` shows it. The owner declined to build a picker on the strength of that sentence (D-061), which **D-062 supersedes**: the menu IS the control, and a second one now sits on the account page with a "Follow my browser" option so a choice can be undone. **A claim that something does not exist ANYWHERE needs the search that establishes it, exactly as a claim about history needs its `git log`** — this repository's rule was written for the past tense and broken in the present. `I18n::chooseLanguage()` is the ONE place a choice is stored OR cleared (`switchLocale()` is a thin caller) and `I18n::storedChoice()` the ONE place it is read; the account handler asks `array_key_exists('locale', $_POST)` and NEVER `?? ''`, because an empty value is a real instruction here and #677 cost a cleared portal address to that exact shape. Still open on #779: no setting for which languages an installation offers, and `?lang=` is a state-changing GET with no form token (nuisance only, and the dropdown is a GET link by design). Tests: `tools/i18n-member-language-selftest.php` (34 checks, real MySQL) and the hard gate `tools/audit-checks/check_member_language_applied.py`; thirteen plants over two rounds, each caught by the check meant to catch it — **and THREE of the first five got past the gate**: the call wrapped in `if (false)` (#709's lesson again), the call with its ARGUMENT DROPPED (legal PHP, the parameter defaults to null, and it restores half of #763 in full while reading as a tidy-up), and `&& false` appended to the `?lang=` condition. Brace depth is compared with `Auth::ensureSession()`'s and the whole condition is pinned as one string. **A THIRD ROUND FOUND SIX MORE, FIVE IN ROUND 2'S GUARD WORK, AND THE PATTERN IS NOW THE FINDING: round 1 handed me `if (false) { }` so I answered with a brace count, and `if (false)` with NO BRACES and `false &&` walked through; round 2 handed me "the argument dropped" so I answered "are the brackets empty", and the literal `(null)` walked through; round 2 pinned the gate CONDITIONS, so emptying the block BODY walked through, and so did `chooseLanguage(null)` — every profile save deleting every member's language — and `$langStored === false` in the option loop. SIX spellings, ONE fault: the statement is still there and does nothing.** The remedy was not a fifth pattern but the one #485 took four rounds to reach: **pin the whole region as TEXT.** Three regions, in `tools/audit-checks/member-language.pinned.txt`, regenerated by `--repin`; the four substring pins and the brace-depth test came OUT, because two guards over one fact drift apart. **AND THE SEVENTH VERSION STOPPED HAND-PARSING PHP AND ASKED PHP — that is the lesson of the whole package.** Six rounds of regular expressions were defeated by `$endif` (matched `\bendif\b`, cancelling a real `if (false):`), a `}` inside a heredoc body, `#[Deprecated]` read as a `#` comment, `'?>'` inside a string ending the span so `/* } */` counted as code, and `<!-- <?php if (false) { ?> -->`, which **PHP EXECUTES** and the reader blanked — plus `$exit = false;` counted as an exit, so it cried WOLF on correct code. **None of those distinctions is in the TEXT; they are in the GRAMMAR**, so `tools/audit-checks/member-language-structure.php` reads the regions with `token_get_all()` and the Python check is a wrapper that **refuses loudly with NO fallback** (six rounds proved there is no second-best reading to drop to; the precedent is `check_static_calls.py`). Fourteen wrapping spellings red, three cry-wolf cases green. **AND THEN MOST OF THE GUARD WAS DELETED (the owner, 5 November 2026 — D-065), WHICH IS THE LESSON OF THE WHOLE PACKAGE.** Eight review rounds: 12, 7, 6, 6, 3, 3, 6, 9 — **52 findings, every one after the first in the GUARD and NOT ONE in the fix.** Six rounds each found a new way to wrap a region in dead code and each was answered with a sharper pattern; the eighth found it still REFUSING correct work (a passing comment mentioning the guarded call reported the pin "worthless"; an ordinary helper above the region reported it had "moved") — and **a check that cries wolf gets switched off (#483)**, so those rounds bought nothing. **The INSTRUMENT was the mistake, not the sharpening:** "is this code reached" is BEHAVIOURAL, and the forms no source-reading check can see (`while (true) { }`, `trigger_error` at `E_USER_ERROR`, a conditional `require`) are the same question, not edge cases. The guard went from ~1,235 lines to 521 — presence and ORDER only, which produced no finding in eight rounds and cannot cry wolf — and `tools/i18n-member-language-http-selftest.php` (24 checks, real HTTP) now MEASURES it: sign in, load the real account page, choose a language, save, read the column back. **Every wrapping the pin caught also fails it, and so do the four classes it could not see.** **THE RULE: where the property is "this code RUNS", drive it; where it is "this code LOOKS like this", pin it.** (#485's pinned script STAYS — its justification is the opposite case, that nothing executes that file.) Two of the new checks are driven by nothing else, both shapes already paid for here: a post carrying NO `locale` field must leave the language alone (#677), and exactly ONE option must carry `selected`, because a `<select>` with none is a vote for option one — here "forget my choice". Section F plants the fault and proves the test goes red. **And the review rule changed (D-064):** a round whose findings are ALL in checks, tests or tooling counts towards the three-round cap; the protected arm stays open for money, personal details, authentication, erasure, backups and stored data. **Judge each ROUND by where its findings landed, never the package by where it started.** **A SEVENTH ROUND HAD FOUND SIX MORE, ALL IN THE GUARD, AND THREE WERE CRY-WOLF — which under #483 is what gets a check switched off.** The anchor was found and counted in the RAW source, the one path left that did not ask PHP: a COMMENT quoting it reported "appears 2 times, so this pin is worthless" (and `index.php` already carries two such comments, saved only by their missing semicolon), while a STRING quoting it relocated the region inside the string. `else:`/`elseif:` were counted as alternative-syntax OPENERS, so an ordinary added `else` branch eighty-five lines away reported the region had MOVED. And `die` as a plain name was counted as an exit "to be safe" — measured on PHP 8.5, `die` is `T_EXIT` wherever it exits and a plain name only as a METHOD, so the branch caught no exit and fired on `$log->die()`: **"counting both to be safe" was the cry-wolf direction.** Also: mode `endif` stopped at the FIRST alternative closer, so the account page's region ended at its inner `endforeach;` and the control's last ten lines were outside a pin whose docstring said every statement in it was covered; and TWO things were dead under authoritative comments (`HEREDOC_MARK`, named in a comment as the heredoc mechanism when the probe's token flag does that work, and a sixty-line `block_end()` nothing called) — **the second time in this package a comment named a heredoc safeguard that did not do the work, the first having never been written at all.** **AND "neither are the ones nobody has thought of" WAS UNTRUE:** `if (true) { exit; }`, a bare `{ exit; }` and `while (true) { }`, each one ordinary statement above a region, each `php -l` clean, each killing it with no change reported. The first two are caught now by a second count of exits at ANY depth (cost stated: a new guard above the region needs a `--repin`). **The third cannot be caught** — "does this loop finish" is not a question a parser answers, and neither is "can this condition be false", which is why a legitimate `if (badRequest) { exit; }` must not read as a kill. That is the honest end of a static guard here, and why **#781** exists: all three fail a test that drives the page. **When a guard has to understand a language, use that language's own parser.** And writing that file broke it in the most fitting way available: a comment explaining the HTML-comment case wrote the example in a `//` comment, and **a closing PHP tag inside a single-line comment ENDS PHP MODE** — unclosed brace, sixty lines later. The grammar holds surprises for the author too. **What it still cannot see, honestly:** it reports where a region SITS, not that it RUNS — a conditional `require`, a `goto`, a throw above it. **Proving that needs a test that drives the page, which is #781, and six rounds went into inferring statically what one HTTP request would have measured.** **(a) The earlier whole-region pin had THREE holes, not the "exactly one" I first wrote. It cannot see something glued IN FRONT of its first statement** (`if (false)` on the line above, no braces, makes the whole region the body of something that never runs, region text untouched, gate green) — so **a pinned region must BEGIN A FRESH STATEMENT**: the last thing before it, whitespace aside, `;`, `}` or an opening `<?php`. **THAT RULE CALLED ITSELF GENERAL THREE TIMES AND WAS DEFEATED THREE TIMES, AND THE FIFTH ROUND DIAGNOSED IT BETTER THAN ANY OF MY FIXES: "`;`/`}` is not a weaker allow-list than `;{}:>`, it is the same KIND of rule. Nothing about the last character can distinguish 'a fresh statement at the level the region belongs to' from 'a fresh statement inside a dead block'."** Measured, `if (false) { ;` and `switch (0) { case 1: {}` both end in an ALLOWED character and both wrap a whole region in dead code — and combining one with a differently-spelled live copy defeated the uniqueness rule too. **So the question is NESTING, and `structure_at()` pins three numbers beside each region's text:** open braces, open alternative-syntax blocks (which change no brace count), and `return`/`exit`/`die` at the region's own level before it (a bare `return;` changes neither count and hides it just as well). Nine spellings go red now, **five of which nobody handed me** — `while (false) { }`, `else if (false) {`, `foreach ([] as $x): … endforeach;`, `do { } while (false);`, the bare `return;`. **It still cannot prove reachability** (a conditional `require` of the file, a `goto`, a throw above it) and says so. **AND THE STRUCTURE CHECK NEARLY SHIPPED INERT:** it looked for the anchor in a strings-MASKED copy, the anchor contains `'locale'`, so it was never found and returned a neutral sentinel I then PINNED — a check that could not fail, dressed as the strongest guard in the file, and **the only tell was the negative numbers in the sidecar**. Two views now, offsets shared because masking preserves length. **AND `readable_code()` APPLIED PHP'S COMMENT RULES TO RAW MARKUP** (#485's U+2028 shape one level up: the reader's rule is not the rule that applies where it is applied) — one line, `# <?php $langStored = ''; ?>`, above the option loop was blanked as a comment together with the real PHP after it, so the pin matched byte for byte while the select carried NO `selected` attribute and the member's next Save deleted their language. `php_spans()` confines PHP's rules to `<?php … ?>`; outside, only `<!-- -->` is a comment — and **my first version of that rule allowed `:` and `{`, both of which reach across** (`if (false): ... endif;` and `function neverCalled() { ... }`, each measured `php -l` clean with the guard printing "nothing is glued in front of any of them"; `switch (0) { case 1:` is the same shape). **An allow-list written from the spellings you were handed is not a rule** — this file's own lesson, one level up. It also cried wolf the other way on the account page's own `<?php`, so both directions must be checked together (#483). **(b) The region is found by its FIRST match, so a dead duplicate ABOVE the live code satisfies the pin while the live code is gutted** — measured, the only surviving `chooseLanguage()` call being the dead one; the start anchor must appear exactly ONCE. **(c) A heredoc inside a region** makes two different regions compare equal, because its internal whitespace is significant and the normalisation frees whitespace; it is refused outright, and the comment claiming `readable_code()` already did that cited a `NO_HEREDOC` **that had never been written** — so the enumeration #485's rule demands was itself unsupported, the worse kind of wrong. **And `--repin` now REFUSES over any of the three**, because repinning a glued or duplicated region writes the fault into the sidecar and prints success — the one way a text pin fails silently. **Say the cost: a pure reformat fails a text pin** (whitespace runs are freed, but PHP needs a space where a rewrapped call had none; removing whitespace entirely is not available — `new Foo` becomes `newFoo`), and the justification is #485's exactly — nothing EXECUTES this code in any test. **The sixth finding was a DATA-LOSS path my own round-2 fix brought into view:** `storedChoice()` answered null for BOTH "has not chosen" and "could not be read" under a docblock asserting "nothing a caller could do differs" — the account page could, and after a failed read it drew "Follow my browser" as selected so the member's next Save wrote SQL NULL over the choice they still had, silently. **A reassuring sentence in a docblock is how that survived two rounds.** It answers THREE things now (a code, null, **false**) and the page leaves the control out and says why. And **one of the three wrong documents was the guard's OWN failure message**, still claiming the two controls "can never offer different languages" (the menu hides anything below `portal.i18n.minimum_coverage_for_switcher`, and `coverage('cy')` is 0.30, so at 0.95 the MENU VANISHES while the page still offers Welsh) and still claiming the list keeps an untranslated right-to-left language off — the behaviour round 2 deliberately removed. **That is the sentence whoever hits the failure acts on.** **A SECOND ROUND THEN FOUND SEVEN MORE, SIX IN ROUND 1'S OWN FIXES, AND THE HEADLINE IS: I CLOSED THE SPELLINGS I WAS HANDED.** Round 1's plant was "the argument dropped", so check G asked whether the brackets held ANYTHING — and `reconsiderNowSessionExists(null)` passed it, one word, the same harm, while the check printed "the reconsider call is passed a connection". The account checks were presence-only, so `false &&` on the save gate left a member picking a language, pressing Save, being told it worked and nothing stored. **#485's four-round lesson re-made inside one round; the remedy is the same — pin the WHOLE statement.** FIVE are pinned now (`PINNED_LANG_CONDITION`, `PINNED_RECONSIDER_CALL`, `PINNED_LOCALE_GATE`, `PINNED_STORED_READ`, plus the stored-language-offered condition). **AND THE WORST LIVE BUG WAS THE CONTROL I HAD JUST BUILT, WHICH DELETED THINGS:** the select was filled from `enabledLocales()` (readable `_lang/*.php` files, TWO on a stock installation) while a choice is accepted by `isSupported()` (a hard-coded THIRTEEN), so a stored `ar` matched no option AND `$langStored !== ''` denied the empty option its `selected` too — **a `<select>` with no `selected` option is not neutral, it is a vote for option one**, so every browser pre-selected `value=""` and pressing Save **to correct a telephone number** wiped the member's language. The stored language is always offered now, labelled rather than hidden. **And the "Follow my browser (X today)" label was built from `I18n::locale()`** — the language the page is DRAWN in, which after this fix IS the stored choice — so it was wrong for every single person who would read it, the option existing only to undo a choice; `I18n::browserLanguage()` exists for that. **When a label describes an alternative, build it from the alternative, never from the current state.** Two more reassuring untruths: "both offer the same languages" (the menu also hides anything below `portal.i18n.minimum_coverage_for_switcher`, and `coverage('cy')` is 0.30, so at the threshold its own comment suggests the MENU VANISHES while the account page still offers Welsh); and **D-060, written in the very commit that corrected "no control anywhere" and "twelve languages" everywhere else, contained BOTH** — it has a note now, and the lesson is that **writing a correction is not the same as applying it to the thing you are writing**. And **a THIRD reader of the column was missed after I wrote the rule for finding it**: `ReportRegistry` offers `u.locale` on the Members report and report definitions are SAVED, so a saved filter `Locale = en` went from matching everybody to matching nobody (#780) — enumerate with `grep -rn "u\.locale\|\`locale\`" web/_apps web/_core`, which answers exactly three, not from memory, and **neither can tell whether a page RENDERS in the chosen language** — that needs HTTP and a seeded installation, and both say so in their own output. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "A question asked too early (#763)".
- **A time on a clock is not a moment, and the TEST for that cannot work (#777 part one).** `I18n::formatClockTime()` is the ONE place a stored clock reading becomes words, and it builds NO moment — hours and minutes come off the digits. Four pages used `date('g:i A', strtotime($clock))`, which resolves a bare reading against TODAY in PHP's DEFAULT zone: measured in Europe/London on a stored `01:30`, **2:30 AM on 29 March 2026**, and `01:00` and `01:30` both read as the same words that day. The round trip was right on every OTHER day because both halves used the same zone. **AND THAT ZONE IS NOT THE FAULT — this sentence said "the same WRONG zone" and was corrected in round 3 of the check; the correction reached THIS file and not `.OpenAI/CONTEXT.md` or `DEV_NOTES.md`, where it sat for thirteen rounds until rounds 16 and 17 found it in each.** `web/_core/bootstrap.php` runs `date_default_timezone_set(Site::timezone())` during start-up, so on any page reached through the router PHP's default zone IS the organisation's. The fault is the DATE that `strtotime()` supplies without being asked — today's — because on one morning a year the hour the reading names does not exist. Same fix, different reason, and **a wrong reason sends the next reader to change the zone**.
