(The old claim IS true of a scheduled job using `Site::forceContext()`, which does not reset PHP's zone; none of #777's sites is such a job.) `web/_apps/rota/` already did it correctly by SLICING THE TEXT and is the model; `Venues.php:3658` compares clock readings as TEXT (#435's own fix) and `Livestream.php` gives a reading a date AND a zone together with `createFromFormat(…, $tzObj)` — both RIGHT, leave them. **THE MOST USEFUL THING: WITH THE OLD CODE PUT BACK, `tools/clock-change-selftest.php` STAYED AT 40 OF 40.** The two agree on every ordinary day, differ only inside the hour that does not exist, and PHP cannot be told that today is that morning — #549's own trap ("a test written in December passes in London whatever the code does") arriving inside the test written to close it. So the formatter's BODY is pinned by `tools/audit-checks/check_clock_time_not_a_moment.py` (a hard gate), which fails if it ever contains `strtotime`, `date(` or `DateTime` again; the column names come out of `full_schema.sql`, never a list typed in the check (#706) — every `TIME` column, **and since migration 252 every `DATETIME` whose own comment declares it a WALL-CLOCK reading**, which is how the three resource-booking reader pages are guarded at all; that SET is pinned in `clock_wall_clock_columns.pinned.txt` and regenerated only by `--repin`, because round 4 measured that rewording two comments took it from six columns to four with every check green and a real fault live. **D-065's rule in the other direction: where no test running today can tell, pin the source — and say WHY, or the pin reads as belt and braces.** What remains of #777 is the bulk: **486 time-shaped columns and only 125 with any comment** (`grep -cE '\`[A-Za-z0-9_]+\` +(DATETIME|TIMESTAMP|DATE|TIME)\b' web/_sql/full_schema.sql` answers 486, the same with `.*COMMENT` answers 125, so 361 carry none — this said 123 and 363 in six places, the FIFTH wrong count in the package and the one sitting in the paragraph whose own rule is "run the command, read its answer, paste its answer"), so "does the column say whether it holds a moment or a clock reading" is answered NO for three quarters before any reading starts. Raised rather than bundled: **#782**, eighteen hard-coded `Europe/London` literals across nine files plus two columns that DEFAULT to it (#763's shape, but those columns ARE written by their own pages, so some rows really are choices). **AND THE INDEPENDENT CHECK FOUND FOUR MORE, TWO OF THEM REAL FAULTS I HAD MISSED AND ONE OF THEM A WRITE PATH.** **I wrote "NONE anywhere" for date-plus-clock-time concatenation and `resources/book.php` does exactly that** — and it does not merely DISPLAY the wrong hour, it STORES it: measured on 2026-03-29 in London, a room asked for 01:00-01:30 was saved as **02:00-02:30**, a half-hour from 00:30 became two hours, and 01:30-02:00 was REFUSED with "End time must be after start time", which was untrue. The conflict check then used the moved values. The fix keeps everything on the WALL CLOCK (what the column holds and what the query compares): the posted reading is stored as given, start-before-end compares as TEXT, the buffer is added arithmetically in UTC *because UTC has no transitions*, and the one question needing a moment — "is the start in the past" — is the one place a zone appears, the organisation's via `Site::timezone()`. **A row reading "NONE anywhere" over a case that exists is worse than no row:** it tells the next reader to stop looking, in an append-only record. **Write what the command returned, and quote the command** — the `CONCAT()` search really did return nothing; the PHP form was never searched with a pattern that could find a differently-named variable. **Two more of the same class were where the gate's own header SAYS it is blind** ("a reading pulled into a variable several lines earlier"): `attendance/api/create.php` and `update.php` used `createFromFormat('H:i', $raw)` with no date and no zone, then compared `format('H:i')` with what was posted — so on the change morning every valid session time from 01:00 to 01:59 answered **400 "sessionTime must be a valid H:i time"**. Both validate the TEXT now. **A stated blind spot is a place to go looking, not a place to stop.** **AND THE GATE WAS WRONG BOTH WAYS.** It cried wolf because `[^;]{0,200}` blocks a semicolon but NOT a newline, so it walked across a multi-line statement and reported a date and a time side by side in one array literal — ordinary, and already the shape of two live files. And it passed on broken code because `new\s+DateTime` disallowed a LEADING BACKSLASH, and `new \DateTimeImmutable` is live in this codebase, including in a file this very package fixed (**NOT the "majority style, 87 against 79" an earlier version of this very bullet claimed while correcting that same figure forty lines below — those counts had no word boundary and counted `DateTimeZone`; for the class itself it is 51 against 55, so the backslashed form is the MINORITY. A document asserting a fix while restating the thing it fixed is worse than one that says nothing, because the next reader stops looking**). It also skipped EVERY `createFromFormat` where the header claimed only the date-and-zone form was skipped. Eight cases are measured each way now. **AND ROUND 2 FOUND EIGHT MORE, OF WHICH THE WORST WAS A REGRESSION MY OWN FIX CREATED.** Fixing the booking WRITE path made THREE READERS wrong: `tblResourceBooking.startAt/endAt` hold wall-clock values, and `my-bookings.php`, `approvals.php` and `resource.php` all read them back with `date(…, strtotime($stored))` — so with the database finally holding what was asked, a booking stored at 01:30 was SHOWN as 02:30 and the member would arrive an hour late while the approver approved a window they were reading wrongly. **Before the fix the two wrongs cancelled out and nothing looked odd.** `I18n::formatClockDateTime()` is the ONE place a wall-clock DATE AND TIME becomes words; it builds its moment in **UTC on purpose, because UTC has no transitions**, so a reading put in comes straight back out. **THE RULE: a fix on one side of a stored value makes the other side's fault VISIBLE — look at both sides before calling it done.** Second live fault: `$wellFormed` checked the SHAPE and not the RANGE (`\d{2}` accepts `25`), so a crafted `startTime=25:00` reached `shiftWallClock()` and threw `DateMalformedStringException` → HTTP 500 and a `tblErrors` row, where the code it replaced had answered politely. **A caller that promises "well formed" has to check the range too.** **And THREE WRONG NUMBERS IN THREE CONSECUTIVE COMMITS**, each cheap to check: the column breakdown dropped the 2 `TIMESTAMP` columns (402+66+16 = 484 under its own 486); "six changed PHP files" where five changed, inside the section headed FIGURES CORRECTED; and "`new \DateTimeImmutable` is the MAJORITY style, 87 against 79" — those counts had **no word boundary and counted `DateTimeZone`**; the class itself is 51 against 55, so the backslashed form is the MINORITY. Allowing it is still right (51 places is not a corner case) but the evidence was not what the command returned, in the commit whose own lesson was to quote the command. **A word boundary is not a detail when you are counting.** **AND THEN I DID IT A FOURTH TIME, IN THE COMMIT CORRECTING THE THIRD:** it said "all five changed PHP files" where `git show --name-only --pretty= 61eebfc | grep -c '\.php$'` answers **6** — the sixth being `tools/clock-change-selftest.php`, which I was counting as a test rather than as a PHP file. So each wrong figure was not a guess but a number derived from a CATEGORY IN MY HEAD instead of from the command. **THE RULE IS A SEQUENCE, NOT AN INTENTION: run the command, read its answer, paste its answer — in that order, BEFORE the sentence around it is written.** Four rounds of "be careful with numbers" produced four wrong numbers; the ordering is what fixes it, because a figure written before the command is run is a guess however carefully it is phrased. Also: `shiftWallClock()`'s docblock credited `mktime()`, which it never calls and which resolves through PHP's DEFAULT zone — a comment misdescribing its own code, in the file whose fault was a zone-dependent conversion. And the gate exempted `createFromFormat` with a DATE but no ZONE while the constant was named `CORRECT_WITH_DATE_AND_ZONE`; it now requires a third argument, counted at the call's own bracket depth. Its blind spots are both written down now: a reading converted several lines later, and **a call split over lines** (the `[^;\n]` that stopped it crying wolf). **ROUNDS 3 AND 4, AND THE SHAPE THEY SHARE.** Round 3 found that the three reader pages round 2 fixed were **guarded by NOTHING** — planted, the old `date('D j M, H:i', strtotime($r['startAt']))` back in `my-bookings.php` left all 65 audit scripts and all 51 clock checks GREEN, because `tblResourceBooking.startAt` is a `DATETIME` (which the gate rightly ignores when it says nothing) and the behavioural test drives `I18n` directly without touching a page. **The answer was not a list of two column names in the check** (#706's shape): migration 252 makes the SCHEMA say the columns hold a wall-clock reading, and the gate sweeps every `DATETIME` whose comment says so. `I18n::formatClockDateTime()` is the ONE place a wall-clock DATE AND TIME becomes words, built in **UTC on purpose because UTC has no transitions**. Reading those declarations immediately found **four columns that had said the same thing since before this work** (`tblEvents.startDateTime`/`endDateTime`, `tblEventSnapshots.snapStart`/`snapEnd`) and files still treating them as moments — listed in `tools/audit-checks/clock_wall_clock_datetime_pending.txt`, the bulk of part two. **PART TWO (a) EMPTIED THAT LIST (#777, #784, #783; review closed at round 1 under D-066).** `I18n::formatClockDateTime()` is the ONE place a wall-clock date and time becomes words — `FeedAdmin::showWallClock()` and the reminder job's `eventClockText()` call it; never write another. A page walking a reading across days uses `I18n::wallClockReading()` (built in UTC, NOT a moment: never compare it with a real now); "has it begun?" is a text comparison with `I18n::wallClockNow(Site::zoneFor($event['timezone']))`, on the EVENT's clock; a real instant is `I18n::wallClockInstant()`. A booking's "still to come" is the organisation's clock bound as a parameter, never SQL `NOW()`. **What no guard here can see is a page that keeps these methods but passes the WRONG ZONE** — three such reverts were planted with everything green (#805). **#804 FIXED NINE OF THEM (review closed at round 1, D-066): `Events::nowOnItsOwnClock()` is the ONE place a query asks whether an event is still to come, under way or today** — each zone's "now" worked out in PHP and bound into one `CAST(CASE e.timezone WHEN ? THEN ? … ELSE ? END AS DATETIME)`, exact per row inside SQL so `COUNT`, pages and `LIMIT` stay right (never `CONVERT_TZ()` with a zone name: shared hosting rarely loads the zone tables). With no events there are no zones, and a `CASE` with no `WHEN` is not SQL — the dashboard answered 500 on every empty installation until that was caught. `I18n::wallClockDaySpan()`/`wallClockDays()` are the ONE place a page asks which days an event covers (#806); an end of exactly 00:00:00 does not occupy its own day. #810 IS FIXED: the registrations clear-out (`_retention-sweep.php`) compared event readings with `NOW()` across a line break, which is why #804's search missed it — and it was events on clocks BEHIND the server that lost registrations EARLY (the issue said 'ahead'; measured otherwise). **A DELETION timed by an event asks `Events::nowForADeletionDeadline()`, never `nowOnItsOwnClock()`**: it can only be late (the later of n clock-days and n × 24 hours; the ELSE is `Etc/GMT+12`; the caller passes its LONGEST period; the per-zone rule `Events::deadlineReadingFor()` is driven at fixed moments). Zones are compared `CAST(… AS BINARY)` in both methods. A failed read throws under strict reporting and deletes nothing. Still open: #811, #814, #816 (#812 FIXED: the sweep's rule 3 now requires the event to belong to the pathway's organisation, and the job decides the switch per organisation via `AppRegistry::isEnabledForSite()`, skipping inactive organisations). **#783** was raised rather than fixed: `my-bookings.php` and `resource.php` decide which bookings are still to come by comparing a wall-clock column with `NOW()`, the DATABASE SERVER's clock in a session zone that is `SYSTEM` by default, so an organisation abroad gets its list cut hours from its own clock; `cron/event-reminders.php` does the same against `UTC_TIMESTAMP()` on `tblEvents` (harmless there, a ±3-day prefilter). **Round 4 then found EIGHT, every one in a previous round's fix and TWO of them guards passing on broken code.** (a) **The backlog exempted the FILE, not the column**, so part one's own rule was switched off in 28 of 912 files — planted, a brand-new `date('g:i A', strtotime($event['meetingTime']))` in `calendar/event.php` left the gate green, and two listed files already handle `TIME` columns (`venues/booking.php` is a booking form with four). It forgives a COLUMN now. **The pending file's own header had promised "the hole closes behind us", written in the same commit by the same hand — the #645 shape.** (b) **Nothing pinned WHICH columns say WALL-CLOCK**: rewording two comments took the set from six to four, with the reader fault live and every check green. The set is pinned in `clock_wall_clock_columns.pinned.txt`, regenerated only by `--repin` (#485/#763's settled remedy). (c) **The gate REFUSED the constructor form of the correct construction** — `new DateTimeImmutable($date . ' ' . $time, $tz)` gives a date and a zone in one call exactly as `createFromFormat` does, and only the latter was recognised, so three live files were reported as faults and two went into the backlog as work to do; **crying wolf and miscounting its own backlog in one breath** (#483). (d) **"28 files" was the count of nothing** — two pass a zone in the same call, one reads `$_POST` and takes date parts only (now `ALLOWED`), and **two more that DO do it are missing** because they pull the column into a variable first, which the gate says it cannot see: `calendar/zoom-create.php` (the value sent to Zoom as `start_time`) and `_core/Newsletter.php`. **A stated blind spot is a place to go looking, not a place to stop** — the second time that lesson was needed in this package. (e) A `<?=` block following a `<?php` block was **invisible** to the gate, because it looked for `<?php` first and `<?=` only as a fallback; no live instance, but `<?=` is ordinary PHP. (f) The gate's own header said **"two do today"** where six do. **Do not read the length of the backlog as the size of the work, and count every figure here from the command.** **ROUND 5 FOUND SIX, AND THE FIRST IS THE WORST THING IN THE PACKAGE: A FORM OPEN TO THE PUBLIC WAS STILL STORING THE MOVED HOUR.** `calendar/submit-save.php` took a `datetime-local` value, put it through `strtotime()` and `date('Y-m-d H:i:s', …)`, and bound the result into `INSERT INTO tblEvents` — so an event submitted for 01:30 on the spring-forward morning was stored as **02:30 FOR EVER** (measured), and every later reader shows 02:30 faithfully. **Nothing cancels this out, unlike the reader faults**, and the variable was called `$startUtc`, wrong twice over. **FOUR ROUNDS MISSED IT AND IT WAS FOUND BY READING THE FORM, NOT BY ANY CHECK** — because this gate matches a column being READ, and a write path builds its value from `$_POST` and names the column only in the INSERT, so the whole class of "store the moved hour" is invisible to it. Both instances of that class here (`resources/book.php`, `submit-save.php`) were found by eye; the class is **#785**, whose cheap first step is the the fingerprint that WOULD have caught both, measured at `05a8097^`: a moment assigned to a variable, then that variable reaching `date('Y-m-d H:i(:s)', …)`. **#785 IS BUILT: `tools/audit-checks/check_wall_clock_writes_not_moments.py` is the guard for the write-path class, a hard gate with its own test (`tools/wall-clock-writes-selftest.py`: a case per list entry generated from a pinned copy, and a case per construct the probe follows, each measured to go red when that construct is switched off).** It follows the VALUE with PHP's tokeniser from a moment function to the `?` it fills, inside one function (and a closure's `use` list); it does not see parameters, other files, or SQL handed in as a parameter. It went red on all five write-path faults #777 found by eye, and on its FIRST RUN found a sixth, live: the CSV import's loose-spreadsheet step stored a cell in the skipped hour moved (in Nuuk a whole day late). **It is now stored as the reading the cell WROTE -- PHP's own reading of the cell in UTC -- but only when `date_parse()` reports no time arithmetic AND that reading equals PHP's reading of a canonical cell built from what was written** (import step (d), driven by K07-K07g). Both tests are needed: `date_parse()` reports no msec or usec, and it MISREPORTS some date rules. **The remaining limit is a CLASS, on #946:** any rule it misreports, cancelled by hidden msec/usec. **Five earlier versions were wrong, each differently.** **Never store a value rebuilt from a parser's report, never trust a parser to report everything it applied, never call a branch "unreachable" because no case you wrote reaches it, and add a new branch's case in the same edit as the branch.** **NOT the same-line `date('Y-m-d H:i:s', … strtotime …)` idiom round 5 specified — that was wrong in all three of its parts (four files not seven; it would have caught NEITHER fault, the two calls being 7 and 15 lines apart; and one of the four was a real fault), re-measured on the issue**. The fix is `book.php`'s: validate the TEXT for shape AND range, store the text, build no moment — and say that it is STRICTER, since `2026-02-31` used to roll silently to 3 March. **AN EXPLICIT `null` ZONE DEFEATED ROUND 4'S NEW RULE IN ONE WORD:** `new DateTimeImmutable($clock, null)` has the comma and no zone, `null` means PHP's DEFAULT zone, and measured it really gives `02:30` — so the whole fault with the gate green, and an explicit `(null)` is one of the shapes that already walked through #763's guard. **COUNT THE ARGUMENT, NEVER THE SEPARATOR.** **AND THE PER-COLUMN EXEMPTION FORGAVE SIX COLUMNS WHERE THREE PLACES SAID FOUR:** round 4 narrowed it from a FILE to a COLUMN and then used all six declared wall-clock columns, so `tblResourceBooking.startAt`/`endAt` were forgiven in all 25 listed files — planted, a `startAt` fault in `venues/booking.php` left the gate green with byte-identical output, while the pending file said "THESE FOUR COLUMNS … AND NOTHING ELSE". **Round 4's own finding 1 one level on: the narrowing was right, the note overstated it, and the overstatement was exactly where the hole was.** `BACKLOG_COLUMNS` names the four. Three more sentences were untrue: the mirror `.OpenAI/CONTEXT.md` STILL asserted the withdrawn "87 against 79" twenty-eight lines above its own correction, surviving three rounds including the one that added 73 lines to that file to bring both into line — **the fifth repeat of "corrected in one place, not the others", inside the finding that names the pattern, so when a finding is "both files", DIFF THE TWO AGAINST EACH OTHER afterwards**; the constructor-with-zone trio named `_core/Venues.php`, which the gate NEVER SAW (it reads the column into a variable first) so it cannot have been "told to rewrite" — the real third is `events/api/create.php`; and "gains no hit" gains one, `Livestream.php:99`, verified by diffing both patterns' match sets over all 912 files. Also `--repin` wrote a pin holding ZERO columns and printed success — #697's lesson about that exact shape — so it refuses an empty set and refuses a shrinking one without `--repin-shrink`. And the variable blind spot is swept MECHANICALLY now, because it has yielded a real fault twice: four sites, two faults and two correct (each passing a real zone on purpose). **ROUND 6 FOUND NINE AND THE FIRST INVERTS THIS WHOLE RULE: the events API had the same fault WITH THE CORRECT ZONE PASSED, and MY OWN GATE DECLARED IT CORRECT.** `events/api/create.php:85` and `update.php:105` wrote the wall-clock columns as `(new \DateTimeImmutable($reading, $zone))->setTimezone($zone)`, where `$zone` is the EVENT'S OWN — and it still moves the skipped hour, measured: `2026-03-29T01:30` in Europe/London → `02:30`, `2026-03-08T02:30` in America/New_York → `03:30`. **"We passed the correct zone" is NOT a defence: building a moment AT ALL is the fault**, so #777's rule cuts the other way for a value about to be STORED as a reading. **Round 4's `CORRECT_WITH_DATE_AND_ZONE` exemption is sound for a READ and wrong for a WRITE, and nothing in the check knew the difference** — planted, removing the zone makes the gate name the line, so the zone was the only thing keeping it green: a check that passed on broken code, in the round that added it. Both endpoints are seeded live (`full_schema.sql:3750-3751`) and the documented example is the affected shape. **The fix keeps the feature that made the old code look reasonable:** an OFFSET in the value (`+02:00`, trailing `Z`) IS a zone written into the value, so such a value really is an instant and is still converted — no offset means store the text. A THIRD write path, `calendar/manage/import.php`, round-tripped a CSV row **under a comment describing that as the design**, and sat on the part-two backlog, whose prose calls its entries DISPLAY work — **so stored-data corruption was queued behind cosmetic work; a reader shows a wrong hour until somebody fixes it, a writer is wrong FOR EVER.** **THE RULE NOW LIVES IN ONE PLACE** — `I18n::wallClockFromInput()` and `I18n::inputCarriesOffset()` — because the four fixes started as four closures (#768's shape) and a test holding its own copy tests nothing (#647); each caller needs its own `use` line, which `php -l` cannot see (#536). **Round 5's fix was pinned by NOTHING** (65 scripts and 51 checks green with the round trip put back, because the only test posting there uses a fixture 24 days out at 19:00 — #549's trap), so section I of `clock-change-selftest.php` drives the REAL methods over the hour that does not exist in two zones that resolve differently, pinning the OLD behaviour beside each so no check is vacuous. **`--repin` still wrote a wrong pin on an EQUAL-SIZED SWAP** — its refusal tested `len()`, the exact shape it cited #697 for — and printed `FAIL:` then exited 0 on success. **The public form emitted EIGHT error codes and showed NONE**, so every refusal was a bounce to a blank form with the visitor's typing gone; and `submit-save.php` stored **every submitted venue as `'0'`** from a one-character `bind_param` type fault predating this work (#604, `d8177e4`), under a comment that had always stated the right string. **#785's specification was wrong in ALL THREE parts** (four files not seven; it would have caught NEITHER fault, the calls being 7 and 15 lines apart; and one of the four was a real fault) — re-measured on the issue, following the VALUE rather than the idiom. **And part two is NOT cosmetic: 24 listed files now disagree VISIBLY with correct data**, `admin/calendar/moderation.php` — the screen a submission is reviewed on — among them. **ROUND 7 FOUND FIVE, AND THE FIRST IS THE MOST USEFUL FIX IN THE PACKAGE: ALL THREE WRITE-PATH FIXES WERE PINNED BY NOTHING.** Reverting the events API's create and update AND the public submission form, all at once, left `php -l` clean, **all 65 audit scripts green, `clock-change-selftest.php` at 71 of 71, and `time-zones-selftest.php` ENTIRELY green** — the file that really does post to `/api/events/create`, `/api/events/update` and `/calendar/submit-save`. Every fixture in it is `clockIn(25..30, '19:00:00')`, an ordinary evening 25-30 days out, and **on an ordinary day the old round trip and the new code agree exactly**. #549's own trap inside the file written to close it; round 6 had recorded this hole (its section I pins `I18n`, nothing pinned the CALLERS) and left it. **The fix DRIVES the real endpoints over an hour that does not exist (D-065: where the property is "this code RUNS", drive it)** — W09d-W09h, six checks, with the premise asserted FIRST (`W09d`: is this hour really skipped?) because otherwise every check below is vacuous. **The submission form needed a DIFFERENT hour and getting that wrong would have made its check vacuous:** it stores the ORGANISATION's zone and the old code read through PHP's default, which start-up sets to the same one, so the skipped hour must be `Pacific/Auckland`'s (2028-09-24 02:30) not `America/New_York`'s (2028-03-12 02:30, an ordinary time in Auckland). W09g is the opposite direction — a value carrying its own offset must STILL convert, or the fix breaks every integration sending an instant. Measured: the three reverts go red, the two premise checks and W09g stay green, 140 of 140 restored. **AND ROUND 6's OWN FIX WAS NEW DATA LOSS:** the CSV fallback's `str_replace('T', ' ', trim($text))` replaces EVERY capital `T`, where the old code passed the cell untouched — `"Tue,"` → `" ue,"` (refused), `"UTC"` → `"U C"` read as **10:30, eight hours out, a value the old code could not produce** — so Excel's default long-date format stopped importing, under a comment promising "the old round trip, deliberately" and a commit message saying "measured unchanged". **And only the START was guarded**, so a row with an unreadable end imported with `ok => true` and "Imported as event #N" **and NO end time**, bound as SQL NULL, while the preview screen prints the cell raw so nothing warned anybody. **A refused row is visible and recoverable; a silently emptied column is neither.** **THE API's PUBLISHED CONTRACT SAID THE OPPOSITE OF THE CODE:** `api-spec.json` promised "Any date text PHP can read is accepted" while three refusals were wrong rather than merely stricter — a lower-case `z` (RFC 3339's own ABNF allows it, and it sat in the branch round 6 called identical, the pattern lacking an `i` flag), fractional seconds (`toISOString()` sends `.000` as a matter of course), and a bare date (the API has an `isAllDay` field). All three accepted now, none needing a moment built; the loosening STOPS there, because `June 1 2026 10:00` and a day-first date need `strtotime()`, which is the fault — so they stay refused and **the description states the accepted shapes exactly**. I21-I30 pin both halves. **A THIRD BLIND SPOT: a reading reaching a conversion through a FUNCTION PARAMETER.** `series-repattern.php:1140` showed a stored `01:30` as `02:30` on the one screen whose purpose is comparing a date's current time with its new one, and NOTHING could report it — the gate needs a column name within 200 characters on the same line, the one-hop sweep looks for `$x = $row['<col>']`. The backlog's header now names all three and admits its promise holds only **for the shapes the check can see**. And **two comments round 6 quoted as part of the fault were still saying it** (`import.php:213`, `submit-save.php:222`): both conclusions were right throughout, but the stated REASON had been deleted, and in this repository the reason is what the next reader checks the design against. **ROUND 8 FOUND SIX, FIVE IN ROUND 7's OWN WORK, AND ITS DIAGNOSIS IS THE ONE TO KEEP: A ROUND THAT CLOSES WHAT IT WAS HANDED IS NOT A ROUND THAT CLOSES THE CLASS.** Round 7 closed three write paths and left (a) the FOURTH — the CSV import, revertible with `$clock = null;` in one line while 65 audit scripts, 81 clock checks AND all 143 of `time-zones-selftest.php` stayed green, because W07 asserts only the ZONE and never the stored start or end — and (b) its OWN two new fixes: `series-repattern.php` was pinned by nothing (reverting it left `event-repeat-pages-selftest.php` at 251 of 251, since that test drives the page and never reads the displayed time). **AND THE EVENTS API STORED A DATE SIX MONTHS WRONG.** `create.php` asks `inputCarriesOffset()` FIRST and the offset branch checked NO SHAPE AT ALL, so anything ending in something offset-shaped went straight to the constructor: measured, `01/06/2026 10:00 +0100` stored **2026-01-06** (PHP reads a slash date month-first) for a client meaning 1 June, HTTP 200 and no warning; a bare `Z` or `-05` stored the CURRENT moment; `2026-02-31T10:00:00Z` rolled to 3 March. **Every one was named in `api-spec.json` as refused with 400, and I28-I30 read as pinning that sentence while testing only the offset-FREE half.** `I18n::instantFromInput()` is the ONE place an offset-bearing value becomes a stored reading and requires the SAME shape as the other half — which is where `checkdate()` lives, so an impossible date is refused on both sides; I31-I38 pin both directions. **THE IMPORT ALSO REFUSED A LEGITIMATE ROW WITH AN UNTRUE MESSAGE:** storage became reading-based in rounds 6-7 while the row validation still compared two MOMENTS, so an ordinary 45-minute event from 01:30 to 02:15 on the change morning was refused as "endDateTime not parseable / before start" — **the identical shape fixed in `book.php` (round 2) and `submit-save.php` (round 5), left on moments here, with round 7 writing twenty lines about end-time refusal directly beneath it.** **AND `submit-save.php`'s OWN NOTE SAID THE OPPOSITE OF THE CODE:** round 7 loosened the SHARED `wallClockFromInput()` to accept a bare date for the API's all-day case, **all four callers share it**, so the public form inherited the change while the handler's comment still promised `?err=date` and the CHANGELOG scoped the loosening to "the events API" — left accepted, with the cost of sharing now stated where the rule lives: **a change made for one caller reaches all four.** The gate still said "Two blind spots" after round 7 found a third, in the sentence every green run PRINTS, which is the line somebody acts on. **And the first version of the `series-repattern` pin CRIED WOLF IMMEDIATELY, because `formatClockDateTime` contains the word `DateTime`** — crying wolf inside the check written to stop crying wolf (#483). **Lastly, adding three checks BROKE `time-zones-selftest.php` outright** (`Duplicate entry '960470' for key 'tblEvents.PRIMARY'`): it depends on auto-increment landing on particular IDs because later blocks INSERT explicit ones, nothing said so, and round 7's six checks had used the last two spare slots. `$withoutConsumingIds()` puts the counter back. **A real fault in the test, found by ADDING to it rather than by reading it, and the error names a line 600 lines from the cause.** **ROUND 9 FOUND SEVEN, SIX IN ROUND 8's WORK, AND ROUND 8's OWN DIAGNOSIS APPLIES TO ROUND 8: its headline fix — the one that stopped a date being stored SIX MONTHS WRONG — WAS PINNED BY NOTHING AT THE HANDLER.** `instantFromInput()` is well pinned as a METHOD (I31-I38 go red when its shape check goes), but nothing proved the handler CALLS it: planting the pre-round-8 constructor back into `create.php`'s offset branch left 65 audit scripts, 89 clock checks and all 143 endpoint checks green while `01/06/2026 10:00 +0100` stored 6 January and a bare `Z` stored the current moment — **the endpoint checks send WELL-FORMED values, where old and new agree.** Round 7's finding 1 one level up, for the third time. **THE GUARD THAT CLOSES IT STOPS ASKING WHAT THE CODE DOES AND ASKS WHAT IT CONTAINS:** `WRITE_PATHS_WITHOUT_MOMENTS` — no LIVE moment construction in `events/api/create.php`, `events/api/update.php` or `calendar/submit-save.php`, which is exact rather than approximate because each now routes every reading through `I18n` (measured: all three contain zero). **It asks PHP's TOKENISER, never a text search, because every one of those files QUOTES the old code in a comment** and a text search would report the comment recording the fix. **THE IMPORT's COMPARISON WAS STILL WRONG IN THE HALF ROUND 8 DID NOT TEST:** its own comment said the moment fallback was "kept FOR THOSE ONLY", and the ternary fell back whenever EITHER cell was loose — so `2026-03-29 01:30` → `29 March 2026 2:15am` was REFUSED as "end before start" (the readings it would store are 01:30 and 02:15, a legitimate 45-minute event: **the exact harm round 8's finding 3 describes, surviving in the looser half**) while the mirror pair was ACCEPTED and stored backwards. **The fix REMOVES THE BRANCH rather than rewording the claim** — the check asks `$fmtClock()` for both values, the one thing that decides what is stored, so it cannot disagree with storage in either format; that meant lifting `$fmtClock` out of the confirm block, where it sat 180 lines AFTER the check that needed it, and `_startTs`/`_endTs` went with it, being set and `unset()` without ever being read. The loose-format fallback was itself covered by nothing (`: false;` in one word left 143 green) because **W07d's fixture is two clean ISO values and could not reach the branch it existed to guard** — W07e/W07f are the mixed cases and both go red on round 8's code. **ROUND 8's NEW PIN WAS DEFEATED BY A DEAD DUPLICATE ABOVE THE LIVE HELPER** (`body.find()` takes the FIRST match — **a lesson already written down twice here**, #485 round 2 and #763: "the start anchor must appear exactly ONCE"), and it CRIED WOLF on one extra space, the aligned-assignment style this codebase uses elsewhere, **while its failure message named a `REPATTERN_HELPER` constant that did not exist** — the one sentence somebody acts on. **AND ROUND 8 HAD NEWLY REFUSED ISO 8601 *BASIC* FORM, WHICH IS THIS PORTAL'S OWN iCALENDAR FORMAT:** `20260601T100000Z` and `20260601T100000+0200` are genuine unambiguous instants that worked until round 8, and `Ical.php`, `calendar/export.php` and `IcsReader.php` all write and read `Ymd\THis` — **so a script reading this portal's own `.ics` feed and pushing the events back through the API worked one day and got a 400 the next.** By this package's own standard that is wrong rather than stricter (`inputCarriesOffset()`'s round-7 comment says exactly that about the lower-case `z`), and round 8's claim that "every genuine instant still converts" was true only of the four forms it happened to test. Normalised rather than accepted separately, so there is still ONE shape rule; a bare eight-digit number is deliberately NOT normalised. **#786** raised: `events/api/update.php` never compares end with start — there is no comparison in the file — while `api-spec.json` documents the update body as a subset of create's and create's own description promises the rule. **ROUND 10 FOUND SEVEN, SIX IN ROUND 9's WORK, AND ONE OBSERVATION WORTH MORE THAN ANY OF THEM: every earlier round moved the code from "stores something wrong" to "REFUSES"; round 9 moved a REFUSAL to "stores something wrong".** The first widening in this package to lose data — a legal ISO 8601 decimal time (`T10.5` means HALF PAST TEN) was accepted and stored as `10:00:00`, **up to 59 minutes gone silently, HTTP 200, on a live API and a public form, where it had been an honest 400** (measured against `3d753a4`). The fractional-seconds strip matched a fraction on ANY component; it is anchored to follow `:MM:SS` now, and to the SECONDS not the minutes, because a fraction on the minute means seconds (`T10:00.5` is 10:00:30) — the same truncation one component up. **THAT IS THE DIRECTION TO CHECK FIRST ON ANY SHAPE ACCEPTED FROM HERE ON.** **AND THE WRITE-PATH GATE WAS BYPASSED BY ONE WORD:** `date_create_immutable()` is PHP's procedural alias for the constructor, and round 9's pattern listed the four spellings round 8 had used — planted, the whole pre-round-8 revert came back with the gate at exit 0, 65 audit scripts green and 95 clock checks green, `01/06/2026 10:00 +0100` storing 6 January again. **This repository's own written lesson, verbatim: "an allow-list written from the spellings you were handed is not a rule."** The list now holds every internal PHP function returning a DateTime-ish value, enumerated from `get_defined_functions()` rather than from memory, and **the real guard is a TEXT PIN on `create.php`'s routing closure** — #485's remedy after four rounds and #763's after six, and **round 10 is the THIRD ROUND RUNNING to find this same fix unguarded** (proved three ways: the alias red, a variable class name red via the pin only, a pure reformat green). The `$showReading` pin **cried wolf again one condition over** — round 9 made `static` mandatory and `= function` (used 14 times elsewhere under `web/`) turned a behaviour-neutral edit RED, **with a message saying the helper was "renamed or removed" when it was on the line just read**: round 9's own finding 5, one condition over. **The gate's own failure path BLAMED THE FILES**: with `php` off the PATH it printed three paragraphs asserting three correct files build a moment; it raises `ProbeUnavailable` now — **and writing that found a second hole, that `token_get_all()` does NOT fail on broken PHP, so a file that did not parse came back as "no moments found" and the gate PASSED** (it asks `php -l` first now). `import.php`'s own comment claimed asking `$fmtClock()` both questions "cannot disagree with storage" while the line immediately below still used `strtotime()`, so **the same text was refused in the start column and accepted in the end column of one row** — in the very shape round 9 had just legalised. **A FIFTH WRITE PATH, on no list: `calendar/manage/save.php`** derived four date parts with `date('j'|'n'|'Y-m-d', strtotime($posted))`. A date part looks safe to derive that way until it is MEASURED: **in TWO zones of 419 the clocks go forward at 23:00**, so a reading of 23:00-23:59 that night lands on the NEXT DAY — `America/Nuuk` and `America/Scoresbysund`, 64 readings each to 2041, swept across every IANA zone. **My own first two sweeps returned the wrong zone list** (zero zones, then one) because they tested the transition's own local date when the affected reading is the day BEFORE. The cost was not cosmetic: the monthly pattern was stored on the 29th, `$firstWanted` then disagreed with the typed start, and the handler UPDATED the event onto the pattern's date while telling the administrator the first date had moved — **every occurrence of that series a day late, permanently, reading as deliberate.** All four slice the text now — **AND THAT WAS NOT THE FIX: ROUND 11 MEASURED THE SERIES STILL STORED ON THE WRONG DATE, WITH MONTHLY MADE WORSE.** The other half is `EventRecurrence::datesFor()`, one call away, whose ANCHOR was `createFromFormat('Y-m-d H:i:s', $firstStart, $zone)` — so the reading was resolved after all and EVERY value taken off it was the moved one: the time of day, the weekday AND the day of the month. Measured in `America/Nuuk` from a typed `2026-03-28 23:30`, a monthly series began on **28 APRIL** (March lost outright) and a weekly one a week late, every occurrence an hour out — **worse than before round 10**, because the sliced rule asked for the 28th while the generator had resolved to the 29th and the two no longer agreed; before, both were wrong the same way, which is why it looked fixed. The anchor is built in **UTC** now, which has no transitions, and the event's real zone is used in the ONE place it should be — the deliberate `pastTheHourThatIsMissing()` step (C13, and **D-006**). **A fix on one side of a stored value is not a fix: follow the value to the thing that finally produces it** — round 2 learned that across a write and its readers, round 11 across a handler and the class it calls. Pinned by NOTHING (all 60 recurrence checks green either way, because EVERY clock-change fixture was Europe/London or America/New_York, where a 01:00 transition moves the hour but never the DATE); **ROUND 19 FOUND EIGHT, THREE IN THE PORTAL, AND THE WORST WAS DAMAGE MY OWN CORRECTION DID: a splice in `DEV_NOTES.md` replaced a verb phrase with the two words "Round 15 had", leaving a subject with no verb AND crediting round 15 with round 16's widening and with breaking Excel's long date** — so the correcting paragraph was followed by a restatement of the error it corrected. **Both mirrors read correctly, so they agreed with each other and disagreed with DEV_NOTES: diffing the mirrors is not enough.** This project's own rule — never slice between two markers, which has already deleted working code once — applies to prose: **rewrite the sentence, do not splice it.** The withdrawn "only case where the two readings genuinely differ" also **survived in DEV_NOTES twenty-five lines below that file's own correction of it**, under its own "enumerate every place a claim lives" lesson. **Section K passed on broken code a THIRD way** — `[1-9]` → `[359]`, one character, left 107/107 and 65 scripts green while six of the nine one-digit fractions stored again, worst `10.8` as 10:08 against 10:48, **forty minutes wrong**; all three bypasses were one mistake, **a list covering one value of a dimension the rule ranges over — cover the dimension, not an example of it.** The component-specific message made the HOUR sentence the catch-all, so `10:0.5` and `10:00:5.5` were explained as an hour with no decimal point in it; the colons are counted now and there is no default. **`10:00:5.5` IS NO LONGER REFUSED AT ALL — round 20 made a fraction on the SECONDS exempt** (a `DATETIME` holds no fractional part, so both readings store the identical value), and this sentence stood in both mirrors describing a refusal that had been deleted; round 21 found it, and the two files agreed with each other, so diffing them could not. **"89 zone spellings" could not be reproduced by anybody, including me** — against round 16's own closure it is 4 of 419 identifiers and 34 of 598, and a different figure again once an offset family is generated — **round 20 could not reproduce my replacement figure either, which makes the point twice: the number is a property of the list you sweep, not of the code, so NO count is given now**; I took it from a report without re-deriving it, and **a figure nobody can reproduce is worse than none, because it reads as evidence**. Two precedent labels were wrong (`20260601T100000Z` was NEVER refused by any version; the offset case was round 13, not 15), two sentences claimed "nothing POSTS a CSV" and "section K is the ONLY thing guarding the import reader" when six CSVs are posted and W07b guards the round trip, and **the section-K counts were stale in FOUR homes at once** — pinned in the test now and deliberately not written in prose anywhere.

**ROUND 20 FOUND NINE, THREE IN THE PORTAL, AND THE REFUSAL HAD SWALLOWED A CASE THAT CANNOT BE AMBIGUOUS.** The one-digit fraction rule exists because `10.5` could mean five past ten or half past ten; **a fraction on the SECONDS has no such problem, and the reason is the column** — a `DATETIME` holds no fractional part, so both readings of `10:00:05.5` store the identical value. It was refused anyway, so an ordinary database or log export was turned away with a message about an ambiguity that does not arise; the earlier correction had restored only the form with a zone AFTER the fraction. It is EXEMPT now, tested first, and the third refusal sentence — which existed only to explain the seconds case — is deleted. **And it blamed a decimal point for a cell that simply cannot be read:** `1.6. 10:00` is a dotted date PHP cannot read at all, and the fraction rule reached it first, so the administrator was told it "writes the hour with a single digit after a decimal point" about a cell with no hour in it; the readability test runs AHEAD of the fraction rule now. **Order the refusals so each is only ever reached by a cell it can describe truthfully.** **SECTION K PASSED ON BROKEN CODE A FOURTH TIME AND ALL FOUR WERE ONE MISTAKE** — each time the matrix held one representative of a dimension the rule ranges over, so narrowing along that dimension left every cell green (`\d{1,2}`→`\d{2}` on the hour, because every one-digit-fraction cell had a two-digit hour; dropping the `:SS.` exemption, because every fractional-second cell had three or six digits; `[1-9]`→`[359]`, because only three of nine digit values appeared; requiring a padded component, because every cell was zero-padded). **Cover the DIMENSION, not an example of it:** a matrix is only a matrix along the axes you varied.

**ROUND 21 FOUND ONE PORTAL FAULT AND WITHDREW MY VERDICT, AND BOTH ARE WORTH KEEPING. The guard was still a SPELLING LIST, and the value it RETURNED was never tested:** `date('Y-m-d H:i:s', $moment)` will format a moment outside the documented `DATETIME` range (1000-01-01 to 9999-12-31) — of which MySQL REFUSES only negative and five-digit years and ACCEPTS 0001-0999 silently (measured, round 22), so the year-1000 floor is the portal's own rule — and `strtotime()` reaches such moments from ordinary-looking cells — measured on the live closure, `@-62167219200` gave `-0001-12-31 23:58:45`, `9999-12-31 +1 day` gave `10000-01-01`, `2026-06-01 +1000000 years` gave `1002026-06-01`. **`-0001-…` is the SAME SHAPE round 14 recorded as the worst thing in this package**, still reachable: under the portal's strict mysqli reporting `execute()` THROWS on such a literal and the insert loop had no try/catch (it has had one per row since #790, D-099), so the import stopped part-way **with earlier rows already written**, no per-row report, and the activity record never reached — after a preview saying every row was fine. Likelihood is low (the reachable inputs are `@`-timestamps and relative expressions, and 84 plausible wrong-column cells reached none of them) and the impact was established. **The fix is round 11's own rule — follow the value to the thing that finally produces it:** ONE test on the returned string covers every spelling at once, including ones nobody has thought of. **AND PUTTING IT IN STEP 3 WAS THE SAME MISTAKE ONE LEVEL UP:** `0500-01-01 10:00` returns from step 2's strict reader, so a test at the bottom of step 3 never saw it — it is a WRAPPER (`$fmtStore` over `$fmtClockRaw`) covering every path. **Section K had to be changed with it, or the new guard was untested while every check stayed green**, because the test lifts the closure by name and would have lifted the reader. **A LEAP-YEAR TIME BOMB:** `Feb 29 10:00` is the one year-less, year-DEPENDENT cell, and sitting in the must-refuse list it would have turned the test RED for the whole of 2028, 2032, 2036 … on correct code — the cry-wolf direction (#483), in the package whose own lesson is that a test written in one month passes whatever the code does. K06 then asserted BOTH directions against the real year — right about the time bomb, and wrong about the behaviour, because it pinned #797's fault (a year-less cell stored in whichever year the import ran) as correct; since #797 it asserts that such a cell is refused in every year, which cannot go stale either. **AND "THE READER HAS CONVERGED, TEN ROUNDS OF CHURN ARE OVER" IS WITHDRAWN:** it cited an 18-versus-19 sweep — the two versions BEFORE round 20's edit — as round 20's verdict on round 20, and round 20 then newly stored a large number of cells (the figure first written here depended on an uncommitted cell list, so none is given). **A sweep comparing two OLD versions cannot be the verdict on a NEW one, and "the churn is over" is not a measurement at all.** Three more unreproducible figures (2,160, 6,480, 64,224) were written in the very commit withdrawing "91" for that reason, an independent sweep getting roughly thirty and nineteen times the first two; **no cell count is written in `import.php`, the self-test or these files any more** — the committed matrix is section K, whose two counts are pinned there as K04/K04b.

**ROUND 18 FOUND FIVE, TWO IN THE PORTAL, AND BOTH WERE ABOUT WHAT THE CODE SAYS RATHER THAN WHAT IT DOES — its behavioural verdict was that the reader has CONVERGED** (2,496 cases across all seven committed closures: all 1,440 `HH.MM` readings stored exactly, zero of 598 zone spellings leaked, and every difference from the earliest a refusal, not one changed or newly-stored value). **The justification for storing two-digit fractions was measurably FALSE and it was the load-bearing sentence:** I wrote that one digit is "the only case where the two readings genuinely differ" and that "Two digits agree", and of 100 two-digit fractions 60 are parseable and the two readings agree in just TWO (`10.30` is 10:30 to PHP and **10:18** as a decimal hour). So the stated rule was "refuse where the readings differ", and **a reader applying it literally would refuse all 58, which is round 16's fault restored.** The honest ground is that PHP's reading matches what a spreadsheet writer INTENDS — nobody typing `19.30` means 19:18 — **a product judgement, not arithmetic, and dressing it as arithmetic was the fault.** The refusal message was untrue of the minute half of its own rule (there the choice is seconds against part of a MINUTE). And **section K passed on broken code TWICE**: `\d{1,2}` → `\d{2}` stored `9.5` as 09:05 (every one-digit-fraction cell used a two-digit hour), and deleting the `:SS.` exemption re-refused the formats round 16 existed to restore (every fractional-second cell used three or six digits). Also a wrong attribution: round 12 and round 15 introduced refusals I had credited to round 16, in the field whose purpose is naming the round that broke a format.

**ROUND 17 FOUND NINE, FOUR IN THE PORTAL, AND ITS FIRST FINDING OVERTURNED THE PREMISE FOUR ROUNDS HAD RESTED ON: `strtotime()` DOES NOT READ A DECIMAL HOUR AT ALL — it reads the dot as a MINUTE SEPARATOR, proved by its refusing minutes above 59 (`10.60` and `10.75` answer FALSE).** So `10.5` → 10:05 differs from ISO's 10:30, but `19.30` → 19:30 is what PHP reads AND what the writer meant. **ROUND 15 first refused the ISO-dated `HH.MM` readings and ROUND 12 the `T`-form before it — round 18 established that by running every committed closure over the same cells, so round 16's contribution was WIDENING an existing refusal, not introducing it, and "all five earlier versions stored it" is true of the Excel form alone; section K's precedent labels said "round 16" for all of them, which is the field whose whole purpose is naming the round that broke a format giving the wrong one.** Round 16 pushed it into every date spelling, so `Tue, 3 Mar 2026 19.30`, **Excel's long date format**, stopped importing where all five earlier versions stored it; it was also internally inconsistent (`19.30` refused, `19.30:00` stored) and the administrator's message was untrue of every one. **Not one of section K's cells was a two-digit `HH.MM`, so the generalisation was tested by nothing — round 16's own finding 1 one level on.** The rule is ONE DIGIT, non-zero, on the hour or minute, which is the only genuinely ambiguous case — **and that dissolved finding 2 in the same breath:** round 16's trailing set allowed letters, `/` and `_` but not digits, `+` or `-`, so **a run of zone spellings** (`GMT+1`, `Etc/GMT+1`, `America/Port-au-Prince`) fell through and stored the wrong hour under a comment saying a zone-trailing fraction was refused — **a date-spelling list replaced by a zone-spelling list**; narrowing to one digit removes the need for any trailing set. Also: the three refusal reasons were **pinned by nothing** (reverting them left 65 scripts and 104 clock checks green — the fourth round running to find a headline fix unguarded), `$mustStore` held 40 entries but **39 distinct** (a duplicate I added — the seventh wrong count), **section K could shrink unnoticed** (deleting ten entries left 104/104, so both counts are pinned now as K04/K04b), a comment claimed "43 cases measured both ways in section K" which exists nowhere, a citation said "thirty lines above" for a record a hundred lines BELOW, the `FLASH_BRANCHES` preamble still promised "ordinary rewording stays green" which the whole-condition pins made false, and — the one to keep — **`DEV_NOTES.md`, the file this bullet names as the technical record, still carried the withdrawn "same WRONG zone" claim after fourteen rounds.** Round 16's lesson was "when a finding says 'both files', diff the two against each other"; **two files was not the set** — the claim lived in four places, and round 16 changed no DEV_NOTES line at all. **Enumerate every place a claim lives, by searching for it, before calling a correction done.** Section K's counts are PINNED IN THE TEST (K04/K04b) and deliberately not repeated here — this said "47 must-store and 48 must-refuse" while the pins had moved, in four homes at once, so read them from the file that fails on them.

**ROUND 16 FOUND FIVE, THREE IN THE PORTAL, AND FINDING 1 IS THE SAME MISTAKE A THIRD TIME: round 15's fraction rule REFUSED 189 MACHINE FORMATS that imported in every previous version including the original** — fractional SECONDS with anything after them (`2026-06-01 10:00:00.000 UTC`, a database or log export with microseconds and a zone name, an ordinary CSV cell), because step 2 strips a fraction only at the END of the string and needs a zero-padded date, so none of them reaches it. **My comment said "Legitimate fractional SECONDS are unaffected, measured rather than assumed" — and I had measured the only two shapes where step 2 applies**, which are exactly the two I then added to section K. **Measuring the set you picked and writing "measured" is the fault this package has now produced three times.** And finding 2: the rule was STILL a date-spelling list after I declared that criticism fixed — `1 June 2026 10.5`, `01/06/2026 10.5`, `2026-152 10.5` and `2026-W23-1 10.5` all stored five past the hour, **two of them forms round 15 ADDED to the supported list in the same commit**. **The rule is TIME-SHAPED now and mentions no date at all:** a fraction is legitimate exactly when it follows `HH:MM:SS`, and otherwise, if it ends the value or is followed only by a zone or an offset, it is refused — with a lookbehind so a dotted European date (`31.12.2026`, `01.06.2026 10:00`) is not mistaken for a fraction. Section K's counts are pinned in the test (K04/K04b) rather than written here — see round 19 — and round 15's rule turns BOTH red. Finding 3: **"startDateTime not parseable" was untrue of every value these rules refuse** — all are readable; the portal chose to refuse them — and the same untruth is recorded as a REPAIRED fault elsewhere in that same file; three distinct reasons are given now. **And "thirty lines above" was itself wrong** — the record sits about a hundred lines BELOW the closure's end, so the citation sent the reader the wrong way, in the comment written about a wrong claim. This project's rule is never to cite by line number, and a direction is a line number with the digits filed off. Finding 4: **my mapping pin was substring-presence, so `&& false` left it green** — the one-edit bypass this repository has recorded five times; the WHOLE condition is pinned now and three appendings go red. Finding 5: `.OpenAI/CONTEXT.md` still carried the "same WRONG zone" explanation that `.claude/CLAUDE.md` records as withdrawn, **for thirteen rounds** — the fifth repeat of "corrected in one place, not the mirror", so when a finding says "both files", DIFF THE TWO AGAINST EACH OTHER afterwards.

**ROUND 15 FOUND FOUR, THREE IN THE PORTAL, AND ITS STRUCTURAL VERDICT IS THE GOOD NEWS: over 13,431 generated cells there were ZERO cases where round 14 stores a value neither the original code nor round 13 would, zero where round 13 stored and round 14 refuses, and zero where both store differently — so the churn class really did close.** What round 14 went too far on was its own narrowing. **Its impossible-date rule let an impossible date through in ELEVEN SPELLINGS under a comment saying "IN ANY SPELLING"**: it required day 1-31, month 1-12 and a numeric year, so every cell whose broken component was a ZERO, or which had no year, fell to `strtotime()` and was rolled silently — `2026-06-00` → 31 May, `2026-00-10` → December 2025, `Feb 31 10:00` → 3 March, and **`0000-00-00`, the MySQL zero date a legacy dump exports, → `-0001-11-30`, which a DATETIME REFUSES, so the insert THROWS under the portal's strict reporting and the whole import stops part-way with earlier rows already written** (the loop had no try/catch there; since #790 each row has its own). The range test is now what it should always have been: anything a day or month COULD be, zero included, with the year `strtotime()` itself assumes when the cell gives none — and the upper bounds are what still admit an ISO ordinal date, since day 152 is a PARSER DISAGREEMENT rather than an impossible date. **And round 14's own fraction rule was spelling-dependent:** it silently tightened `\d{1,2}` to `\d{2}`, so `2026-6-1T10.5` stored 10:05 while the padded form was refused; the separator covers a space now too, which is a DELIBERATE narrowing of behaviour the original code had. **"Planted, the same one-word change goes red" was UNTRUE** — moving the arithmetic into `whyFirstDateMoved()` pins the RULE, not WHICH ANSWER the page branches on, and `=== 'gap'` → `=== 'pattern'` left 65 scripts, 75 recurrence, 104 clock AND 251 HTTP checks green; **pinning a rule is not pinning its use**, and the mapping is pinned as text now. **And `readingIsReal()` is called by NOTHING under `web/`** (`grep -rn "readingIsReal" web/ tools/`) while its docblock had claimed a caller for three rounds — #687's shape, kept for the tests with that said honestly. Section K is 31 must-store and 32 must-refuse now, and both of round 14's rules go red against it.

**ROUND 14 FOUND EIGHT, FIVE IN THE PORTAL AND ALL OF THEM INSIDE ROUND 13's OWN WORK, THREE BEING FAULTS ROUND 13 NAMED AND RE-MADE.** The import closure had broken legitimate formats on TWO consecutive rewrites and the cause is a shape: **a strict rule written for ISO values leaked into the permissive spreadsheet path.** Round 12 refused every offset-bearing ISO value (`toISOString()` output); round 13 refused RFC 2822 — an e-mail `Date:` header — plus three unpadded or basic ISO forms, and its `date_parse()` rule refused a legal ISO ordinal date, because **`date_parse()` is a DIFFERENT PARSER from `strtotime()`**: it reads `2026-152` as day 152 of month 1 and calls it invalid while `strtotime()` reads 1 June correctly. **Ask the function that does the work, not a neighbouring one.** The cure is structural — **steps 1 and 2 are ATTEMPTS, not GATES**, falling through rather than refusing, and nothing may refuse a value for the SHAPE it is written in; the only refusals are an impossible calendar day (1-31, in a month lacking it) and the one fractional shape PHP misreads. **THE REMEDY FOR THE CHURN IS THE PART TO KEEP: the matrix is COMMITTED** as section K of `clock-change-selftest.php` — 26 formats that must store, 12 that must be refused, each annotated with the round that broke it, lifting the real closure out of the page so it needs no database; both earlier regressions planted and both turn K01 red. **A list of cases you chose yourself cannot tell you about the class you forgot.** Also: **J14 held its own copy of the page's arithmetic and so tested nothing** while its comment claimed a wrong branch was caught — one word changed in `save.php` left 65 scripts, J14, clock-change AND 251 HTTP checks green, because `event-repeat-pages-selftest.php`'s M02 asserts only the DATE substring every branch produces; `EventRecurrence::whyFirstDateMoved()` is the ONE place now and J14 asks it. **And I made the docblock mistake AGAIN in the commit that wrote the lesson** — `readingPastTheGap()` went between `readingIsReal()`'s docblock and its signature, leaving it undocumented by reflection, under the new comment saying never to do that. **Writing a lesson down is not applying it to the edit you make while writing it.** The clocks-moved-it sentence also said the typed time "does not exist on that date" while naming the MOVED-TO date, where it exists perfectly well. Section J is now FIFTEEN checks (J00-J14) of which SIX go red on the old anchor — four in the two zones that go forward at 23:00 and **two in Europe/London and America/Chicago**, which go forward at 01:00 and 02:00; premise first (J00) and over-reach held down (J04). **This line said "six checks in the two zones" for a round, two clauses above its own mention of London and Chicago** — count them with `php tools/event-recurrence-selftest.php | grep -c "— J"`, never from memory (the sixth wrong count here). **And round 10's own finding 5 was reported, NOT fixed, then written up in the past tense in three documents** — `import.php` is byte-identical at `86e1ae8`; fixed in round 11. Two more of round 10's: the slug's fallback sliced RAW POSTED TEXT into a stored URL (MySQL accepts any punctuation as a DATETIME delimiter, so `2026"06"01 10:00:00` inserted cleanly and the slug kept the quote; a `/` makes the event unreachable at its own address), and `api-spec.json` still promised fractional seconds independent of `:SS`, which round 10 made untrue. Its `READING_ROUTER` text pin was defeated by a **REASSIGNMENT** — the exact class this file already records about #485 — so every use of the name must now be the pinned declaration or a call; **round 12 then found that sentence blamed the wrong cause** — it said "to match the repeat you chose" in a case where the repeat was satisfied by the typed date, so it pointed the administrator at a control that could fix nothing and never mentioned the TIME had changed too; `EventRecurrence::readingIsReal()` is the ONE test of whether the typed reading exists in the zone (it ASKS `pastTheHourThatIsMissing()` rather than repeating it) and there are two sentences now. Wording it truthfully removed the last live `strtotime` from `save.php`, so the file has **ZERO** moment constructions and has JOINED `WRITE_PATHS_WITHOUT_MOMENTS` — the strongest guard there is for it, stronger than three text pins. **The old note here cited `save.php:1210` and was stale by LINE before it was stale by FACT** (round 11 added 31 lines above it, in the very commit carrying the citation), which is this repository's own never-cite-a-line-number rule broken in both mirror files identically — so diffing them could not catch it. **Round 12 also found the CHANGELOG scoping a 130-zone fault to two:** measured across all 419 zones, the UTC-anchor fix changed generated dates in **130**, two in the DATE (both Greenland) and 130 in the TIME OF DAY, Europe/London and America/Chicago included — so every occurrence after the first carried the wrong hour for ever, and a customer reading "two zones" concluded it did not reach them. **And the reason no fixture caught it is not the zone's transition hour but that every clock-change fixture starts the series a WEEK BEFORE the transition, so the anchor was never in a gap: ask what the fixture puts in the faulty position, not whether the zone is realistic.** J10/J11 are London and Chicago for that. The CSV import's fallback silently rolled `2026-02-31 10:00` to **3 March** and imported it as a SUCCESS, under a round-11 comment of mine asserting it was refused — both halves false; it also read the legal ISO decimal `T10.5` as 10:05. Both are refused now, and nine spreadsheet formats were measured still importing. **And round 11's own new hard gate passed on the very spelling round 11 fixed** (`createFromFormat`, plus `date_create_from_format`) because I wrote a fresh five-spelling list forty lines below `MOMENT_BUILDERS`, which exists because hand-written spelling lists do not work — it is DERIVED from that one list now, and a missing word boundary before a bare `date` had made `validate(`/`candidate(` cry wolf with a message quoting a call that was not there (#483's direction). The date-only `strtotime` is retired, and its sweep is kept because it is still true: **a date with no time moves in ZERO zones.** And #786 was FIXED rather than left — **raising an issue does not make a published sentence true** — in three lines, because both sides are now readings on one clock; doing so **immediately caught two of the test's own fixtures storing events that end before they begin.** **ROUND 23 WAS #777'S LAST (D-066): every finding was in a test, a check or a note.** Round 22's assignment-count pin was switched off by five other spellings of an assignment with every check green, so the fix is to DRIVE the real import (#796); and round 22 had withdrawn the "NOTHING ELSE" range claim in one comment while it stood in four more places, these two files among them. Outside scope it found a real storage fault older than round 22 — a cell with no date, such as `2026`, stored as today at 20:26 (#797) — fixed by reading each cell against TWO reference moments and refusing it when they disagree, which also refuses a date with no year (it used to land in whichever year the import ran) and retires round 21's leap-year branch in K06, which had pinned the fault as correct. The technical record is DEV_NOTES.md, "A time on a clock is not a moment (#777, part one)".

The push goes before the e-mails, and a push claim is always followed by its call. Quiet hours hold nothing (owner, 13 October 2026). - **The off-site backup's settings are real now, and the page says what it cannot do (#671).** Until 30 October 2026 the six `backup.offsite.*` settings were read by **NOTHING in the repository** — the sync script used the variables in its own copy on the server — so saving a destination or an alert address changed nothing anywhere, on any installation, and the page said "settings saved". **A seeded setting that nothing reads is a lie with a Save button; no automatic check here looks for that shape.** `tools/offsite-backup/read-offsite-settings.php` is the ONE place a setting crosses from the database into the shell script, and it **REFUSES rather than escapes**: a value failing its pattern is not printed at all, the script keeps its own, and a `WARNING` names the setting — an escaped destination is still a destination nobody chose, and the whole database is what is being sent. The script re-checks every value in bash too (the guard that would have to hold if the bridge were edited on the server), reads the output line by line and never with `source` or `eval`. **`backup.offsite.enabled` is seeded `'0'`, so copying the new script onto an installation that was already running it on a schedule makes it start SKIPPING** — honest, recorded as a `skipped` row, and the page says so, but it is a change in behaviour. A second, separate fault: `log-offsite-result.php` required `'../../web/_core/bootstrap.php'`, right only when the portal's folder is literally named `web` — true in a checkout, where it was tested, and false on a customer's server — so **no run was ever recorded on a real installation and the page showed "No runs logged yet" however many copies had been sent**, a failure included. The setup guide had written that down as a limitation rather than treating it as a bug. Both files now find the portal instead of assuming its name. The page reads the script actually on the server to say whether the settings are in force, **stripping `#` comment lines first, because the script names the bridge in its own prose and a plain search passes on a commented-out call** (proved: disabling the stripping fails exactly four checks in section O and no others). The S3 and SFTP addresses are still not on the page (#688). **`Portal\Core\OffsiteSettings` is the ONE place that decides whether a stored off-site value can be used** (`whyRemoteUnusable()`, `whyAlertUnusable()`, `destinationIsKnown()`, `clampKeep()`, `MAX_LENGTH` 256), asked by the bridge AND the admin page so they cannot drift — the rules lived only in the bridge at first, so the page accepted an rclone remote with a space or a capital, said "saved", badged it "in force", and every run used the remote inside the script file, sending the copy somewhere nobody chose. **A refusal is only a safety feature if somebody is told:** the page now warns at Save and under the box, and shows each run's warnings. **`log-offsite-result.php` threw away every run's log** — `/^--([a-z\-]+)(=(.*))?$/` has no `s` modifier, so `.` cannot cross a newline and the WHOLE match fails rather than truncating, and a run log is never one line; `output` was NULL for every run ever recorded, which was the only record of a refused setting. Split at the first `=`, never match the whole argument. **Exit 0 stopped meaning "a copy went"** when the skip path was added, and the one reader was not changed, so "Run now" said "completed in 1s" on every fresh install (the setting is seeded `'0'`); it now reads the status of the row the run wrote. **Check every page claim against the FALLBACK, not the happy path** — three sentences added by this package were measured false, including "Switched off, so nothing is being sent", which the same package's own guide contradicts. **Never blank a value you cannot use:** an empty alert address means "do not e-mail", so blanking an over-long one would silence failure mail nobody asked to silence. A 200,000-character value also broke the pipe the script read the first line through and aborted every backup under `set -o pipefail`, blaming a closing `fi`. **Two more faults of mine, from the second round.** "Run now" read `ORDER BY logID DESC LIMIT 1` with nothing tying the row to the run just made, so "recorded no run" meant "the table has never held a row" — measured, with one older `success` row on file and a script that recorded nothing, the page said "Off-site sync completed in 0s." in GREEN. **A fix that only works on a fresh installation is a fix for the one case where the fault is invisible.** Read `MAX(logID)` BEFORE `exec()` and ask only about rows after it; the same misreading had disabled the fallback insert beside it, so a failed run that never reached its logger recorded nothing on any installation with history. **A test that starts from a clean table cannot see a bug caused by state that was already there** — O11b of `backup-page-selftest.php` is deliberately the only check in its section an empty table cannot satisfy. And **one rule ended up in four copies** (the save handler, the display clamp, the input's own `min`/`max`, and the bridge): the class was created to stop the page and the bridge drifting and two more copies were left in the same file, because they did not look like the thing being fixed. Tests: `tools/offsite-settings-selftest.php` (real MySQL, real bash, real `openssl`, real rclone to a folder on the test machine) and section O of `tools/backup-page-selftest.php` (real HTTP), with 29 planted faults each proved applied, caught and reversed byte-identically — count them from the code, not from here. **`tools/access-pages-selftest.php` must run ALONE** — it counts through `mysql.general_log`, which is server-wide, so anything else on the same MySQL server makes it fail spuriously (#689), which cost two false failures in this run.
- **The in-app help is read by something now (#744, 4 November 2026).** Until then **THIRTEEN of the twenty were loaded by nothing**, and the other seven were fetched over HTTP only for their WORDS by six other self-tests — **not one of the seven checked whether the page FINISHED**. (An earlier version of this bullet said "NOTHING loaded any help page", which was untrue and carried no command, against this file's own rule. Established by `git grep -nE "(req|pw_req|[$]get)[(].*'/help" 55b0f78^ -- tools`.) Twenty pages, read by end users, on code that can throw. Two guards, and **NEITHER judges whether the advice is TRUE — no check can, and both say so**: `tools/audit-checks/check_help_page_references.py` (a hard gate, the owner's choice) requires every runbook section number a help page SHOWS A READER to be a real heading in `docs/disaster-recovery-runbook.md`; `tools/help-pages-selftest.php` (50 checks over HTTP) requires every help address to answer a WHOLE page to an administrator and a whole page or a clean refusal to a member. **"Whole" is the point: a page that throws part-way still answers 200**, because its header has already gone, so only the closing `</html>` shows it finished (#669 and #715 are both that shape). Three traps, each measured: the reference check reads only the text OUTSIDE `<?php ... ?>`, because `help/disaster-recovery.php` still holds "section 4.4" twice **inside a comment recording the #583 fix** — a check reading the whole file would fail on the comment documenting the repair; replacing those blocks with a SPACE cried wolf three times (it pulled a distant "runbook" into the 60-character window, so "Version 4.4", "4.4 million" and the date "4.4.2026" were all reported) so each block is now a FENCE the window cannot cross; and **"a whole page OR a clean refusal" is satisfied by twenty refusals**, so B99 counts how many a member really read (20 of 20) and fails if it is none — the I7d lesson (#645) again. All seven live runbook references are correct today, so the gate is a tripwire, not a repair.

## Rules that load only with their folder

Three traps matter only in one part of the code, so they live in `.claude/rules/`, and Claude Code loads each one when it opens a matching file. Here they are in one line each, so that a plan made before any file is opened still sees them:

- **`.claude/rules/database.md`** (files under `web/_sql/`) — "Every database change goes in the install script too" and "SQL dialect trap": **every database change goes in BOTH `web/_sql/full_schema.sql` and a numbered migration, and the two must agree** (owner, 11 September 2026); migrations must be safe to run twice; the storage engine stays InnoDB; and the MySQL 8 dialect trap — no MariaDB-only `IF [NOT] EXISTS` on columns or indexes.
- **`.claude/rules/api-router.md`** (handlers under `web/_apps/**/api/`) — "ApiRouter routing trap": `api/*` addresses ignore `tblRoutes`; a handler is reachable only at `_apps/{app}/api/{action}.php`, and only once `api.{app}.{action}.enabled` is seeded.
- **`.claude/rules/app-pages.md`** (pages under `web/_apps/`) — "Two variables, and only two": a page inherits only `$mysqli` and `$SETTINGS` — nothing else, not even `$db`.

The quoted names are the old section headings, kept here because code comments and other documents still point at them by name.
Codex reads the same rules as rules 13, 14, 15 and 17 of `.OpenAI/CONTEXT.md`. Change both together.

## Report at a commit boundary — and keep going in the same turn (D-045)

The owner, 4 November 2026, having said it more than once: *"as stated
previously report on those commit boundaries, but continue to run, dont stop!"*

**A commit is a place to say what landed, not a place to stop.** A clean working
tree is not permission to end the turn — it is the cheapest moment to start the
next thing, because nothing is half-done. Commit, say in a few lines what landed
and what is next, and **in the same turn make the next tool call**.

**Why it kept happening**, named so the next reader can catch it: a commit
boundary FEELS like a finish. The tests are green, there is something
satisfying to report, and the report gets written as a closing statement instead
of an aside. The cost falls on the owner, who then has to nudge for work that
was already decided.

The only reasons to end a turn remain the ones that were always allowed: a
question only the owner can answer — ask it and carry on with everything not
blocked — or something genuinely destructive that needs approval first.

## Every decision is in `.claude/DECISIONS.md`, and it is SEARCHED before any question

Set by the owner on 4 November 2026: *"we need to be firm about recording
decisions and checking those records on this device"*. The rule is machine-wide,
in `~/.claude/CLAUDE.md`, so it binds every project here; this section records
what it means for this repository.

**`docs/decisions/` is the one register — ONE FILE PER DECISION, plus
`docs/decisions/README.md` as the index — and the decision also goes on its
GitHub issue.** It sits outside `.claude/` and `.OpenAI/` deliberately (the
owner, 4 November 2026 — D-027): a record inside one assistant's folder is
invisible to every other tool unless it is copied, and copies are the fault.
**This file and `.OpenAI/CONTEXT.md` carry POINTERS — a `D-NNN` number — never a
restatement.**

Each file: the date, the question in plain words, the answer, what it rules OUT,
where it is implemented, and which decision it supersedes. **Never edit one** —
a decision taken again is a NEW file naming the one it supersedes, so "we
decided this twice" is visible rather than hidden.

**Maintenance is part of the rule.** At every documentation sweep, check the
register against the code and against this file, and realign what has drifted.

**Search it before raising any question.** If a row covers it, follow the row and
say so. **A re-ask is legitimate only when a MEASURED fact makes the recorded
answer unworkable**, and then the question must quote the row and state the fact.

**Why, with the instance.** Whether `alpha` should refuse a change until the
tests pass was asked **three times** on 3-4 November 2026 and answered three
different ways (D-020, D-021, D-024). The second ask was defensible — a measured
fact had unseated the first answer. The third was the owner's own prompt. But
three answers now exist for one question, and the owner's words for the cost are
why this is a rule: it *"delays development and release readiness but also risks
convoluted/fragmented code"*.

**The structural cause the register fixes:** decisions WERE being recorded — ten
dated claims in this file, four memory files, the handoff's "Decisions in force",
and issue comments. Four places, none complete, nothing to search. **Scattered
records read as no record.**

**Where a decision goes, every time, in the same sitting:** the register first;
then its issue; then this file if it changes a standing rule; then memory. The
register is the one that must never be skipped, because it is the one searched.

`tools/audit-checks/check_decisions_recorded.py` is a hard gate on the register's
shape. **It cannot tell whether anybody searched it, nor whether a row is true** —
those are habits, and its header says so.

## A claim that something "never worked" must carry the command that proved it

Set after the owner asked, on 31 October 2026, why lessons already written down
were not being applied. The answer was that each lesson was stored as a story
about one incident, so a new surface matched nothing.

**Any statement of the form "this has never worked", "it was always broken",
"this has never been run", "nothing reads/prints/calls this" must be accompanied
by the command that established it** — normally `git show HEAD:<path>` or
`git log -S'<text>' -- <path>` — and the commit it names. This is the same
principle as "never cite another document by line number; cite issue numbers and
commit hashes" (#678): a SHA does not move, and a claim about history needs a
source in history.

**A backup you made yourself is not evidence about the original.** On 31 October
a `.bak` taken *after* a first edit was read as the original file; it agreed with
the hypothesis because the edit had caused it, and a working page was then
"fixed" into a broken one. Only git, or an untouched checkout, answers "what did
this do before I touched it".

**And a rule written here about pages applies to tools, tests and scripts.** The
#536 rule "never join two built addresses" was quoted about portal code and
broken inside a self-test harness in the same hour. Before deciding a rule is out
of scope, ask where else its shape exists.

## Plain English — applies to everything written
